Draft manuscript · generated from the UNIRA research

UNIRA: One Humanity, One Future

UNIRA: One Humanity, One Future — Book plan

Readers: general readers (no economics background), plus policymakers and researchers who want the evidence. Every number in the book comes from the reproducible UNIRA research (V6.2–V7.2) and is sourced in the endnotes; nothing is invented. Voice: clear, warm, honest. It shows what is possible and what could go wrong. Short chapters (5,000–7,000 words), each opening with a human scene and closing with "What the evidence says / What we still don't know". Length: about 80,000 words in 4 parts and 16 chapters.

Part I — Why change is possible

  1. Enough for everyone. Today's world already produces enough food and energy for a decent life for all 8.1 billion people. The problem is distribution, waste and organisation, not scarcity. (V6.5)
  2. Where our working hours go. 45 billion hours of French work, followed product by product: what serves needs and what serves selling. (V6.2, V6.3)
  3. The 30-hour week. Why it is for families and time together, what it costs in output (20% more per hour), and how universal work closes the gap. (V6.2–V6.3, rule 14)
  4. What we learned from the past. The 35-hour week, the Soviet shortage economy, the welfare state, the EU. What worked, what failed, and why UNIRA is different. (evidence reviews)

Part II — How UNIRA works

  1. One Earth, many regions. One world entity, regions with local autonomy, no commercial competition, allocation by needs × population. (rules 3–5, 9)
  2. The U: money that serves people. A salary scale from 5,000 to 25,000 U, fixed prices, and the money cap that keeps everything stable. (V6.4, rules 6–8, 10, 16–18)
  3. A home for every family. Prices of 100,000–500,000 U, at most two homes, zero-interest credit, and building 109 bn m² with low-carbon materials. (V6.4, V7.0)
  4. Health, education and care for all. Free care, the 59-million health-worker gap, and why disarmament and healthier lives help. (V6.6)
  5. Security, not scarcity. Why people stop hoarding when they feel safe, and why energy and travel must still be metered. (V7.1)

Part III — Within Earth's limits

  1. A comfortable life inside the climate budget. 40 GJ per person, 6–13% less energy than today, solar and wind at twice the record pace. (V6.6)
  2. Materials, land and food. A 2.7× cut in materials, halved waste, diets that fit the planet. (V6.6)
  3. No more wars. 27 million soldiers, 2.5% of world output and 5.5% of emissions freed for life. (rule 19, V6.6, V6.8)

Part IV — Getting there

  1. Better than the best market world. The matched-resources comparison: why needs-first allocation converges and markets do not. (V6.8)
  2. Guarding against power. Errors, leakage and corruption, and the safeguard articles. (V6.9, constitution Articles 33–39)
  3. The transition. Who joins first, what happens to property, savings and debts, and how a union of willing regions grows. (V7.2)
  4. One humanity, one future. What remains uncertain, how to test it, and an invitation to criticise and improve.

Appendices: the 20 design rules; the draft constitution articles; data sources; how to reproduce every number.

Chapter 1 — Enough for everyone

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.5–V6.6) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Imagine two kitchens on the same evening.

In the first, in a suburb of Lyon, a family clears the table. Half a baguette goes into the bin, and so does a bowl of salad nobody finished. The fridge holds more than the family will eat this week, and some of it will be thrown away on Sunday. Nobody in this kitchen thinks of themselves as wasteful. This is simply what normal looks like when food is never in doubt.

In the second kitchen, in a town in northern Nigeria, a mother divides a pot of millet porridge among five children. Tonight there is enough for everyone to eat something. Tomorrow depends on the price at the market, on whether her husband finds work, on whether the rains came on time.

These two kitchens are not invented to make you feel guilty. They are composites, and they stand for two facts that the numbers in this chapter will make precise. The first is that the world, taken as a whole, already produces enough food for everyone. The second is that it does not reach everyone. This book is about the distance between those two facts, and about whether a different way of organising the world could close it.

This chapter is a stocktaking. It asks a plain question: if all the food, energy, health workers, homes and working hours that exist today were counted against what every person needs, would there be enough? It does not yet ask how to share them. That is the work of the rest of the book.

How to count "enough"

Before the numbers, a word on method, because everything that follows rests on it.

The UNIRA research compares two quantities for each basic need.

The first is supply: what the world actually has today. It is measured in physical units. Food is counted in kilocalories, energy in gigajoules, health workers in people, homes in square metres. No money enters the count. A tonne of wheat is a tonne of wheat, whatever it sells for.

The second is need: what every person requires for a given standard of living, multiplied by the number of people. The research calls this needs × population. It is also the rule UNIRA proposes for sharing: each region receives according to what its people need and how many people live there.

The research uses two standards.

  • Decent is a floor. It is the minimum for a dignified life: enough food, a safe home, clean water, basic health care, schooling and a way to get around. It comes from a body of research called the Decent Living Standards.
  • Comfortable is UNIRA's aim. It sits above the floor but well below what the richest people use today. The research's own phrase for it is "comfortable, not castles".

Divide world supply by world need, and you get one number. If it is above 1, the world has enough. If it is below 1, it does not. A result of 1.16 means that for every 100 units the world needs, it has about 116.

The data cover 217 countries and more than 99% of the world's people, about 8.14 billion in 2024. They come from the World Bank, the Food and Agriculture Organization, the World Health Organization, the International Labour Organization and other official sources.

The world's pantry

Start with food, the most basic need of all.

Every year, the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) adds up how much food each country produces, imports, exports, feeds to animals and loses before it reaches a shop. What remains is called the dietary energy supply: the calories available, on average, to each person each day.

For the world as a whole, that figure is about 3,017 kilocalories per person per day.

How much does a person need? Researchers who study what a "decent life" requires put the food a person actually eats at around 2,000 to 2,150 kilocalories a day, averaged across children and adults, active and less active people. Some food is always lost between the shop and the plate, and consumers waste about 19% of what reaches them. Allowing for that, the world needs to supply about 2,590 kilocalories per person per day for everyone to eat decently.

The world supplies 3,017. That is 1.16 times what is needed.

There is enough. Not in a few rich countries, and not in theory: there is enough today, counted over all 8.1 billion people alive.

And yet about 1.6 billion people live in countries where the average supply falls below that decent level. Inside those countries, and inside rich ones too, many people get less than the average.

How much food would have to move from where there is more than enough to where there is too little, for everyone to reach the decent level? About 7% of the world's food supply. Not half, not a third: seven parts in a hundred.

A closer look: from the field to the plate

The word "supply" hides a long journey, and it matters where along that journey the counting happens.

Think of a sack of rice. Some grain is lost before it ever reaches a shop, somewhere between the field and the shelf. The FAO measures this loss between harvest and retail at 13.3% of food in 2023.

What survives reaches shops, markets, restaurants and homes. There, a second share is thrown away: food that goes off in the fridge, plates left half eaten, bread past its date. The UN Environment Programme measures this at 19% of the food available to consumers. In 2022 that was 1.05 billion tonnes, or about 132 kilograms for every person on Earth.

The FAO's dietary energy supply is counted after most of the first loss. So the research applies only the second one, the 19% wasted by shops, restaurants and households. Count both together, and the world loses about 775 kilocalories per person per day. Growing that lost food uses roughly 20% of the world's farmland.

So the arithmetic runs like this. Of every 100 kilocalories that reach a shop or a home, about 81 are eaten. To put 2,100 kilocalories on each plate, about 2,590 must be supplied. The world supplies 3,017.

The FAO also publishes a minimum dietary energy requirement for the world: 1,836 kilocalories a day in 2024. It is a cut-off set for a sedentary person, a floor rather than a target.

Enough for comfort, if we waste less

"Decent" is a minimum. UNIRA aims higher: a comfortable life, where food is varied, plentiful and enjoyed, not just sufficient. Researchers put a comfortable diet about 15% above the decent level, around 2,470 kilocalories actually eaten each day.

At today's level of waste, the world's food supply covers a comfortable diet for everyone almost exactly: 0.99 times the need. One percent short.

Now halve consumer waste, from 19% to about 9.5%. This is not a fantasy: much household waste comes from buying too much, storing badly and throwing away food that is still good. Then the world's supply covers a comfortable diet for everyone 1.11 times over.

Halving waste helps the decent standard too. The number of people living in countries below the decent level falls from about 1.57 billion to about 0.48 billion, before any food is moved at all.

Food standardToday's wasteHalf the waste
Decent (2,100 kcal eaten)1.161.30
Comfortable (2,470 kcal eaten)0.991.11
Share of food that would need to moveabout 7%about 7%

This is the first lesson of this book, and it will come back again and again. Abundance for everyone is not mainly a question of producing more. It is a question of wasting less and sharing better.

An honest complication about waste

The two kitchens at the start of this chapter suggest that waste is a habit of the rich. The evidence is more awkward than that.

When researchers compare household food waste across countries grouped by income, the difference is small: about 7 kilograms per person per year between groups. People in poorer countries throw food away too. In middle-income countries, waste can even rise as people overbuy "to avoid running out".

That matters for UNIRA. Halving waste is not simply asking rich households to be more careful. It is also about habits, storage, and the confidence that tomorrow's food will be there. Chapter 9 looks at that last point, and finds that security alone helps only a little with waste. Rules, storage and habits matter more.

What about protein, and the planet?

Calories are not the whole of a diet. A person also needs protein. The World Health Organization puts the safe level at 0.83 grams per kilogram of body weight each day, about 50 to 55 grams for an adult. The world supplies about 92 grams per person per day. Here too, there is enough on average.

But today's food is grown with today's methods, on today's land. Feeding about 9.7 billion people in 2050 comfortably is a different question. The research finds that it fits within Earth's limits only if waste is halved and diets shift towards what is called a planetary-health diet, with less ruminant meat. Chapter 11 tells that part of the story.

A decent life is more than food

Food is the easiest need to count. The same researchers have set floors for the other parts of a decent life too. They are worth seeing side by side, because they show how modest "decent" is, and how far "comfortable" sits above it.

NeedDecent (minimum)Comfortable (reference)
Food eaten2,000–2,150 kcal a dayabout 2,300–2,470 kcal
Final energy15.3 GJ a yearup to about 40 GJ
Homeabout 15 m² per person, shared space includedabout 27 m² per person
Health workers44.5 per 10,000 people (WHO)about 131 per 10,000 (EU)
Schooling9 years12 years
Domestic water50 litres a day (20 litres a basic emergency floor)about 100 litres
Travel5,000–15,000 passenger-km a year—

Two points about this table matter for everything that follows.

First, these are references, not commandments. The researchers who set the decent floors say they "should be decided at a local level". A cold country needs more heating; a hot one, more water and cooling. An ageing population needs more care. The comfortable column is a choice UNIRA must make openly, not a scientific finding.

Second, "comfortable" is not luxury. For comparison, the average German has about 49.5 m² of home. A UK study of a "good life", measured as a full consumption footprint, puts its energy at about 73 GJ a year per person. UNIRA's comfortable level sits between the floor and today's rich-country habits.

Energy: three times enough for a decent life

Food keeps us alive. Energy keeps the modern world running: heating and cooling homes, cooking, light, hot water, transport, hospitals, schools, factories.

Researchers have calculated how much energy a decent life needs if it is delivered with good, modern technology. It includes a home kept at a healthy temperature, a fridge, a washing machine, cooking, lighting, internet, public and private transport, and a share of schools, hospitals and food production. The answer is about 15.3 gigajoules of final energy per person per year.

A gigajoule is a unit of energy. To give a feel for the scale: the decent-living study found that, depending on climate and population, countries need between 13 and 18.4 gigajoules for the same decent life. Another study, using a different method, found a range from about 9 in South Asia to about 36 in North America. Climate and the make-up of the population explain much of the difference.

Today the world uses close to 80 gigajoules of primary energy per person each year. Converted to the energy people actually use at the point of use (final energy), that is about 51 (the International Energy Agency's estimate is nearer 55). The world already has 3.3 times the energy a decent life requires.

What about comfort? A comfortable life, with larger homes, more travel and more appliances, but without excess, needs up to about 40 gigajoules of final energy per person. The world has 1.27 times that.

What the world cannot do is give everyone the energy an average European uses today: about 82 gigajoules of final energy per person. That would need 1.6 times today's world supply. An American level is further out of reach still.

Energy standard (final, per person per year)World supply ÷ needPeople in countries below it today
Decent, 15.3 GJ3.332.07 billion
Comfortable, 40 GJ1.274.45 billion
Today's EU level, 82 GJ0.625.49 billion

A closer look: primary and final energy

Two words in this section need explaining, because confusing them leads to wrong answers.

Primary energy is energy as it is found: coal in the ground, oil in a well, the electricity a wind turbine produces. Final energy is what reaches the user: petrol in the tank, electricity at the plug, gas at the boiler. Between the two, much is lost, mostly as heat in power stations and engines.

All the decent-living studies count final energy. World statistics usually count primary energy. So the research had to convert one into the other. It used the European Union's ratio for 2024: final energy was about 0.66 of the energy available, or about two-thirds. Applied to the world, close to 80 gigajoules of primary energy becomes about 51 of final energy.

This is an approximation, and the research says so. The ratio differs between countries. It also changes as the world moves to solar and wind, which lose far less on the way to the user. That is one reason a clean energy system can deliver a comfortable life with less total energy than today.

What equal shares would mean for one person

So here is the second lesson, and it is not always comfortable for readers in rich countries. A comfortable life for everyone is possible. Today's rich-country consumption for everyone is not.

If the world's energy were shared equally, a French person would use about 43% less energy than today: 51 instead of 90 gigajoules, still above the comfortable level. An American would use about 72% less. A person in India would have 2.7 times more; a person in Nigeria, about 9 times more.

It helps to picture what that means. Imagine a retired teacher in Lille and a young farmer near Kano. The teacher heats a family house, drives to see grandchildren and flies on holiday once a year. The farmer cooks over wood, has no fridge and walks to the market. These two lives are imagined, but the gap between them is the gap in the numbers. An equal share would mean the teacher's home is better insulated and heated by a heat pump, and the car trip is sometimes a train. It would mean the farmer has electric light, a fridge, a phone that charges at home, and a bus to town.

Neither would be living a castle life. Both would be living comfortably.

Energy, unlike grain, cannot be put in a ship and sent across the ocean. About 40% of the world's final energy would have to "move" for everyone to reach the comfortable level. In practice, moving energy means building solar panels, wind turbines, grids and efficient homes where people live. That takes years, which is why Part III of this book looks at how fast it can be done, and within what climate limits. Chapter 10 shows that 40 gigajoules for everyone in 2050 adds up to about 6–13% less energy than the world uses today.

Health workers: the real shortage

If food and energy are a story of enough, health care is not.

The world has about 47 million doctors, nurses and midwives, about 58 for every 10,000 people. The World Health Organization considers 44.5 per 10,000 a minimum below which basic health goals cannot be met. On average the world is above that line. But health workers cannot be shipped like food. About 4.2 billion people live in countries below the minimum. In those countries alone, about 7.7 million health workers are missing.

The WHO's line is a low bar. It matches only middling progress on twelve basic health indicators that the WHO tracks. It marks a shortage, not a good service.

And the minimum is not comfort. The countries of the European Union have about 131 doctors, nurses and midwives per 10,000 people, about three times the WHO line. To give everyone on Earth that level of care, the world would need about 59 million more health workers: more than double today's number. As the population grows towards 2050, the total need at that level comes to about 107 million.

Health-worker standardWorld supply ÷ needPeople in countries below it today
WHO minimum, 44.5 per 10,0001.324.21 billion
EU level, 131 per 10,0000.457.27 billion

The first row looks reassuring: 1.32 is above 1. It is misleading. It would be true only if nurses could be moved around the world like sacks of grain. They cannot, and UNIRA rejects forced relocation. So the honest reading is the second column: billions of people live where there are too few.

This is the hardest number in the whole of the UNIRA research. Food can be redistributed this year. Energy systems can be built in a decade or two. A nurse takes three years to train, and a doctor ten. Chapter 8 shows how long it would take, and what disarmament and healthier lives can contribute.

Why rich regions must not simply give up their nurses

Strict equal shares would be a mistake here, and the research says so plainly.

If health workers were shared equally, France would fall from about 134 health workers per 10,000 people to about 59. That is still above the WHO minimum. But it would be a collapse in care for millions of French patients, and it would do little for Nigeria, because nurses cannot be moved by decision.

So for health, "equal shares" must mean something different. It means building up the regions that have too few, by training health workers where people live. It does not mean taking from the regions that have enough. This is one of the clearest places where UNIRA's rule of needs × population has to be applied with judgement, not mechanically.

Homes: room to live

The fourth basic need is a home. Here, too, the world average hides enormous differences.

Across the world, people have on average about 26 square metres of home per person. In Ethiopia the figure is about 7. In India, about 10.5. In France, about 45. In the United States, about 55.

To picture 7 square metres per person: a family of five would share about 35 square metres for sleeping, cooking, washing and living.

Researchers put a decent minimum at about 15 square metres per person, shared space included. Another study sets a floor of 30 m² for a household, plus 10 m² for each person beyond three. A comfortable level is about 27 m² per person.

Housing standardWorld supply ÷ needPeople in countries below itNew floor area to build
Decent, 15 m² per person1.703.5 billionabout 13 billion m²
Comfortable, 27 m² per person0.945.0 billionabout 65 billion m²

Homes, like nurses, cannot be moved, so they must be built where people live, most of them in Africa and South Asia. The world's population is also still growing, which adds about another 40 billion square metres of need by the end of a 30-year plan.

Building on this scale has a cost in energy. Making the materials for a home takes about 2 to 4 gigajoules per square metre. Spread over 30 years, 65 billion square metres comes to about 6.5 exajoules a year. That is affordable in energy. It is heavy in materials and in workers. Chapter 7 shows that it can be done in about a generation, and why the materials matter as much as the speed.

One caution applies to every housing number. No official world statistics on floor area exist. These figures come from a regional model, not from counting homes. They are the best available estimate, and they are uncertain.

Hands and hours

Everything above depends on one more resource: human work.

Today about 3.5 billion people have a job, and they work on average about 41 hours a week. UNIRA proposes a 30-hour week, six hours a day, five days a week, so that families have time together. Would there be enough work to go round?

If 80% of adults of working age had a job, which is UNIRA's principle that everyone able to work does work, and each worked 30 hours, the world would have 88% of the working hours it has today. The gap is 12%. That is about 4.2 billion people at work, against 3.5 billion today.

The world's gap is smaller than France's. A 30-hour week in France, with nothing else changing, would remove about 17% of its hours. The world has a larger reserve of people who could work but do not have a job today, so more of the gap can be filled by bringing them in.

How much work does a decent life really require? One study traced all the labour behind what people consume in the United Kingdom, including the supply chains abroad. It found that a decent standard of living for everyone took the equivalent of about 17.5 to 18 hours of work a week. A "good life" took about 45.5 to 46 hours. These are footprints under today's technology, measured in a different way from the rest of this chapter. But they suggest that the 30-hour week is not an extreme demand.

Chapter 3 shows how the 12% gap can be closed: by bringing into work the people who are excluded today, by stopping work that serves selling rather than needs, and by the higher productivity that comes with shorter, less exhausting days.

Three questions a reader might ask

"If there is enough food, why are people hungry?" Because food is not shared by need today. The research is clear that the binding problem is distribution and waste, not production. The numbers in this chapter do not say that anyone is cruel. They say that the problem is how food is shared, not how much exists.

"Won't rich countries simply lose?" In energy, yes, somewhat. Under strict equal shares, French final energy would fall by about 43%, but still stay above the comfortable level. For food, French supply would fall from about 3,490 to 3,017 kilocalories per person per day, still above a comfortable diet. For health care and homes, rich regions would not give anything up; the poor regions would be built up. Chapter 15 shows that, in a full UNIRA, the richest regions would give up about 6–12% of their energy use, and gain peace, time and a stable climate.

"Are these standards just Western ideas of a good life?" Partly, and that is a fair worry. The decent-living floors were built to be universal: food, shelter, health, schooling, water and mobility. But the researchers themselves say the exact values should be set locally. The comfortable column draws on European levels. UNIRA's constitution leaves each region free to follow its own culture and traditions. What it shares across the world is the floor below which no one may fall.

Back to the two kitchens

Return to the evening in Lyon and the evening in northern Nigeria.

Nothing in this chapter asks the family in Lyon to go hungry. Their supply would stay above a comfortable diet. What it asks is that less of their food ends up in the bin, and that the food the world already grows reaches the second kitchen too. Moving about seven parts in a hundred would do it.

The mother in the second kitchen would gain more than food. Her town would gain electricity, a clinic with enough nurses, and room to live. Those cannot be shipped. They have to be built and trained where she lives, over years.

That is the shape of the whole book. Some things can be shared quickly. Others take a generation. And the world, taken as one, already has more than most people think.

What the evidence says

  • The world already produces enough food for a decent diet for every person: 1.16 times the need. With half the waste, it covers a comfortable diet: 1.11 times. Moving about 7% of food supply would reach everyone.
  • About 19% of food reaching consumers is wasted, and another 13.3% is lost between harvest and shop. Together that is about 775 kilocalories per person per day.
  • The world has 3.3 times the energy a decent life needs and 1.27 times what a comfortable life needs. It does not have enough for everyone to live like today's Europeans or Americans.
  • Health workers are the real shortage: 59 million more are needed for everyone to have European-level care. For health, equal shares must mean building up, not taking away.
  • A comfortable home for everyone means building about 65 billion square metres, mostly in Africa and South Asia.
  • A 30-hour week with universal work gives the world 88% of today's working hours.

What we still don't know

  • These are national averages. Inside every country, rich and poor live very differently, and the averages hide that.
  • The food-waste figures are reported by the UN Environment Programme. They are carefully estimated but uncertain.
  • "Decent" and "comfortable" are defined by researchers. Different cultures and climates may need different amounts. A cold country needs more heating; an old population needs more care. The thresholds used here are not adjusted for climate, age or region.
  • Converting primary to final energy uses the EU's ratio for the whole world. That is an approximation.
  • There are no official statistics on floor area for most countries. The housing figures are model estimates.
  • Working hours are missing for some countries, including China.
  • The numbers show what is physically possible. They do not show that the world can organise itself to do it. That is what the rest of this book examines.

---

Chapter 2 — Where our working hours go

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.2–V6.3) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Picture a Monday morning in France. Before most people wake, bakers are already kneading dough. Nurses are halfway through the night shift. Lorry drivers are on the motorway. By nine o'clock, most of the country's thirty million working people have started their day: in shops and offices, in factories, schools and hospitals, on building sites and in kitchens.

Each of those hours is a piece of someone's life. If we want to give people more time, for their children, their friends, their rest, we first need to know where the hours go today. What do they produce? Who do they serve? And how many of them serve real needs?

This chapter follows France's working hours, product by product, from the person who works to the person who uses what is made. France is our first "region" because its statistics are among the most detailed in the world. The method works for any country.

There is a reason to start here, before any talk of a shorter week. Chapter 1 showed that the world as a whole has enough food and energy for a comfortable life. But a 30-hour week is a promise about time, and time is the one resource that cannot be stored or shipped. To know whether a shorter week is possible, we need to know what today's long weeks are for.

Forty-five billion hours

In 2023, people working in France worked about 45.6 billion hours in total. That counts everyone: employees and self-employed, full-time and part-time. Spread over about 30.4 million working people, it comes to about 1,500 hours per person per year.

That number surprises many people. A full-time employee usually works about 39 hours a week, but part-time work, holidays, public holidays, sickness and training all bring the yearly average down. To understand a shorter week, we have to start from these real hours, not from the 35 hours written in French law.

The total splits into two groups:

GroupHours a yearPeople
Employees37.8 billion27.1 million
Self-employed7.8 billion3.3 million
All45.6 billion30.4 million

Behind these averages are very different working lives. France's statistics office, INSEE, reports them separately:

WhoUsual hours a weekHours actually worked in a year
Full-time employee38.91,656
Part-time employee23.2966
Self-employed41.31,903

About 17.6% of employees work part time. The self-employed work the longest weeks of all.

Here is a worked example. A full-time employee usually works 38.9 hours a week. Over a year they actually work 1,656 hours. Divide one by the other, and the result is about 42.6 weeks of real work. The rest of the year goes to paid holidays, public holidays, sick days, training and the extra days off that French employees receive in return for working more than 35 hours.

One more complication deserves honesty. The national accounts and the household survey do not agree exactly. Using national-accounts hours, a full-time employee works about 10% fewer hours a year than INSEE's survey suggests. The two sources define hours differently, and the gap is not resolved. The research avoids the problem by working with ratios of weekly hours, which both sources agree on closely, rather than with yearly levels.

A closer look: why real hours matter

It may seem pedantic to insist on the true number of hours. It is not. An earlier version of the UNIRA research got this wrong, and the mistake is worth telling.

Version 6.1 took the yearly hours of a full-time employee, 1,656, and applied them to all 30.5 million people in work. Most part-timers and many others work far less than that. The result overstated how many extra workers a shorter week would need.

The same version also used a rule of thumb: 30 hours a week times 46 working weeks, or 1,380 hours a year. That sounds like a 30-hour week. But French employees actually work about 39 weeks a year in the national accounts, after holidays and absences. Spread over 39 weeks, 1,380 hours is about 35.7 hours a week. So the "30-hour" scenario was not a 30-hour week at all.

An independent review, run separately from the main research, caught both errors. The current research measures weekly hours directly, by group, and caps them at 30. Chapter 3 uses the corrected numbers.

There is a second lesson in that review. Averages by sector can mislead. In public administration, education and health, average yearly hours per worker are already between 1,140 and 1,297, below the 1,380 of the old rule of thumb. That is because of part-time work and school calendars. A model built on those averages would wrongly conclude that these sectors need no extra staff for a 30-hour week. In fact, the full-time nurses and teachers inside those averages work far more than 30 hours. Only by separating full-time and part-time workers does the real picture appear.

Why hours, not euros

Most books about the economy count in money. This one counts in hours. The choice is deliberate, and it is worth explaining.

UNIRA would have its own currency, the U, with fixed prices and a salary scale set by rule. Its money would not work like today's euros or dollars. So the research never converts U into euros, and it never treats today's prices as a guide to what things will cost in UNIRA. Today's economy is used for one purpose only: to measure the physical starting point that any future must begin from. What is made, what it is made from, and how many hours of human work it takes.

Money can also hide what matters. Take one example from the research. A 30-hour week in France, with nothing else changing, would put about €448 billion of today's output "at risk", valued at market prices. That sounds alarming. But it is not a measure of how much worse off people would be. It counts advertising and nursing at whatever price they fetch today. An hour of work, by contrast, is the same size wherever it is spent. It is the unit in which a shorter week is actually paid for.

Following the hours to the people who use them

Every product carries hours inside it. A loaf of bread contains the baker's hours, but also some of the farmer's, the miller's, the truck driver's, and the hours of the people who made the oven and the electricity. Economists can trace these chains using input-output tables, which show what each industry buys from every other.

We used France's official table for 2022, which counted about 45 billion hours. We asked a simple question: when all the chains are followed to the end, who uses the hours?

What the hours end up servingBillion hoursShare of all hoursShare in 2019
What households buy (food, clothes, housing services, leisure…)14.432.0%33.9%
Public services (health, education, administration)11.625.6%25.0%
Investment (new buildings, machines, roads)7.717.2%17.8%
Exports (goods and services sent to other countries)9.721.5%19.5%
Non-profit organisations1.53.4%3.4%

Only about one hour in three ends up in what households buy for themselves. A quarter goes to public services such as health care and schools. A sixth builds the future: homes, machines, infrastructure. And more than a fifth is work for people in other countries.

The 2019 column is a check. The 2022 table came just after the pandemic, which might have distorted it. The shares in 2019 are very close. The picture is not an accident of one odd year.

That last point matters for a world federation. France also receives work from other countries: if it made everything it imports itself, it would need about 10.9 billion more hours. France sends about 9.7 billion hours of work abroad in its exports. It receives more work from the world than it sends. In UNIRA, these flows stop being "trade" and become what they really are: people in different regions working for each other.

A closer look: how an hour is followed

How can anyone know that a farmer's hour ends up in a restaurant meal, and not in an export or a school?

Here is a short illustration. It is imagined, but it follows the method exactly.

Imagine a plate of roast chicken served in a café in Toulouse. The cook and the waiter work directly for the customer. That is the first round of hours. But the café also bought the chicken from a food manufacturer, the vegetables from a wholesaler, and electricity from a power company. Each of those had workers. That is the second round. The food manufacturer, in turn, bought chickens from a farm, packaging from a factory and transport from a lorry firm. That is the third round. And so on, through dozens of rounds, each one smaller than the last.

An input-output table records, for every one of 65 product groups, what share of its inputs comes from each of the others. With a standard piece of mathematics, the research adds up all the rounds at once. For every product bought by a final user, it finds the total hours behind it, across the whole chain.

Three cautions apply, and the research states them openly.

  • The table is in euros. It is used only to measure how much of each input goes into each product, never to price anything. But euros distort the picture a little. Services with high margins look as if they use more inputs than they really do. Later versions will use physical units such as tonnes and kilowatt-hours.
  • Some hours had to be estimated. France does not publish hours for every small industry. For about 51% of the hours, the research split a published total between related industries in proportion to the number of people working in each. That assumes equal hours per person inside a group. Retail, for example, has more part-time work than wholesale, so this method slightly overstates retail hours.
  • Imports are counted at French technology. The "10.9 billion hours" in imports is how long it would take France to make those goods. It is not a count of hours actually worked in China or Germany.

The longest chains

Which things that households buy carry the most hours, counting the whole chain behind them?

  • Shopping itself. Retail trade carries about 3.0 billion hours: the shops, the shelves, the tills, the stock rooms.
  • Restaurants and hotels: about 2.4 billion hours.
  • Food manufacturing: about 1.3 billion hours.
  • Insurance: about 0.66 billion hours.
  • Residential care for elderly and disabled people: about 0.60 billion hours.
  • Health care paid by households: about 0.57 billion hours.

Some of these hours are clearly essential. Others exist partly because of how today's economy is organised, which is the question of the next section.

Notice what tops the list. More of the hours in what French households buy go into selling things in shops than into making the food they eat. That is not a sign that shop workers are idle. Shops do real work: they store, display and hand over goods. But it shows how much of a modern economy is about getting things to people, rather than making them.

Hours that serve selling

Some work exists mainly because businesses compete to sell. Advertising and market research tell us to buy one brand instead of another. Part of finance, and much real-estate dealing, moves money and property from one owner to the next without producing anything new. Part of legal, accounting and consulting work deals with competition, tax optimisation and disputes over property.

In UNIRA there is no commercial competition for profit, no speculation, and simpler rules. Some of this work would not disappear. People still need information, insurance against risk, legal advice and payments. But a share of it would.

The research flagged these activities as candidates for reduction:

  • advertising and market research;
  • real-estate services, not counting the rent that home-owners are deemed to pay themselves;
  • financial services;
  • brokerage and fund management;
  • insurance;
  • legal, accounting, head-office and consulting work.

How much? We cannot know exactly, and deciding which activities to reduce is a democratic choice, not a calculation. What we can measure is how many hours each level of reduction would free, including all the hours in the supply chains behind those activities:

Reduction of market-specific activityHours freedShare of all hours (2022)Share in 2019
Modest (5–25%)about 0.8 billion1.7%1.6%
Moderate (15–50%)about 1.75 billion3.9%3.7%
Deep (30–75%)about 3.0 billion6.6%6.3%

These shares are not huge, but they matter, as Chapter 3 will show: they cover a real part of the hours a 30-hour week needs.

A closer look: what "hours freed" means

The research uses a method called hypothetical extraction. It asks: if a product were reduced by a given share, as something people buy and as an input to other products, how many hours would disappear from the whole economy?

This gives an upper bound. It assumes that nobody who used the product needs a substitute. In reality, a firm that stops paying for advertising might spend some of that effort elsewhere. And UNIRA itself needs work that does not exist today: a payment and invoice network, allocation bodies, audits, democratic assemblies. Those hours have not yet been counted. So the true saving will be somewhat smaller than the table shows.

One trap is worth naming. Temporary-work agencies appear in the statistics as a large activity, but their workers actually work in factories, warehouses and offices. Removing the agencies would not remove that work, so we did not count it as saved.

What these hours do for a shorter week

How much of the 30-hour gap could this work cover? Chapter 3 tells the full story, but the short answer is: a real part, not all.

Under central assumptions, the research finds that a 30-hour week in France leaves about 10.1% of all hours uncovered, after the other offsets. A moderate reduction of market-specific activity brings that down to 6.2%. A deep reduction brings it to 3.5%. So a moderate reduction covers about 40% of what is left.

This is the first part of UNIRA's advantage that the research could actually measure. It comes from one of UNIRA's institutions, the end of commercial competition for profit. It is not an assumed bonus. But a reformed market economy could also shrink some of these activities. Chapter 13 tests that fairly.

Two objections

"These are real jobs held by real people. What happens to them?" Nobody in UNIRA loses their income or their place in society. Everyone able to work does work, and everyone keeps the right to choose their occupation. What changes is the work itself. The hours freed from selling are exactly what makes a shorter week possible for everyone. In the research, they are not lost. They are counted as hours available to cover the 30-hour week, in care, building and everything else that is needed.

It helps to picture one such change. Imagine Camille, who spends her days today testing which shade of blue on a cereal box sells best. She is imagined, not interviewed. In UNIRA, the cereal box no longer competes with another brand on the shelf, so most of that work is not needed. Camille keeps her income while she trains. She might choose the care-assistant course, which in France takes about 11 months. Or she might use the same skills in surveys that measure what people in her region actually need, which UNIRA's planning depends on. Either way, her hours move from selling to serving.

"Isn't insurance, or legal advice, useful?" Often, yes. That is why no activity is cut to zero in any scenario. Even the deep level keeps between a quarter and seven-tenths of each activity. People still need protection against risk, help with the law and ways to pay. The question is only how much of today's volume exists because firms compete to sell, and that is for people to decide together.

Where a shorter week bites

Knowing where the hours go also shows where a shorter week would be felt most. If every employee's usual week were capped at 30 hours, and nothing else changed, each sector would need to produce more per hour to keep its output. The research measured how much, sector by sector.

The answer differs widely:

  • Agriculture would need only about 7.7% more output per hour. Most of its hours are worked by the self-employed, who are not capped in this scenario.
  • Electricity and gas would need about 30.4%, the most of any sector, because almost everyone there works full time.
  • Health and social work would need about 16.9%, and other services about 14.3%. These sectors already have many part-timers, whose hours a 30-hour cap barely touches.

Another way to see the same thing is in people. If the lost hours were filled by new workers with the same working patterns as today's, the extra people would be needed mostly in:

SectorExtra people needed
Trade (shops and wholesale)0.81 million
Health and social work0.78 million
Manufacturing0.73 million
Public administration0.66 million
Professional services0.56 million
Administrative support0.53 million
Education0.43 million

Public administration, education and health together would need about 1.87 million more people. These are the services UNIRA guarantees. They cannot be cut, and Chapter 3 shows that in care, working faster is not an option.

A closer look: why a school has no productivity figure

It would be natural to ask how fast each sector becomes more productive on its own, and so how long each would take to absorb a shorter week. The research did measure this, and found two surprises.

First, in eight sectors, measured output per hour did not rise at all between 2015 and 2019. They include agriculture, energy, transport, hotels and restaurants, real estate, administrative support and other services. In those sectors, waiting for productivity to grow would never close the gap.

Second, in public administration, education and health, the figure means very little. Statisticians measure the "output" of a school or a hospital largely by what goes into it: the staff, the buildings, the supplies. Roughly speaking, more teachers counts as more teaching. So the official productivity trend in these sectors says almost nothing about how much more efficient they could become.

That is one more reason the research treats care separately. A nurse's hour at a bedside is not something that can be squeezed.

Hours the statistics do not see

Everything in this chapter is paid work. It is what the national accounts count.

A great deal of work is not counted at all: cooking at home, cleaning, caring for children, looking after an elderly parent. This unpaid work is essential, yet the national accounts leave it out entirely.

A shorter paid week could change that hidden work in two ways. People would have more time for their families, which is the whole purpose of UNIRA's 30-hour week. But some of the time freed may also shift into unpaid work of this kind. The research has not measured how much. It is a real gap, and it is listed at the end of the chapter.

Public services and investment are not on the list

It is worth saying clearly what the research does not propose to cut.

Public services take about 25.6% of France's hours. They deliver health care, schooling and the administration that keeps a society running. UNIRA guarantees free health care and education for life. These hours are not a candidate for reduction. If anything, Chapter 3 shows that health and care will need more hours, not fewer.

Investment takes about 17.2% of hours. It builds homes, hospitals, railways and power lines. In UNIRA, this is how the energy transition and the world's housing programme are made. These hours are not avoidable either.

So when people ask whether a shorter week means less of the things that matter, the answer from the data is: it should not. The hours that can be saved come from elsewhere.

What stock-first saves, and where

UNIRA's Article 11 says: maintain, repair and reuse before building new. Things that last longer need replacing less often. How many French hours would that save?

Surprisingly few. The domestic hours that go into the durable goods households and businesses buy (electronics, machines, vehicles, furniture, textiles) are only about 0.9% of France's hours, because most of these goods are made abroad. Even if they lasted twice as long, after allowing for the extra repair work, France would save only about 0.35% of its hours. Making France's imported durables would take about 1.27 billion hours, three times more than the domestic figure.

So stock-first mainly saves work, materials and emissions in other regions: in the factories of Asia and the mines of Africa and South America. That is exactly why UNIRA thinks of the world as one entity. A rule that looks small in one region can be large for humanity. Chapter 11 shows how much it matters for the planet's materials.

Food

Producing the food French households buy, through agriculture, fishing and food manufacturing, takes about 3.6% of all hours. Less food waste would save part of that, but the research has not yet measured how much in France. Chapter 1 showed that halving waste is also what makes a comfortable diet possible for everyone on Earth.

Beyond France

France is a pilot region, not the whole story. When the research treats the European Union as one pooled region, the picture is similar. Every European region faces the challenge of a shorter week at the same time.

For the world, the research has not yet followed hours product by product. That needs a world input-output model, which is one of the next steps. Chapter 1 gave the world's working hours in total. This chapter shows, for one region, what those hours are for.

Monday morning, seen again

Go back to that Monday in France.

The baker, the nurse and the lorry driver are still at work. So are the shop assistant, the teacher and the builder. Nothing in this chapter says their work is useless. Most of it is not.

What the chapter shows is the shape of the whole. About one hour in three ends up in what households buy, and of that, more goes into selling than into making the food. A quarter keeps public services running. A sixth builds the future. A fifth works for people in other regions, and slightly more comes back the other way. A few hours in every hundred exist mainly because firms compete to sell.

Those few hours are where UNIRA starts. They are not enough on their own to pay for a shorter week. But they are real, they are measurable, and they come from changing how the economy is organised, not from asking anyone to work harder. The next chapter puts them together with everything else.

What the evidence says

  • France worked about 45.6 billion hours in 2023, about 1,500 per working person. A full-time employee works about 1,656 hours a year; a part-timer, 966.
  • Only about 32% of hours end up in what households buy. 25.6% serve public services, 17.2% build the future, and 21.5% serve other countries. France receives about 10.9 billion hours of work in imports and sends about 9.7 billion in exports.
  • Moderate reductions in advertising, speculative finance, real-estate dealing and part of legal and consulting work would free about 4% of all hours. Deep reductions would free about 6.6%. A moderate reduction covers about 40% of the hours a 30-hour week leaves uncovered in the central case.
  • Public services and investment are not candidates for cuts.
  • Longer-lasting goods save few French hours but many hours abroad: imported durables carry about 1.27 billion hours of work.

What we still don't know

  • The input-output tables are in euros. We use them only to trace how products depend on each other, but prices distort the picture slightly. Future versions will use physical units.
  • About half of the hours had to be shared between related industries in proportion to the number of people working in them.
  • The hours freed are upper bounds. UNIRA's own institutions will need hours that are not yet counted.
  • The table is for 2022; the hours gap for a shorter week uses 2023. The two years differ slightly.
  • Unpaid work at home is not in the national accounts. A shorter paid week may shift time into it, and that has not been measured.
  • In public services, official productivity figures are measured mostly from inputs, so they say little about possible efficiency gains.
  • Which activities to reduce, and by how much, is a choice for people to make together, not for a model.

---

Chapter 3 — The 30-hour week

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.2–V6.4) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

It is half past three on a Tuesday afternoon. In a primary school in Lille, children pour out of the gate. Waiting for them, among the grandparents and childminders, is a father who has just finished his six-hour day. He takes his daughter's hand. They walk to the park, then home, where he cooks while she does her homework at the kitchen table. Her mother gets home at four.

This is an imagined scene, not a report. But it is the reason for this chapter. UNIRA's 30-hour week, six hours a day for five days, is not mainly an economic idea. It is a family idea: time for children, partners, parents and friends. The question this chapter asks is whether the world can afford it.

Why 30 hours, and why from the start

UNIRA's draft constitution lists time among the basic rights, next to food, a home and health care. It promises "a standard working week of 30 hours (6 hours × 5 days), so that families can live together".

The author of UNIRA made two choices that shape this whole chapter.

The first is that the 30-hour week starts from day one. The research also tested a gentler path: an enforced 35-hour week first, then 30 later. The author rejected it. The 35-hour results are kept in this chapter only as a comparison, to show how much harder 30 hours is.

The second choice is that everyone able to work does work. The author called this the most important rule of all. The shorter week is for families. Universal work is what pays for it.

The rest of this chapter tests whether those two choices fit together. It starts with France, where the data are richest, and ends with the world.

What a shorter week costs

Today a full-time employee in France usually works about 38.9 hours a week. If everyone's working week were capped at 30 hours and nothing else changed, France would work about 16.7% fewer hours.

To produce the same amount with fewer hours, each hour would have to produce more. How much more? About 20% more output per hour. If the self-employed, who often work long weeks, were capped too, the figure would be about 29%.

Is that a lot? Between 2015 and 2019, output per hour in France grew by only about 0.6% a year. At that pace, a 20% gain would take about 32 years. Simply waiting for productivity to rise will not deliver a 30-hour week soon.

So something else is needed. The rest of this chapter looks at four sources, and finds that together they can be enough, if one condition is met.

A closer look: from 16.7% to 20%

Why does losing 16.7% of hours need a 20% gain, not a 16.7% one? It is a quirk of percentages, and it trips up many people.

Start with France's 45.6 billion hours in 2023. Capping every employee's usual week at 30 hours removes about 7.6 billion of them. About 38.0 billion remain. To make the same output with 38.0 billion hours instead of 45.6 billion, each remaining hour must do the work of 45.6 ÷ 38.0, or about 1.20 hours today. That is the 20% gain.

The research did not simply cut everyone to 30 hours. It looked at each sector separately, and at full-timers and part-timers separately. A full-timer at 38.9 hours loses almost 9 hours. A part-timer at 23 hours loses nothing. Only by separating them does the true loss appear.

It also assumed that holidays, public holidays and sickness stay as they are today, so that yearly hours fall in proportion to weekly hours. Some choices would change the result:

Scenario, France 2023Hours leftExtra output per hour needed
Employees capped at 30 h; self-employed unchanged (main case)37.97 billion20.1%
Everyone capped, self-employed included35.33 billion29.1%
Main case, but full-timers give back two weeks of extra days off39.31 billion16.0%
Main case, using 2024 data—19.9%

The result barely moves between years. The main uncertainty is the self-employed. The two official sources disagree by about 21% on how many hours they work, so the research leaves them uncapped in the main case. UNIRA has not yet decided how the 30-hour rule applies to them.

Against history, two ways

The 32 years quoted above uses France's productivity growth from 2015 to 2019. The research also measured a second window, 2015 to 2023, which includes the years after the pandemic. In that window, output per hour grew by only about 0.06% a year. At that pace, a 20% gain would take about 301 years.

Neither figure is a forecast. Both say the same thing: ordinary productivity growth will not deliver a 30-hour week in any reasonable time.

For comparison: an enforced 35-hour week

France's legal week is already 35 hours, but full-timers actually work 38.9. What if 35 hours were enforced as a real cap?

France, employees capped30 hours35 hours
Hours lost16.7%7.2%
Extra output per hour needed, no offsets20.1%7.8%
Years of 2015–19 productivity growth3213
France's reserve of people who could work, as a share of the gap51–59%142–161%

At 35 hours, France already has more people willing and available to work than the hours it would lose. On central assumptions, output holds with no extra productivity at all. The research concludes that an enforced 35-hour week is within reach on today's evidence. A 30-hour week is about 2.5 times harder.

That is the price of the author's choice. It is a price paid in organisation, not in comfort, as the rest of the chapter shows.

Source one: work freed from selling

Chapter 2 showed that moderate reductions in advertising, speculative finance and similar activities would free about 4% of France's hours, and deep reductions about 6.6%. In UNIRA, where nobody competes to sell for profit, that is not a hope. It follows from the rules.

Source two: rested people work better

People who work shorter days are less tired, make fewer mistakes, and organise their work better. When France moved towards a 35-hour week around the year 2000, firms that cut hours raised output per hour enough to recover roughly 40–50% of the hours they gave up. That figure has an important caveat: those were volunteer firms receiving subsidies. Chapter 4 tells that story in full.

Evidence from many studies suggests that, in ordinary businesses, between 10% and 35% of lost hours come back through higher productivity. In hospitals, care homes and schools, where much of the work is simply being there for people, it is nearer 0–15%.

So shorter hours pay back part of their own cost. But not all of it, and less in care.

A closer look: how much does a tired hour cost?

Researchers call the share of lost hours that comes back through higher productivity theta. If theta is zero, output falls exactly in line with hours. If theta is one, output does not fall at all. The question is where real workplaces sit between the two.

Two studies matter most for a cut down to 30 hours.

The first looked at British munitions workers, mostly women, during the First World War. Their hours varied a great deal, and their output was carefully recorded. The economist Pencavel found that "below 49 weekly hours, variations in output are proportional to variations in hours". Above about 49 hours, tiredness set in, and extra hours added less and less. Output peaked at about 63 hours a week. So cutting very long weeks costs little. Cutting already moderate weeks costs almost in full.

The second study followed agents in a call centre, most of whom worked part time, around 24 hours a week. Their schedules were set centrally, which made a fair comparison possible. The finding: "an increase in working hours by 1 percent leads to an increase in output by only 0.9 percent". In other words, theta is about 0.1 in the range from 20 to 35 hours.

Put together, these studies say that the first few hours cut from a 39-hour week come partly back, and the hours cut below 35 come back much less. The research therefore treats the cut in two steps. From 38.9 to 35 hours it uses the evidence from real reforms. From 35 to 30 it uses the fatigue evidence. The combined central value for businesses is about 0.20 in the short run.

What about the famous four-day-week trials? In the United Kingdom in 2022, 61 volunteer firms with about 2,900 staff cut hours from about 38 to 34 a week, and reported revenue holding up. But the firms chose to take part, and there was no comparison group. The research does not use such trials as evidence for a whole economy. Firms that expect it to work are the ones that sign up.

Source three: everyone who can work, works

This is the largest source, and it is UNIRA's choice. Today, many adults who could work do not have a job, or work fewer hours than they want.

In France in 2023, about 2.3 million people were unemployed, and almost 4.8 million belonged to the wider group who want work: the unemployed, part-timers who want more hours, and people available for work but not searching.

The first lesson from history is sobering: cutting working hours does not, by itself, create jobs. The French 35-hour reform created jobs mainly where it was paid for by subsidies. Studies in Chile, Japan and Portugal found no net job creation. If UNIRA wants more people at work, it must organise it deliberately, with training, childcare, accessible jobs and the guaranteed salary.

A closer look: how many people really find work?

How fast can people who are out of work today be brought in? An earlier version of the research simply assumed that half of them would join in the first year. The current version measures it.

Eurostat publishes how many people in each group have a job one year later. For France in 2023:

GroupShare with a job one year laterBest three countries in Europe
Unemployed41%61%
Available but not searching, or searching but not available25%36%
Part-timers moving to full time23%—

These figures look encouraging, but they hide a trap. Most of these moves are ordinary churn. People find jobs every year anyway, and other people lose them. The pools refill. A 41% job-finding rate does not mean a new policy would add 41% of the unemployed to the workforce.

So the research asked a more modest question: what if France matched the best job-finding rates in Europe? That adds about 0.53 million people in the first year. The earlier assumption had been 1.8 million. The research calls this the most important correction in its labour work. Ordinary policy, even at its best, moves people slowly.

That is why UNIRA's rule matters. It does not wait for people to find jobs. It sets a target for how many adults work, and builds what is needed to reach it.

Universal work

What if UNIRA does that? In 2025, about 75.4% of French adults aged 20 to 64 had a job. The best countries in Europe reach about 83–86%: Iceland 85.8%, Malta 83.6% and the Netherlands 83.4%. UNIRA's principle is that everyone able to work, works.

Employment rate, ages 20–64New workers in FranceExtra output per hour still needed in year 1
Today's job-finding (best practice)about 0.5 million10.2%
84% (today's best in Europe)about 3.6 million2.6%
88% (beyond any country today)about 5.1 million0% — the gap is closed

With universal work at about 88%, France keeps today's output under a 30-hour week from the first year, on central assumptions. Every adult works less, and more adults work.

The same holds for Europe as a whole, treated as one pooled region, if a little higher. At 84%, Europe would still need 4.4% more output per hour. At 88%, 1.3%. At 92%, nothing.

These results assume that new workers are only 75% as productive as experienced ones in their first year. They also assume three conditions that the research names plainly:

  • workplaces, equipment and supervision for 4 to 5 million new workers in France in year one;
  • childcare and eldercare, so that parents and carers can work. Those care hours are themselves part of the demand;
  • an employment rate higher than any country has ever reached.

A closer look: the year-one ledger

The research brings all four sources together in what it calls a ledger: an account, in hours, of how the gap is filled.

Under today's job-finding at its best, the central case looks like this. A 30-hour week opens a gap of about 7.6 billion hours in France. It is filled:

  • 18% by the productivity response to shorter hours;
  • 6% by bringing people into work at the best rates seen in Europe;
  • 23% by reducing work that serves only selling;
  • the remaining 53%, about 8.9% of all hours, is uncovered.

That uncovered part is what produces the 10.2% figure in the table above. Then the research raised the employment rate step by step. At 84%, most of the uncovered part disappears. At 88%, all of it does.

Year-one case, FranceProductivity responseBringing people inSelling activity cutExtra output per hour still needed
Lowweakbest-practice gapmodest14.7%
Centralcentralbest-practice gapmoderate10.2%
Optimisticstrongfull best-practice ratesdeep0.5%
Universal work at 88%centralrule 14moderate0%

The Netherlands, and an uneven continent

The Netherlands offers a hint that this is not a dream. It has the shortest average working week in Europe, about 31 hours, and one of the highest employment rates, about 83.5%. Three in four of its people aged 55 to 64 are in work. Its own gap for a 30-hour week is the smallest in Europe, about 12.5%.

Across 30 European countries, those with shorter weeks tend to have more people in work. The link is moderate. That is a correlation, not proof, and many other things differ between countries. But it points the same way.

The reserve of people who could work is spread very unevenly. Spain, Italy and Greece hold large reserves, between about 55% and 126% of their own gaps. Countries that already employ most adults have little left: Germany about 25%, Sweden 35%, Czechia 22%, Iceland 19%. In those countries, the gap must be covered in other ways. For a world federation, that is an argument for regions helping one another, with people working where they already live.

Is a duty to work fair?

A rule that everyone able to work must work raises a fair worry. Could it become forced labour?

The draft constitution answers in three lines. Every adult able to work has the right and the duty to contribute, "with free choice of occupation". Illness, disability, caring duties, study and retirement exempt a person from the duty, "without loss of income or rights". And "no one may be forced into a particular job". The research notes that these conditions are what keep the rule compatible with the international conventions against forced labour.

So the duty is real, but it is a duty to contribute, not an order to take a given job. People who cannot work receive a guaranteed income. The guarantee is what makes the duty humane.

Source four: technology gives time back

Over the long run, technology raises output per hour. In most economies today, those gains become more consumption or more profit. In UNIRA, prices are fixed and nothing is produced only to be sold. So gains in productivity become something else: free time. The draft constitution writes this down: "Gains in productivity become shorter working hours, never higher prices or unemployment."

Our simulation of the UNIRA economy over 60 years shows what that means. If productivity grows by 1% a year, the working week can fall from 30 hours to about 23 hours within 30 years, with the same comfortable standard of living, and to about 17 hours after 60 years. At 2% a year, it falls to about 17 hours after 30 years, and about 9 after 60. Artificial intelligence and automation, which many people fear today, would become a source of time rather than unemployment.

There is a discipline hidden in this promise. If average pay drifted up faster than productivity, people would hold more money than there are goods to buy at fixed prices. The research found that a drift of just 0.5% a year leaves demand 13% above supply after 30 years. So the constitution adds a rule: average pay may rise only as fast as productivity. Time, not pay, is where the gains go. Chapter 6 explains why.

Where it is hard: care

One part of the economy does not fit easily: health and care. A nurse cannot care for more patients just because they are less tired. A care home needs staff present day and night.

The evidence on this is grim. A study of hospitals across nine countries found that each extra patient per nurse raised the odds of a patient dying in hospital by 7%. When Iceland moved its workers to a shorter week, most workplaces coped. Hospitals did not. Extra staff had to be hired, and about two-thirds of the extra cost was in health care.

Picture a hospital ward at night. This one is imagined. Two nurses cover twenty beds from nine in the evening to seven in the morning. Under a 30-hour week, each nurse works fewer nights a month. But the ward still needs two nurses every night, every week of the year. The hours that each nurse gives up must be worked by someone else. There is no faster way to sit with a frightened patient at three in the morning.

That is why the research treats care differently from a factory or an office. In a factory, a shorter day can be partly made up by better machines and better organisation. On a ward, most of the work is presence. If each worker's hours fall, coverage falls by the same amount, unless more people are hired. The evidence points to some indirect gains, such as less sickness absence and fewer staff leaving. But those gains are thin, and they do not replace people.

With a 30-hour week, France would need about 16.9% more people in health and social work. In detail:

OccupationWorking todayExtra neededTraining time
Nurses607,644about 103,0003 years
Doctors (upper bound)266,380about 45,00010 years for a GP
Care workers859,956about 145,000about 11 months for a care assistant

Training takes time: about 3 years for a nurse and 10 years for a general practitioner. No country can produce them in a year.

The pipeline is already tight. France trains about 24,800 nurses a year, while about 20,300 leave the profession. The spare capacity is only a few thousand a year. Even if nurse training doubled, it would take about 8 years to close the gap. For doctors, doubling intake would take about 16 years. And the health and social sector already has job vacancy rates of 2.9–3.8%.

What UNIRA can do about it

The research is clear that no training system can respond within the first year. If the 30-hour rule applies from day one, the right to care is at risk unless specific measures are designed. It names four:

  • care-assistant training for the people brought into work, since the course takes about 11 months;
  • voluntary return of qualified staff who have left the profession;
  • help between regions under the One Earth framework;
  • redesigning tasks, so that nurses spend more time on nursing.

Choosing among these is a decision for people and their representatives, not for a model. But one thing is certain. UNIRA must plan for care from the first day: training places, accessible courses for new workers, and help between regions. Chapter 8 returns to it at world scale.

And the world?

Chapter 1 gave the world's answer. If 80% of adults aged 15 to 64 worked 30 hours, the world would have about 88% of today's working hours. That 12% gap is smaller than France's, because the world has a larger reserve of people who could work.

There is a health bonus too. Today about 488 million people work 55 hours a week or more. In one year, those long hours caused about 745,000 deaths from heart disease and stroke. A 30-hour week removes that exposure entirely. Chapter 8 weighs what that means for health care.

Three questions a reader might ask

"Why not start at 35 hours and get to 30 later?" That would be easier, and the research shows it. But the author chose 30 hours from the start, for family time, and the research tests that choice rather than replacing it. The cost is clear: closing the gap in the first years depends on bringing people into work at a scale never seen.

"Will new workers really be as good as experienced ones?" Not at first. The research assumes they are 75% as productive in their first year. That is a guess, not a measurement, and it is listed below as something to test.

"What if universal work fails?" Then the gap reopens. Under today's best job-finding rates, France would be about 9% short of today's output. The research is honest that the gap closes only under the optimistic cases. Something would have to give: higher productivity over time, fewer goods, or a temporary shortfall managed so that essential needs come first.

"Will I earn less if I work less?" In UNIRA, pay is not counted in euros at all, so there is no simple before-and-after. The research did test what a base salary buys. A worker on the lowest salary, 5,000 U a month for a 30-hour week, who saves the maximum of half of it, can still buy about 86% of what an average French person consumes today. By the research's measure, half the people of France, Germany, Italy and Spain would already be better off at that level, even at the bottom of the scale. And health care and education are free on top. Chapter 6 explains how the money works.

"Who looks after the children while more parents work?" This is a real cost, and the research counts it as one. Childcare and eldercare are among the conditions for universal work. Their hours are part of the demand the new workers must meet, not a free extra. A society that wants more parents at work, and shorter days for all, must build the care that lets both happen.

Half past three, revisited

Return to the father at the school gate in Lille.

For him to be there at half past three, many things must be true at once. His employer must have reorganised the work, so that six hours do more than they did. Some of the work that once served only selling must have stopped. His neighbour, who had no job, must now be working too, perhaps in the after-school club where the girl's friends play. And somewhere a care home must have found the extra staff it needs, trained in a course that started a year earlier.

None of these is impossible. Most have happened somewhere, on a smaller scale. What has never happened is all of them together, at once, across a whole region. That is the test UNIRA sets itself.

What the evidence says

  • A 30-hour week in France, with nothing else changing, needs about 20% more output per hour: about 32 years of recent productivity growth. An enforced 35-hour week needs only 7.8%, and is within reach today.
  • Shorter hours partly pay for themselves: 10–35% of lost hours come back in ordinary businesses, 0–15% in care.
  • Cutting hours alone creates no jobs. Bringing more adults into work must be organised. Matching Europe's best job-finding rates adds only about 0.53 million people in year one.
  • With about 88% of adults aged 20–64 at work, France keeps today's output from year 1. Europe's best countries are at about 83–86% today.
  • Technology can then reduce the week further: about 23 hours after 30 years at 1% productivity growth.
  • Health and care are the bottleneck: about 103,000 nurses and 145,000 care workers are needed, and training takes years.

What we still don't know

  • No country has ever reached 88% employment. Whether it can be done, and how fast, is the central question of this chapter.
  • New workers may be less productive in their first year. We assumed 75% as productive, an assumption to test.
  • The evidence on productivity comes from cuts of about 10% in working hours. A cut from 39 to 30 hours is larger than anything studied.
  • How the 30-hour rule applies to the self-employed is not yet decided, and their hours are uncertain.
  • The workplaces and equipment that millions of new workers need take time to build. That lead time is not yet modelled.
  • A shorter paid week may shift some time into unpaid work at home. That is not counted.

---
