Chapter 4 — What we learned from the past

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA evidence reviews (V6.2, V6.3, V6.9, V7.1, V7.2) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Imagine a woman in a small town in East Germany, in the summer of 1990.

She is in her fifties. She has worked in the same factory for most of her life. On 1 July her money changes. Her wages are converted one for one into the West German mark. So are the prices in the shops. Her first 4,000 marks of savings also convert one for one. The rest of her savings convert at two old marks for one new one.

By the autumn, the factory is in trouble. Its products now cost as much as Western ones, and few people want them. Within a few months, output in the region's industry falls to about half of what it was the year before. Her colleagues start to be laid off. Later, so is she.

This woman is a composite, not a real person. But the numbers behind her story are real, and they appear later in this chapter. She stands for a simple truth: big changes have been tried before. Some went well, some badly, most both.

UNIRA proposes a very large change. This chapter looks at six earlier experiments, and asks what each one teaches.

The 35-hour week in France

France cut its legal working week by law. Under the Aubry laws, the legal week went from 39 to 35 hours. On paper, that is a cut of 10.3%.

What happened in practice was smaller. Some firms met the target by no longer counting breaks and holidays as work time. Across the whole economy, actual hours fell by a little under 5% on one official measure, and a little over 6% in the national accounts.

Even today, French full-time employees report about 38.9 hours in a usual week. A law on hours is not the same as fewer hours.

What worked. In the firms that signed up early and received support, the government's own evaluation found that output per hour rose by about 4 to 5%. Productivity gains covered roughly 40 to 50% of the hours cut. Planners had expected only a third. People worked better when they worked less.

When Portugal cut its legal week from 44 to 40 hours, output per hour rose by about 4.4%. In Iceland, about 2,500 public workers moved from 40 hours to 35 or 36, with no pay cut. Productivity and service "remained the same or improved" in most of the workplaces in the trials.

What did not work as hoped. The 35-hour week was also meant to share work and create jobs. The official estimate is about 350,000 jobs over the years 1998 to 2002. But those jobs came together with large cuts in payroll taxes and with wage moderation. Studies that compared France with itself more carefully, region against region or large firms against small ones, found no clear effect on total employment. One concluded that "aggregate employment was unaffected but labour turnover increased."

In Japan, the move from 48 to 40 hours "did not contribute to job creation". Cutting hours does not create jobs on its own.

A closer look: why careful studies disagree with hopeful ones

How can one study find 350,000 new jobs and another find none? The answer lies in what each one compares, and it is a lesson for judging any reform, including UNIRA.

The government's evaluation looked at firms that signed up early for the 35-hour week and received large cuts in payroll taxes. In those firms, effective working time fell by a little more than 10%, and employment rose by about 6 to 7%. The planners had expected productivity to make up a third of the cut. The evaluation concluded it made up 40 to 50%.

But there was a catch, which the authors stated. They did not measure output directly. They assumed that output stayed the same, and worked out productivity from the hours and jobs. Another study compared the same kind of volunteer firms with firms still at 39 hours. It found employment up by about 9.9%. But the firms that switched early had already been growing faster before the reform. They were not a fair comparison.

Two later studies looked for fairer comparisons. One used a quirk of French history. In Alsace-Moselle, the 35-hour rules were applied more weakly than in the rest of France. If shorter hours created jobs, employment there should have grown less. It did not differ significantly. The other compared large firms with small ones. It found that "aggregate employment was unaffected but labour turnover increased."

The pattern is common. When firms choose a reform, and are paid to adopt it, the results look good. When a reform falls on everyone, the gains shrink. The same caution applies to the four-day-week trials discussed in Chapter 3. UNIRA's 30-hour week would fall on everyone. So the research uses the cautious estimates, not the hopeful ones.

Other countries, other cuts

France is not the only country that has cut its working week by law. The research gathered the best studies it could find:

Country and reformWhat happened to jobs
France, 1982: 40 to 39 hoursDirectly affected workers lost jobs, by "between 2% and 4%"; minimum-wage workers were hit hardest
Germany, 1984–94: union agreementsThe main study concluded that shorter hours "may have reduced employment"
Portugal, 1996: 44 to 40 hoursAbout 2% fewer jobs, mainly through fewer hires
Japan: 48 to 40 hours, phased"Did not contribute to job creation"; each hour cut in law cut only about 0.14 hours in practice
Chile, 2005: 48 to 45 hours"No significant effects" on moving into or out of work
Five European reforms, 1995–2007Full work-sharing rejected; small, uncertain effects

The Japanese figure is worth pausing on. For each hour cut from the legal week, actual hours fell by only about a seventh of an hour. A law on hours is not the same as fewer hours, as France also learned.

Some reforms did raise output per hour. In South Korea, a move from 44 to 40 hours raised output per worker in manufacturing plants by about 1.5%, even though each worker worked less. But Korean weeks started very long. Chapter 3 showed that cutting very long weeks costs little, because tired workers produce little in their last hours. The Korean result does not carry over to a cut from 39 to 30 hours.

Health care is different. In Iceland, most workplaces absorbed the shorter week. Hospitals could not. Extra staff had to be hired, and about two-thirds of the extra cost of the national rollout was in health care. A nurse cannot care for a patient faster just because their shift is shorter. And research across nine countries found that each extra patient per nurse raised the odds of a patient dying in hospital by 7%.

Other studies looked at nurses' hours directly, from the other side. In one, errors were more likely when shifts lasted more than twelve hours, or when nurses worked more than forty hours a week. In another, covering 31,627 nurses in 12 countries, very long shifts were linked to exhaustion and to nurses wanting to leave. Long hours harm care. But the lesson does not run backwards. Cutting a reasonable week further does not make each nurse able to cover more beds.

What UNIRA learns. Chapter 3 showed that a 30-hour week in France would need about 20% more output per hour if nothing else changed. The French and Portuguese experience suggests that some of that will come naturally from shorter, less tiring days. It will not come for free, and it will not come in hospitals.

So UNIRA does not rely on shorter hours alone. Its rule that everyone able to work does work answers the jobs lesson. People excluded today must be brought in deliberately, with training, childcare and eldercare. This has never been done at the scale UNIRA needs.

An enforced 35-hour week in France looks within reach on today's evidence. A 30-hour week is about 2.5 times harder. UNIRA has chosen 30 hours anyway, for family time. That choice carries a real risk.

Fixed prices without balance: the shortage economy

UNIRA proposes that the prices of essentials, like food and health, never change. This sounds reassuring. History says it is also dangerous.

The Soviet Union and other planned economies kept the prices of staple goods frozen for decades. The economist Kornai studied what followed, in his book Economics of Shortage (1980).

The result was not stability. It was queues, rationing, informal markets and ever-rising subsidies. The fixed price held. The goods did not.

A price is not only a number on a label. It also matches what people want to buy with what exists. If the price cannot move, something else must. In the shortage economy, that was queues, favours and empty shelves.

Shortage also feeds itself. When people fear that goods will run out, they buy more than they need. We saw this in a modern, rich country. In Ireland in March 2020, at the start of the pandemic, grocery spending rose by 23% and toilet-roll sales by 181%. Scarcity, or the fear of it, drives hoarding.

Planned economies had a second problem: units reported what suited them, not what was true. For the Soviet case, the UNIRA review found only descriptions, no reliable numbers. But the problem is not only historical. In China, local statistics inflated growth by 1.7 percentage points a year between 2008 and 2016.

What UNIRA learns. This is perhaps the most important warning in the whole book. UNIRA keeps fixed prices for essentials. So the burden of balance moves entirely to physical supply. Several design rules exist because of this lesson:

  • production is planned to measured needs, not to a target that suits a factory;
  • each region keeps reserves of goods, sized to the shocks it has actually faced;
  • total money income must not exceed the value of the essentials available at the fixed price;
  • a yearly cap on personal money keeps savings from piling up into demand that cannot be met.

There is a hopeful side. UNIRA's research estimates that security could avoid 25 to 75% of panic-buying spikes. That is a model estimate, not a measurement. Security does not curb the use of energy, travel and data, so those stay metered, unit by unit.

Whether these rules are enough is not proven. This is the failure UNIRA must never repeat.

A closer look: testing UNIRA against its own warning

The research did not just quote Kornai. It built a model of UNIRA's own money, for the French region, and looked for the shortage economy inside it.

It found it. In the first design, every household saved half its income. Only a small part of that saving could become new homes, because builders can only build so fast. The rest piled up as unspent money: about 1,610 billion U a year. After 30 years, the pile was worth 21 years of everything households consume. At fixed prices, that money would be claims on goods that do not exist. If people ever tried to spend it, the result would be exactly the queues and empty shelves that fixed prices are meant to prevent.

This is why UNIRA has a money cap. With a low cap and zero-interest credit for homes, the model's unspent money settles at about 12 months of consumption and stays there. Without a cap, it reaches 152 months after 60 years and keeps rising. Chapter 6 explains how the cap works.

The model found a second, slower leak. If average pay drifts upwards a little faster than productivity, demand at fixed prices runs ahead of supply. So the draft constitution adds a rule: average pay may rise only as fast as productivity.

These are not proofs that UNIRA will avoid shortage. They show that its designers looked for the danger where Kornai found it, and wrote rules against it before anyone has to queue.

Is UNIRA just the old planned economy again?

It is a fair question, and the honest answer is: partly, and that is why this chapter matters.

UNIRA shares two features with the Soviet system. A world entity decides which region produces what. And prices of essentials are fixed. Both failed badly in the past.

It differs in others. Regions join by referendum and may leave by vote. The plan starts from published, measured needs, with the uncertainty stated, not from targets that suit a ministry. Every allocation is public. Independent auditors check every region. People choose their own jobs. Money cannot pile up beyond a cap. And reserves are sized to the shocks each region has actually faced.

One problem remains unsolved, and the research names it. Deciding production for about 8 billion people and millions of products is what economists call the knowledge problem. Modern planning tools and computers make parts of it manageable. Preferences, quality and local conditions still need regional knowledge and freedom. Whether a world allocator can manage this is one of the tests by which UNIRA could be proven wrong. Chapter 16 lists it.

The welfare state: guarantees and how they leak

Many countries built public systems that guarantee health care, education, pensions and help for those who cannot work. UNIRA builds on that idea. Health care and education are free. People who cannot work receive a guaranteed income. Retirees receive an income set by need.

The UNIRA research did not study the welfare state as a whole. It studied how well public help reaches the people it is meant for. Three lessons stand out.

People do not waste help. A common fear is that money given to poor people will be spent on drink or cigarettes. Across 44 estimates from 19 studies of cash transfers, there was no increase in spending on alcohol and tobacco.

Targeting the poor is hard. Tests that try to identify poor households from a few indicators make large mistakes. In nine African countries, they classified 80% of poor households as not poor. Brazil's Bolsa Família missed 59% of the people it was meant for in 2004, and reached many it was not meant for. It was still among the most progressive programmes in the world. Imperfect help is still help.

Money leaks, and leaks can be fixed. In Uganda in the early 1990s, only 13% of school grants reached the schools. After newspapers began publishing how much each school should receive, the share captured along the way fell from about 80% to under 20%. Random public audits in Brazil cut corruption by 7.9%, and by 12.7% with repeat audits.

What UNIRA learns. Universal guarantees avoid the worst targeting errors, because everyone is covered. But leakage is a real risk for any large system. UNIRA's safeguards, in Chapter 14, draw on these results: public information, independent audits and electronic payments.

The European Union: joining step by step

The European Union is a voluntary union of nations. It is the closest real example of what UNIRA proposes.

It grew slowly, by choice. The union began with 6 members in 1958 and grew to 27, in waves. Nobody was forced to join. Norway voted no twice, and that was respected. The United Kingdom left in 2020. Entry was not automatic: candidates had to meet published conditions, the Copenhagen criteria.

Small transfers, big convergence. Rich members send money to poorer ones through what is called cohesion policy. For the donors, the cost was small: only 0.3 to 0.4% of their output. For the receivers, it was large. Between 2010 and 2017, net transfers were 4.0% of output in Hungary, 2.7% in Poland, 2.2% in Slovakia and 1.9% in Czechia.

The countries that joined in 2004 rose from 60% to 80% of the EU's average income. Poland's story is the clearest. In 1995, its income per person was 44% of the EU average. By 2025 it was 81%.

What did not go perfectly. Not all money flowed one way. Profits sent home to foreign owners of firms in these countries were larger than the EU transfers: 4.2 to 7.6% of output. Most older members also kept their job markets closed to the new members for some years. For Bulgaria and Romania, those limits lasted until 2014.

In 2023, Europe's auditors found irregular spending of 9.3% in cohesion funds. Irregular does not mean fraud, but leakage never reaches zero, even in well-organised systems.

What UNIRA learns. UNIRA's transition rules copy the EU's best features. Regions join by referendum, in three stages: observer, associate and full member. Each stage has published criteria. Regions may leave by democratic vote.

Modest transfers worked, but equalisation took decades. UNIRA's own model agrees: closing the gaps in energy, health and housing takes many years.

German reunification: the shock of one day

Return to the woman in the opening scene. Her story is the story of German monetary union, on 1 July 1990.

What worked. Small savings were protected. Adults could convert up to 4,000 marks at one for one. For children up to 14, the limit was 2,000. For people aged 60 or more, it was 6,000. Larger savings, company debts and housing loans were converted at two for one. Debts and savings were treated on the same terms.

What failed. Wages and prices were converted one for one in a single day, at a politically chosen rate. East German firms suddenly had to pay Western wages and compete at world prices. Only 8% of workers were in firms that could still cover their running costs.

Within a few months, industrial output fell to about half its 1989 level. More than 3 million jobs, about a third, disappeared. Within two years, real output fell to two-thirds of its 1989 level.

The agency set up to sell off East German firms, the Treuhand, handled about 8,500 firms with more than 4 million employees. 2.5 million were laid off. It closed with a deficit of DM 256.4 billion. Buyers pledged 1.5 million jobs, and 20 to 30% of those promised jobs never appeared.

Property was another trap. The two German states agreed on "restitution before compensation": property taken in the past would go back to former owners where possible. This created uncertainty over who owned what, and slowed investment.

What UNIRA learns. UNIRA's transition rules are written almost line by line against this experience:

  • there is no single "political" exchange rate for a whole economy;
  • small savings are protected in full, up to the money cap;
  • homes people live in are never seized;
  • old property claims are settled by compensation, never by giving property back;
  • new members receive support to keep their production running during the change.

One risk remains. UNIRA pays everyone on the same scale, from 5,000 to 25,000 U. UNIRA enterprises do not compete on price, which removes the mechanism that destroyed East German firms. But where a region's physical productivity is low, the gap is still there, and it must be modelled, not assumed away.

Debt relief: freeing poor countries to spend on people

Many of the world's poorest countries once spent more on paying debts than on their people. Before relief, the countries in the international debt-relief programmes for heavily indebted poor countries spent more on debt service than on health and education combined.

What worked. Two programmes cancelled much of that debt. They cost about USD 76.2 billion and USD 43.3 billion, about USD 119.5 billion together. 36 of the 39 eligible countries completed the process.

The effect was large. In 2017, these countries spent 7.3% of their output on reducing poverty, against 1.9% on debt service. After relief, they spent about five times more on social services than on debt.

What it costs. Across 180 government debt restructurings in 68 countries, lenders lost 37% on average. Countries that imposed bigger losses then paid higher interest and took longer to borrow again.

What UNIRA learns. UNIRA goes further than any of these programmes. When a region becomes a full member, all its public debts are cancelled. Inside one world entity, a debt between regions would be the entity owing money to itself.

Two lessons shape how. Savers and pension funds holding government bonds are protected: their bonds count as their savings, up to the money cap. Debts owed to lenders outside UNIRA are paid on a schedule until their regions join, because refusing to pay outside lenders has historically brought lawsuits and asset seizures.

Wealth taxes: the problem of the open door

UNIRA caps how much money any person may hold. Money above the cap goes to the One Earth Fund. The closest past experiments are wealth taxes, and they carry a warning.

France taxed large fortunes for decades. In 2007 the tax raised about €4.42 billion, about 1.5% of tax receipts. But wealthy people could leave. In 2006 alone, 843 people left France because of the tax, taking a net €2.8 billion with them. The tax was abolished in 2017–18.

Norway raised its wealth and dividend taxes in 2022. At least 30 very rich people left that year, more than in the previous 13 years combined. Those who moved to Switzerland held about NOK 29 billion.

The lesson is precise. The losses did not come from mass flight. They came from a few hundred very wealthy households who moved. As long as the door is open, a cap on wealth in one place leaks to another.

What UNIRA learns. UNIRA keeps the door open: every person may emigrate, and every region may leave. So the cap is designed against flight. It applies to wealth held on the day a region votes to join, and transfers abroad after the vote still count. It phases in over 10 years. And it applies across the whole federation, not one country at a time. These rules reduce the risk. They do not remove it.

What this means for UNIRA

The changes that worked were voluntary, staged and rule-based. The EU grew over decades, by referendum, with clear conditions. Debt relief followed published rules. Small savings in Germany were protected by a simple, fair limit.

The changes that failed were sudden, or ignored physical reality. East Germany converted a whole economy in one day. The Soviet Union fixed prices without making sure the goods existed. France hoped a shorter week would create jobs on its own.

UNIRA's design learns from both sides. But honesty requires saying where it goes further than any past experiment:

  • 30 hours from the start. No evaluated reform has cut working time this far. All the good evidence comes from cuts of about 10% or less.
  • Fixed prices forever. The economies that froze prices did not avoid shortages. UNIRA's physical rules are designed for this, but they are untested at scale.
  • A money cap. Wealth taxes leak when wealthy people can leave, as France and Norway found. UNIRA's ten-year phase-in and anti-flight rule reduce this risk but do not remove it.
  • One world planner. No allocator in the evidence has worked at this scale.

The past does not prove that UNIRA will work. It shows which mistakes to avoid, and which promises to keep modest.

Six experiments at a glance

ExperimentWhat workedWhat failedUNIRA's answer
France's 35-hour weekOutput per hour rose in firms that adopted itNo net jobs without subsidies; actual hours fell less than the lawUniversal work organised on purpose; care staff planned separately
Fixed prices in planned economiesPrices stayed fixedQueues, rationing, hoarding, false reportsPhysical balance, reserves, money cap, audited data
Welfare and public programmesCash is not wasted; transparency cuts leakageTargeting misses the poor; money leaksUniversal guarantees; public register; audits
European UnionVoluntary, staged joining; modest transfersSlow convergence; leakage stays above 5%Three-stage joining; right to leave; needs-based transfers
German reunificationSmall savings protectedOne-day conversion; restitution froze investmentNo political exchange rate; compensation, never restitution
Debt reliefMore spending on peopleLenders' losses raise future borrowing costsPublic debts cancelled inside UNIRA; outside lenders paid on a schedule

What the evidence says

  • France's 35-hour week raised output per hour in early-adopting firms by about 4 to 5%, covering 40 to 50% of the hours cut. It did not, on its own, create jobs across the economy.
  • Studies of firms that chose and were paid to cut hours look better than studies of reforms that fell on everyone. The fair comparisons, in France, Portugal, Japan, Chile and Germany, find no net job creation.
  • Health care cannot absorb shorter hours by working faster.
  • Fixed prices without physical balance produced queues and rationing. The price held; the goods did not.
  • Leakage in public programmes is real, but transparency and audits cut it sharply.
  • The EU's voluntary, staged enlargement worked. Transfers of about 2 to 4% of the receiver's output, costing donors 0.3 to 0.4%, helped the 2004 entrants rise from 60% to 80% of the EU average.
  • Converting East Germany's economy in one day left only 8% of workers in viable firms.
  • Wealth taxes leaked through a few hundred wealthy households who moved: 843 left France in 2006, at least 30 left Norway in 2022.
  • Debt relief of about USD 119.5 billion let poor countries spend 7.3% of output on poverty reduction, against 1.9% on debt.

What we still don't know

  • Whether productivity gains from a cut from 39 to 35 hours continue from 35 to 30. The best evidence near 30 hours suggests output becomes close to proportional to hours.
  • Whether people excluded from work today can be brought in at the scale UNIRA needs. It has never been done.
  • Whether UNIRA's physical rules, reserves and money cap can keep fixed prices stable through centuries of shocks. No economy has tried.
  • Whether wealthy people will move money out before a region votes to join, despite the anti-flight rule.
  • Much of this evidence comes from Europe. It may not transfer to very different regions.
  • Several figures in the research files were found only in search summaries and are not used here. Some lessons, such as the Soviet "ratchet effect", rest on descriptions rather than numbers.

---

Chapter 5 — One Earth, many regions

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.3–V7.6) and the draft Constitution of One Earth, and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Imagine a morning market in a town anywhere on Earth: in Morocco, in Peru, in Indonesia. The call to prayer, or the church bells, or the temple drums sound at the hour they have always sounded. Children go to school in their own language. The town's festival takes place on the same day as for centuries.

And yet something has changed. The new clinic at the edge of town was built with resources allocated by need. Every unit is published in a register anyone can read. The town's young builders were trained through a programme paid for by the One Earth Fund. Nobody in the capital of a distant, powerful country decided any of it.

The town council still argues about the market's opening hours. The regional parliament still debates the school timetable. Those quarrels belong to the people who live there, and nobody outside may settle them.

This town is imagined, a composite of many places. But each part of the picture comes from the draft Constitution of One Earth. This is what UNIRA proposes: one Earth, many regions. This chapter explains how it is meant to work without any region ruling another. It also shows where the design is still untested.

What a region is

The first article of the draft constitution is short. The Earth is one federation, called One Earth. Its members are regions.

What is a region? The constitution answers: "today's countries, or parts or unions of them, as their people decide." So a region could be France or India as they are today. It could be part of a large country, if its people choose. Or it could be several small countries that decide to join together.

The research worked with today's countries as regions. Its voting tests used 217 regions, covering the world's 8.12 billion people in 2024.

Each region has its own democratic government and parliament, elected by its people. One Earth cannot dissolve a regional government. The only exception is a ruling of the Court of One Earth, for a grave breach of rights.

What stays local, and what is shared

The constitution follows a principle called subsidiarity. In plain words: decisions are taken as close to the people as possible.

Each region governs itself according to its own religion, culture, language and traditions. The constitution names four areas that belong to the region:

  • education;
  • local planning;
  • public holidays;
  • daily life.

Each region also controls its own borders: who may enter, stay, work and settle, under its own visa rules.

One Earth decides only what must be decided together. The constitution lists six things:

  • shared resources;
  • allocation between regions;
  • the currency;
  • rights;
  • the environment;
  • peace.

That list is deliberately short. Everything not on it stays with the regions.

Culture and belief

A region may give a religion, culture or language an official or traditional place in public life. A region with a state church may keep it. A region with a national language may keep it.

But every person keeps freedom of thought, conscience and belief. The constitution spells this out:

  • no one may be forced into a religion or belief;
  • no one may be punished for their religion or belief;
  • no one may be punished for changing it, or for having none.

Minorities in every region are protected in their language, culture and worship.

So the balance is this. The region shapes public life. The person keeps their own conscience. Neither may erase the other.

An imagined case

Picture a family who speak a minority language in their region. Their children attend a school run by the region, in the region's main language. The family also want their children to learn their own language and keep their own festivals.

Under the constitution, the region still sets the school system. That is part of "education", which stays local. But the family's language and worship are protected. If they were punished for them, they could take their case to the Court of One Earth. Every person may bring a case there.

This example is imagined. It shows how the two levels are meant to fit together. It does not show how a real court would rule.

Borders and movement

Each region controls its own borders. That surprises some readers. If the world is one humanity, why keep borders at all?

The author's answer is about choice and culture. A region decides who settles there, as today. But the constitution also guarantees three things to every person:

  • they may leave any region, including their own;
  • they may always return to their own region;
  • they may move freely within their own region.

Entry to another region follows that region's visa rules. These rules must apply equally, without discrimination by origin, religion or ethnicity.

Protection in emergencies

A person whose life is threatened by persecution or disaster is protected. The constitution sets an order:

  1. First, in their own region, through rebuilding and needs-first allocation by One Earth.
  2. Where that is impossible, other regions provide temporary shelter. The burden is shared among regions by their means and population, through the One Earth Fund.
  3. People return home when it is safe again.

Would people still need to flee?

The hope behind these rules is simple. In a world without war, persecution or poverty, people should rarely need to leave home. The research tested that hope against the evidence. It found it largely right for refugees, wrong for disasters, and overstated for economic migration.

Refugees. At the end of 2025, 117.8 million people were forcibly displaced. That is about one person in 70 worldwide. It was down from 123.2 million a year earlier, the first fall in a decade.

Forcibly displaced, end of 2025Number
Refugees41.6 million
Asylum-seekers9 million
People displaced inside their own country by conflict68.7 million
Total117.8 million

UNHCR, which publishes these counts, defines "forcibly displaced" by its causes. They are persecution, conflict, violence, human rights violations and serious public disorder. Those are exactly the causes UNIRA says it removes.

When wars end, many people go home. In 2025, 14.7 million people returned. Chapter 12 tells that story in more detail.

Disasters. Here peace does not help. In 2024, disasters forced people from their homes 45.8 million times. That was the highest number since records began in 2008. The 15-year average is about 24 million a year.

About 99% of those displacements were caused by weather. Storms caused 25.2 million and floods 19.1 million. Many were short evacuations before a storm arrived. Almost all happened inside the person's own country.

In 2024 disasters displaced more than twice as many people as conflict did: 45.8 million against 20.1 million. A just world does not stop the weather. That is why protection starts in the person's own region, with good homes and quick rebuilding.

The future may bring more. The World Bank's Groundswell report projects up to 216 million people moving within their own countries because of climate by 2050. Strong climate and development action could cut that by up to 80%. That would still leave about 43 million.

Economic migration. The United Nations counted about 281 million international migrants in 2020. That is less than 4% of the world's population. Refugees and asylum-seekers were 12% of them. So roughly 88% of migrants were not forced to move.

Most of them moved for a better life. The same worker earns about 2.7 times more in the United States than at home, in the median country. For a worker from the Philippines the gap is about 3.5 times. For Haiti it is more than 7 times.

In UNIRA, pay is on one scale everywhere. That income gap is the main motive, and it would largely disappear. But movement would not. Even rich countries have a few per cent of their people living abroad, for family, study, work or simple preference. The research judges that about 2–4% of people living outside their home region is plausible. That figure is an assumption, not a measurement.

A closer look: the "migration hump"

One finding deserves a warning. As poor countries grow richer, emigration does not fall at first. It rises.

Research by Clemens found that emigration rises until a country reaches about PPP$10,000 of output per person. ("PPP" means the figures are adjusted for local prices.) Before that point, doubling output per person goes with about 35% more emigration overall, and 74% more to rich countries.

Output per personShare of people living abroad
About PPP$1,000about 4%
About PPP$10,000about 11%

So while poorer regions catch up under UNIRA, pressure to move may grow for a while before it falls. Regional visa rules would face that pressure in exactly the years when catching up is fastest.

A closer look: how these numbers were checked

Every migration figure above was read in source text that the research fetched. Two official websites, those of UNHCR and of IDMC, which counts disaster displacement, blocked automated access. For those, the research used exact copies of the official reports held elsewhere. The links are listed in the research files.

Figures that rest on judgement are marked as assumptions. Two are used here: the 2–4% of people living abroad, and the return rates after peace. A third figure, 29.9 million disaster displacements in 2025, was seen only in a search summary. It is not used as a fact.

An honest tension

The research flags one problem with regional border control. It works only if visas cannot be used to shut out people fleeing disasters, or seeking protection.

If a region could refuse everyone, its "control" would collide with the people the constitution promises to protect. The research suggested a different default: free or easy movement between regions, with duties to admit people in emergencies.

The draft constitution chose regional control, plus shared emergency shelter. Whether that balance holds in a real crisis is not yet known.

Fairness first, decided by data and science

The preamble of the draft constitution names its core in one sentence: "Fairness is the core of this Constitution."

Article 3 turns that into four principles for all public action:

  • Evidence: decisions use data and science, published and open to challenge;
  • Equity: resources go first to unmet needs;
  • Justice: rights are equal and enforceable;
  • Sharing: what the Earth provides belongs to all, and its use is accounted for openly.

Data and science in every decision

Article 3a makes the first principle concrete. Every decision on production, allocation, prices, salaries, the money cap, the environment and public services must rest on measured data and the best available science.

The data, the methods and the reasons are published, with their uncertainty. Anyone can check them.

An independent Scientific Council reviews the methods. It is drawn from all regions and all relevant sciences. Its opinions are public. A decision that departs from its advice must say why.

And rules can be wrong. When new evidence shows a rule is wrong, it is reviewed. Some rules carry their own tests of success and failure. Chapter 14 describes the most important one: a built-in alarm on the allocation system.

We produce what we need, for all regions

The same article sets the rule for production:

  • production is planned from measured needs and population in every region, plus studied reserves for catastrophes;
  • no more is produced than is needed;
  • no region is left without what it needs.

This is the opposite of producing to sell. Nothing is made only to keep sales going.

How resources are shared

Here is the heart of the economic design. Each year, new capacity goes to regions by measured needs and number of people, needs first.

What counts as new capacity? The constitution lists five things:

  • energy systems;
  • homes;
  • health workers trained in their own region;
  • food;
  • materials.

Three separate bodies handle this, and none may do another's job.

BodyIts jobIts limits
Statistical OfficeMeasures needs in every region at least once a year, with stated uncertaintyIndependent of all other bodies; uniform, public methods
Allocation AuthorityApplies a public formula to those needsNo power to favour any region
Audit AuthorityChecks every region every yearBudget fixed by the constitution

Changing the formula takes a full law, passed by the two chambers described below.

Resources are not allocated by politicians. That is the point of the design. A minister cannot send an extra power station to a friendly region. The formula decides, and the register shows what it decided.

What "needs first" means in practice

In the research's 30-year world model, the rule is simple. Each year, new capacity goes first to the regions furthest below the comfortable level.

Take energy. The comfortable level is 40 gigajoules of final energy per person per year (Chapter 10). A region far below it receives new solar and wind first. A region above it receives none, and slowly reduces its use.

Surplus beyond a region's needs goes to the One Earth Fund for sharing.

What is not yet decided

One honest gap must be named. The constitution sets the principle, needs first and by population. It does not yet give the exact formula. The author's notes list it as work still to be done.

That matters. A formula decides who gets what, and every region will study it closely. Chapter 16 lists it among the open questions.

A tension to watch: one planner, many regions

There is a second tension in the design, and the research names it openly.

One of UNIRA's design rules says that One Earth decides which region produces what. Everything is then sent to regions by population and need. The research recorded a question beside that rule: is it compatible with local autonomy, and with "no forced relocation"?

Two answers come from the research itself.

First, people are never moved to production. Production goes to where people are. Labour and production are pooled across regions by placing work where people are available.

Second, the research admits the "knowledge problem". Deciding production for about 8 billion people and millions of products is very hard. Modern planning tools and computers make parts of it manageable. But preferences, quality and local conditions still need regional information and regional decisions.

So the balance is not settled. One Earth plans the large flows: energy systems, homes, health workers. Regions know what their people want. How these two levels share information, without one swallowing the other, is a design question still open.

No region rules another

How can 217 regions of very different sizes govern together? Neither the giants nor a crowd of small regions should dominate. The draft constitution answers with two chambers.

The Assembly of the Peoples

The Assembly has 1,000 members. They are elected every five years by direct, secret, universal vote.

Seats depend on population, but not in direct proportion. They follow the square root of each region's population. This rule was proposed by the mathematician Lionel Penrose. The constitution adopts it because it aims to give every citizen equal voting power in a union of regions.

What does a square root do? It shrinks big differences. A region with 100 times the people of another gets 10 times the seats, not 100 times.

Two limits complete the rule:

  • every region has at least 1 seat;
  • no region may hold more than 5% of all seats.

The research tested the rule on 2024 populations.

RegionShare of seats if seats followed populationShare under the square-root rule
India16.5%4.4%
China16.0%4.4%
United States3.9%2.1%
Indonesia3.2%2.0%
Pakistan2.9%1.8%
India and China together32.5%8.8%

Under the square-root rule, no region comes near the 5% ceiling. The ceiling is a safety rail, not a working limit.

The Council of Regions

The second chamber is simpler. Each region sends one representative with one equal vote. Large or small, rich or poor, every region counts the same.

How a law passes

A law needs both chambers.

  1. A majority in the Assembly.
  2. A double majority in the Council:
    • at least 55% of regions;
    • representing at least 65% of the world's population, counted by the square root of each region's population.

The thresholds of 55% and 65% follow the Council of the European Union. The author agreed them on 3 November 2026.

There are no permanent seats, no vetoes, and no privileges of size, wealth or history.

A vote, step by step

Rules like these are easier to follow with an example. The vote below is imagined. The thresholds and the tested numbers come from the research.

Suppose a proposal is tabled to change the allocation formula. That needs a full law.

Step 1: the Assembly. The 1,000 members debate and vote. A majority must vote yes.

Step 2: count the regions. In the Council, 55% of 217 regions is 119.35. Votes come in whole regions, so at least 120 must vote yes.

Step 3: weigh the regions. Each region carries a weight based on the square root of its population. The yes regions together must carry at least 65% of the total weight.

Now imagine two outcomes.

Outcome A. India and China both vote no. Together they carry about 8.8% of the square-root weight. That leaves over 91% of the weight with the other 215 regions. If enough of them agree, the law can still pass. Two giants cannot stop the world.

Outcome B. A large number of small regions vote yes, say 125 of them. They pass Step 2. But small regions carry small weights. If together they hold less than 65% of the weight, the law fails at Step 3. A crowd of small regions cannot impose its will either.

Why the square root matters here too

The research found a trap in the first version of the rule. It counted population raw, person by person.

Under raw counting, a law needs 65% of humanity behind it. So any group holding more than 35% can block it. India and China together hold 35.2% of the world's people. Under raw counting, those two regions alone could block any world law. That is a hidden veto.

Under square-root counting, the picture changes.

What it takesRaw populationSquare-root weighting
Fewest regions that can block a law221
Fewest regions that can pass a law120120
Fewest regions that could meet the population test on their own1663
Regions needed to block by count alone9898

So under the agreed rule, blocking a law takes at least 21 regions. Passing one takes at least 120 of 217. Blocking by headcount alone takes 98 regions, more than 45% of them. No small group can impose its will, and no small group can stop the world.

A closer look: how the voting rules were tested

The voting tests were not opinions. They were calculations on real population figures for 2024, for all 217 regions and 8,117,484,624 people.

The research also tried other thresholds, using raw population.

Thresholds (regions / population)Fewest regions to passFewest regions to block by populationRegions to block by count
55% / 65% (chosen)120298
55% / 60%120498
60% / 65%131287

Lowering the population threshold to 60% only raised the blocking group from two regions to four. Raising the regions threshold to 60% made laws harder to pass. Neither change removed the hidden veto. The square-root count did.

The same tests show how the square root spreads power out. Counted raw, just 16 regions could meet the 65% population test on their own. Counted by square root, it takes 63.

One limit applies to all of this. The tests use population numbers. They do not model how real regions would vote, form alliances or trade favours.

Changing the constitution

Ordinary laws need the double majority. Changing the constitution needs more:

  • two-thirds of the Assembly;
  • a double majority of 65% of regions representing 65% of the population in the Council;
  • and a world referendum.

In numbers, two-thirds of 1,000 is at least 667 members. And 65% of 217 regions is at least 142.

Some things can never be changed. The guaranteed rights of Article 6 cannot be reduced by any amendment. Neither can Article 9, which says no law, decision, sanction or emergency may reduce those rights.

Power divided, power rotated

Power is split into six parts. No person or body may hold two of them:

  • making laws;
  • carrying them out;
  • allocating resources;
  • measuring needs;
  • auditing;
  • judging.

The executive, the Coordination Council, has seven to fifteen members from different regions. The Assembly elects them for a single five-year term. The chair rotates every year, and no region may hold it twice in a row.

The Court of One Earth protects rights, settles disputes between regions, and reviews laws and allocations against the constitution. Its judges serve one 12-year term. They come from all continents. Every person may bring a case.

No one may hold One Earth office for more than 10 years in total. Every office-holder declares their assets publicly each year. They are under the same money cap as everyone else.

Chapter 14 explains why each of these limits exists, and what the evidence says about power and its abuse.

One Earth owns the arms

War is abolished. No region may keep armed forces or weapons of war, or make them. All arms belong to One Earth, in a single Peace Force.

The Force enforces the Court's rulings, protects people whose rights are under attack, and helps in disasters. Whoever controls the only armed force could dominate everyone. So it can be sent only with both a Court ruling and the double majority of regions. It is never used against peaceful protest, elections or a region's democratic institutions.

Regions keep civilian police for everyday crime. Chapter 12 explains the size of the Force, and Chapter 14 its controls.

Does sharing by need actually work?

UNIRA's central economic claim is that directing new resources by need closes the world's gaps faster than a market world. Chapter 13 tests this in full. Chapter 14 tests what happens when the allocator makes mistakes, or when resources are stolen.

The short version fits in one table.

Share of the shortfall closedEnergy, year 15Health workers, year 15Homes, year 30
Reformed market world−14% (the gap grows)−4%−21%
Very cooperative market world14%4%10%
UNIRA, typical failures, no safeguards36%24%19%
UNIRA, strong safeguards52%34%40%
UNIRA, perfect allocation60%38%50%

Even with typical failures and no safeguards, sharing by need beats the most cooperative market world. Strong safeguards keep most of its advantage. The decisive danger is leakage: resources that never reach the people they were meant for.

Joining by choice

Nobody is forced in. A region joins by referendum, in three stages: observer, associate, then full member. A region may also leave by referendum, with a negotiated settlement.

Would regions want to join? The research compared each region's material situation after 30 years, inside UNIRA or in a reformed market world. Compared with that reformed market world, 146 regions, home to 86% of humanity, gain. In the 33 richest, average energy use per person is 6–12% lower. That is a regional average, borne by the heaviest users: nobody becomes poor, and 44% of their people are better off even at the lowest income. (This 86% is a share of people. It is not the same as the 86% of today's consumption that a base worker can buy, in Chapter 6.)

A union of only the regions that gain would still work, at about half speed. Chapter 15 sets out who gains, what history teaches, and the rules for getting there.

Questions a fair reader might ask

"Isn't a world government a tyranny waiting to happen?" It could be, which is why the draft divides power six ways. Terms are single and short. Officials are under the money cap. The only armed force needs both a court and the regions to move. Regional governments cannot be dissolved except by the Court for a grave breach of rights. None of this proves the design is safe. It shows that the danger was designed against from the start.

"Why should India accept 4.4% of the seats, when direct proportion would give it 16.5%?" This is a real cost for India's people, and the book should not hide it. Under the square-root rule, a very large region gets far fewer seats per person than a small one. The constitution's reason is that the rule aims to give every citizen equal voting power in a union of regions. India still holds more seats than any other region, and an equal vote in the Council. Whether its people would accept the trade is a political question.

"Can't the small regions gang up on the big ones?" The double majority stops that. Small regions can reach 120 votes in the Council. But they must also carry 65% of the square-root weight, and small regions carry small weights.

"What if my region keeps losing votes?" Some protections do not depend on any vote. No law, allocation decision, sanction or emergency may reduce the guaranteed rights of Article 6. And a region may leave by referendum.

"Won't everything become the same everywhere?" The design says no. Religion, language, education, local planning, holidays and daily life stay with the regions. One Earth decides only six shared things.

"Can one world institution really know what 8 billion people need?" Not perfectly. The research calls this the knowledge problem and lists it among the tests that could prove UNIRA wrong. The answer it proposes has three parts: measure needs often, publish everything, and leave daily choices to the regions. Whether that is enough has not been shown.

What the evidence says

  • Regions keep religion, culture, language, education, local planning, holidays, daily life and their own borders. One Earth decides only six shared matters.
  • About 117.8 million people were forcibly displaced at the end of 2025, all for causes UNIRA aims to remove. Disasters displaced 45.8 million in 2024, 99% of them weather-related, and peace does not remove those.
  • Economic migration falls as incomes converge, but it does not vanish. While poorer regions catch up, emigration may first rise.
  • Under square-root weighting, India and China hold about 4.4% of Assembly seats each, against 16–17% under direct proportion.
  • With a 55%/65% double majority counted by square root, blocking a law takes at least 21 regions and passing one at least 120 of 217. Counted raw, 2 regions could block.
  • Needs-first allocation closes the world's gaps where a market world, with the same resources, lets them grow. Strong safeguards keep most of that advantage.

What we still don't know

  • No world federation has ever existed. The voting rules were tested on population numbers, not on real political behaviour.
  • The exact allocation formula has not been written.
  • How One Earth's planning of large flows and the regions' own knowledge would share information is an open design question.
  • Whether regional border control and the promise to protect people in emergencies can both hold in a real crisis.
  • The 2–4% share of people living abroad, and the return rates after peace, are assumptions.
  • Whether rich regions would choose peace, security and time over 6–12% of their energy use is a political question no model can answer.

---

  • Voting tests on 2024 populations (217 regions, 8,117,484,624 people; seat shares; alternative thresholds; 16 and 63 regions to meet the population test alone; 98 and 87 regions to block by count): v75/outputs/voting.json. Chapter 6 — The U: money that serves people

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.4, V6.7, V7.2–V7.5) and the draft Constitution of One Earth, and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

A woman in Marseille buys a kilo of tomatoes. It costs 1 U. It cost 1 U last year, and it will cost 1 U in ten years and in a hundred. She does not check the price, because it never changes.

She pays with her card. The payment goes straight to the cooperative that grew the tomatoes, with an invoice attached. Later that day the cooperative pays its workers' salaries. At the end of the year, whatever it does not need goes to the region.

This scene is imagined. The price of tomatoes is the author's own example, not a measured price. But the rules behind the scene are UNIRA's.

This chapter explains how the U works. It covers what people earn, what things cost, what happens to savings, and why the whole system stays stable. It also shows where the research found a serious flaw in the first design, and how the flaw was fixed.

What the U is

The U is the single currency of One Earth. The draft constitution describes it in a few lines.

It is digital, with no physical cash. Every commercial transaction carries a verifiable invoice through a global transaction network. Personal gifts need no invoice.

A digital-only currency raises fair worries. What about privacy? What about people without a phone, or places without a signal? The constitution answers each one. The system must guarantee:

  • privacy;
  • access without a smartphone;
  • offline operation;
  • independent oversight;
  • the right to appeal.

Why digital at all? One reason is honesty. In the research on leakage, direct digital payments cut losses in public programmes in India by 19–24% (Chapter 14). A currency that leaves a trail is harder to steal.

What people earn

Every adult who works earns a salary in U.

  • The base salary is 5,000 U a month.
  • Salaries rise with experience, knowledge and the complexity of the work: 1.2 times the base, 1.3 times, and so on.
  • No salary may exceed 5 times the base, 25,000 U a month.

The limit applies to everyone. That includes the owners and managers of enterprises. Their profit counts as salary, within the same limit.

So pay can differ by up to five times. But the basics of life do not depend on pay. Resources are shared between regions by need. Health care and education are free. A home is guaranteed to anyone without a salary. Pay decides how comfortable a life is, not whether it is decent.

People who do not earn a salary

The rules protect everyone who cannot work for pay.

  • An adult unable to work for health reasons receives 5,000 U a month.
  • A retired person receives an income for life, set by need. It covers food, daily life and one holiday a year. It is lower if they own their home. Couples receive an amount suited to two.
  • Each child brings 1,000 U a month. The limit is that adult income plus child allowances may not exceed 15,000 U.
  • Health care and education are free, for everyone.

Illness, disability, caring for others, study and retirement all exempt a person from the duty to work. They keep their income and their rights.

Five households: a worked example

Rules are easier to grasp in a real budget. The five households below are imagined. Every amount follows from the rules and the research.

The retiree amounts are the research model's own proposal: 2,500 U a month for a single person, 1.5 times that for a couple, and 500 U more for tenants.

Household (imagined)Adult incomeChild allowanceTotal per month
Sam, single, base salary5,000—5,000 U
Ana and Joe, two children; Ana at base, Joe at 1.3×5,000 + 6,500 = 11,5002 × 1,000 = 2,00013,500 U
Lea and Tom, two children; both at 1.4×7,000 + 7,000 = 14,000limited to 1,00015,000 U
Rosa, unable to work, one child5,000 (guarantee)1,0006,000 U
Mia and Raj, retired couple, renting2,500 × 1.5 = 3,750—3,750 + 500 = 4,250 U

Three details are worth noticing.

The child limit. Ana and Joe earn 11,500 U. With two children, their total is 13,500 U, below the limit. So they receive the full allowance. Lea and Tom earn 14,000 U. Two full allowances would take them to 16,000 U, above the limit. So they receive 1,000 U, which brings them to exactly 15,000 U.

The disability guarantee. Rosa's 5,000 U counts as adult income for the child limit. But the constitution says the guarantee itself is never reduced by that limit.

Retirement. Mia and Raj receive less than a working couple. Their income is set by need, not by past pay. If they owned their home, they would receive 3,750 U, because they would pay no rent.

Where the money goes

Now follow Ana and Joe's month. They have no home yet, so they save the most the rules allow, half their salaries.

Ana and Joe, one monthU
Salaries11,500
Child allowances2,000
Saved (50% of salaries)5,750
Left to spend7,750

Is 7,750 U a month enough? The research gives one anchor. A worker on the base salary who saves half spends 2,500 U a month. That buys about 86% of what an average French person consumes today.

Ana and Joe spend a little over three times that amount, for a family of four. Their health care and their children's schooling cost nothing. Saving half is not forever, either. It is meant to last mainly until the first home is paid. After that, a much lower rate is enough.

What things cost

Prices of essentials are fixed, permanently. They are not set by bargaining, or by supply and demand. They are set by the human work and resources each good really takes.

A closer look: how the fixed price was found

The research built a model of the whole France region. It contains every flow of U: salaries, guarantees, retiree incomes, child allowances, spending, saving and the purchase of homes. Every U has a sender and a receiver, and 11 accounting checks confirm that nothing is lost.

The model measures goods in hours of embodied work. That means all the work behind a product, including the supply chain. France's households use goods that embody about 16.4 billion hours of work a year.

The fixed price was set once. It was chosen so that household spending buys exactly the goods available: no shortage, and no overproduction. The answer was about 146 U per hour of embodied work. It never changes afterwards.

Two numbers follow from it.

  • A base worker spending 2,500 U a month buys goods embodying about 205 hours of work a year. An average French person today consumes about 238. That is the 86%.
  • A worker at the average multiplier buys about 1.6 times what an average French person consumes today.

What about the tomatoes? At 146 U an hour, 1 U stands for about 25 seconds of embodied work. That is simple arithmetic, not a measured price for tomatoes. It shows the idea: a price says how much human time a thing takes.

Who earns what in the model

The model needed salary multipliers to work. It used illustrative values by occupation group, from 1.1 to 3.5 times the base. The average came to about 1.9.

These values are placeholders. The research stresses that the real multipliers are for people to decide democratically.

Following the U around the circuit

Where does each U go after it is spent? The model divides the economy into four parts:

  • households, who earn, spend and save;
  • enterprises, which produce goods and services;
  • the regional fund, which pays public-service wages and guarantees;
  • One Earth, which issues U and holds deposits.

Here is one year in the France region, in the first version of the rules.

France region, one yearBillion U
Salaries, all workers3,822
Disability guarantees69
Retiree incomes387
Child allowances64
Household income4,342
Saved (50% of adult income)1,945
Spent on goods and services2,396
Spent on new and renovated homes335
Saved but never spent1,610

The model had about 33.5 million workers. That is 88% of adults, following UNIRA's rule that everyone able to work does work.

Follow the circuit once. Households spend at enterprises. Enterprises pay salaries and keep what they need for production, maintenance and investment. Their surplus goes to the region. The region pays teachers, nurses, guarantees, pensions and child allowances. Whatever the region does not need goes to the One Earth Fund.

In a single year, everything balances. The research found UNIRA's money design coherent as a system of accounts.

A closer look: the regional fund

The regional fund is where public services and guarantees are paid. In the model's first year, it paid about 1,108 billion U in public-service wages. Everyone working in public services is paid from here. It also paid about 520 billion U to households as guarantees, retiree incomes and child allowances.

Enterprises paid about 2,714 billion U in salaries. Their surplus after production, maintenance and investment was small, about 18 billion U. That makes sense. In UNIRA, enterprises do not exist to make a profit. They exist to produce what is needed.

So where does the region's money come from? In this first version, it did not balance on its own. The region needed One Earth to finance the gap, a gap exactly equal to the money households saved but never spent. That was the first clue that something in the design was wrong.

A closer look: how credit works

Zero-interest credit sounds like a gift. In the accounts, it is simpler than that.

When a household borrows to buy a home, new U is created, with a matching debt. Each month, the household repays from salary. Each repayment cancels part of that U again. When the loan is paid, the U it created is gone.

No interest is charged, so the household repays exactly what it borrowed. A 300,000 U home costs 300,000 U, whether bought with savings or with credit. Nobody earns interest from a family's need for a roof.

The design rule adds one more line: credit is repaid directly from salary. That keeps the system simple. It also explains why credit cannot help people without a salary. For them, the rules guarantee public housing instead (Chapter 7).

Sam's month

Go back to Sam, the single worker on the base salary. Sam has no home yet and wants one.

Sam earns 5,000 U. Saving the maximum, Sam puts 2,500 U aside and spends 2,500 U. That spending buys about 86% of what an average French person consumes today. Sam's doctor's visits cost nothing. An evening class costs nothing either.

What does "modest but comfortable" mean? The design rule gives the author's own picture: food, one holiday a year, and new clothes twice a year. Someone who wants more can spend more, and simply saves less.

Saving at the maximum, Sam could buy a 100,000 U home in about 3.3 years. With zero-interest credit, Sam can move in sooner and repay from salary.

Sam is imagined. The amounts are the rules'.

But look at the last line of the table.

What enterprises do with the U

If nobody competes for profit, what is an enterprise for? The draft constitution gives three short answers.

Enterprises are free to organise and innovate. There is no commercial competition for profit. But ideas and methods may be compared openly. A cooperative that finds a better way to grow tomatoes can show it, and others can copy it.

Enterprises pay for what production needs first. That means materials, wages, maintenance and investment in new equipment.

Their surplus goes to the region, and the region's surplus to the One Earth Fund.

The owners and managers of an enterprise are paid a salary, like everyone else. It sits on the same scale, with the same ceiling of 25,000 U a month. Nobody becomes very rich by owning a firm, because there is no profit to own.

This also changes what work is for. Chapter 2 showed how many hours today go into advertising, speculative finance and similar activities. In an economy without commercial competition, part of that work is no longer needed. Chapter 3 showed how those hours help pay for a 30-hour week.

The trap of too much money

In the first design, households saved half their income every year. Of the 1,945 billion U saved each year, only about 335 billion U could become new homes. Builders, materials and land are limited. The other 1,610 billion U sat in accounts, unspent.

That happens every year, and it adds up. After 10 years, the unspent money equals about 7 years of the region's total spending. After 30 years, it equals about 21 years.

At fixed prices, that pile is dangerous. Each U is a claim on goods. If people ever tried to spend it, shelves would empty. This is what happened in economies that froze prices without balancing goods and money: queues, shortages and black markets. Chapter 4 tells that story.

Could people just save less?

The research tested that too. At a saving rate of about 14%, the region balances, with no pile of money. But then a single base earner needs 36 years to buy a 300,000 U home. That is no better than today.

So the research looked for another answer.

The money cap

UNIRA's answer is the money cap. Each person may hold money up to a limit. The limit is set every year by a published coefficient. Its purpose is to keep people working to produce what is needed.

Money above the cap goes to the One Earth Fund. So do large inheritances above the cap.

Homes are not money, and they are never taken.

A closer look: the 60-year simulation

How do you test a rule for a whole lifetime? The research built a simulation of 6,000 adults. Their ages follow France's real population in 2024. Each person:

  • works, and saves between 0% and 50% of their income;
  • buys a home with savings and zero-interest credit, when builders can supply one;
  • retires;
  • dies, and passes on an inheritance, capped like everything else.

The simulation ran for 60 years, three times with different random draws. The results below are the averages.

It compared three rules.

After 60 yearsNo capCap above a home's priceLow cap with zero-interest credit
Cap, in years of base salary—5.0about 1.4
Unspent money, in months of consumption (year 10 / 30 / 60)33 / 83 / 15225 / 35 / 3823 / 12 / 12
Adults at the cap, year 60—28%57%
Flow to the One Earth Fund, share of the region's consumptionabout 0%20%23%

Without a cap, unspent money grows without limit. By year 60 it equals 152 months of consumption, nearly 13 years. And it is still rising.

A cap higher than the price of a home does not solve it. Money still piles up, more slowly.

A low cap, combined with zero-interest credit for homes, works. Unspent money settles at 12 months of consumption, and stays there.

What a low cap means for a person

The working cap is about 1.4 years of base salary. A year of base salary is 60,000 U. So the cap is roughly 84,000 U per person.

That is less than the price of a home. How can anyone buy a home, then? With zero-interest credit. People do not need to save the whole price first. They move in, and repay from their salary. Chapter 7 shows how this works for a family.

Two effects at once

The cap does two jobs.

It keeps money and goods in balance, so fixed prices can last.

It funds sharing. About a fifth of the region's consumption value flows to the One Earth Fund each year. The Fund pays for new energy systems, homes and infrastructure in every region. It pays for the training of health workers and teachers. It covers guarantees wherever a region's own means fall short. It holds the catastrophe reserves.

Everything the Fund receives and spends is published in the Public Allocation Register. Money above the cap returns to people, as resources.

An imagined case

Imagine a nurse called Ines, in her fifties. Her home is paid off. Her children have left. She earns above the base salary and spends modestly, so her savings grow.

One year her balance reaches the cap. From then on, what she does not spend flows to the Fund. Somewhere, it helps pay for a training school for nurses.

Does Ines stop working? The research's answer is careful. Her salary still pays for everything she buys, so she still has a reason to work. What she loses is the point of saving beyond the cap. She might spend more instead. Or she might choose to work fewer hours, if her work allows it.

Ines is imagined. The research has no evidence on how real people would behave at a cap. That is one of the biggest unknowns in this chapter.

Rules that keep prices fixed for ever

The design rule says prices of essentials should not change "even after 1,000 years". That can hold only if a few other rules hold too.

Average pay may rise only as fast as productivity. Experience raises pay. If average pay crept up by 0.5% a year without more goods, demand would exceed supply by 13% after 30 years. At 1% a year, the gap would be 28%. So Article 14 links average pay to productivity.

Productivity gains become free time, not cheaper goods. The research also tested the opposite drift. If productivity rose 0.5% a year while pay stayed fixed, goods would go unsold: demand would fall 14% short of supply after 30 years. Under "no overproduction", the gain must become shorter hours.

The lifecycle simulation measured how much shorter.

Productivity growthWorking week after 30 yearsAfter 60 years
1% a yearabout 23 hoursabout 17 hours
2% a yearabout 17 hoursabout 9 hours

Fixed prices are kept, and nothing is overproduced. Machines and software become a source of free time, not of unemployment.

Energy, travel and data are always paid per unit, never at a flat rate. Chapter 9 explains why.

Each region keeps studied reserves of essentials, used and replaced before they spoil. A bad harvest or a disaster should never force prices up or empty the shelves.

Who gains

Does all this leave ordinary people better or worse off? In rich regions, living standards are already high. The research compared today's income of each tenth of the population with what the lowest UNIRA salary buys.

RegionPeople better off even at the lowest UNIRA salaryGain for the poorest tenth
France50%+177%
Germany50%+153%
Italy50%+244%
Spain50%+258%
Poland40%+146%
EU as a whole50%+177%

That is before counting free health care, education and guaranteed housing. It is also before counting everyone who earns above the base salary. Chapter 15 shows the full picture, tenth by tenth.

Debts and the change itself

Today, according to UNCTAD, about 3.3 billion people live in countries that spend more on interest than on health or education. Under UNIRA, no region carries public debt. Debts between member regions are cancelled. Bonds held by savers and pension funds count as their own protected savings.

How does a region move onto the U? Not overnight at a political rate. That was the mistake of 1990, when East Germany's output fell to about half its 1989 level within months (Chapter 4). Instead:

  • wages move directly onto the UNIRA scale at full membership;
  • savings convert so that they buy the same basket of essentials as before;
  • the money cap phases in over 10 years, applied to wealth held on the day of the joining vote;
  • mortgages become zero-interest credit.

Chapter 15 sets out these transition rules in full.

Questions a fair reader might ask

"Without changing prices, how does anyone know what people want?" This is a real difficulty. In a market, prices rise when something is scarce. In UNIRA, the burden of balance moves to physical planning: measured needs, reserves, and incomes matched to the goods available. The research lists "coordination without prices" as a scientific challenge still to be tested.

"How is the price of a new product set?" The research admits that new products need a price-setting rule. The principle is clear: the price follows the work and resources the product embodies. The detailed rule is not yet written.

"Isn't the money cap just confiscation?" It changes property rights deeply, and the research says so. But three limits apply. Homes are never taken. The cap phases in over ten years. And money above the cap is not destroyed. It returns as homes, energy and care, published line by line.

"With a cap, why would anyone work hard?" Salaries are still spent, so work still pays. What a cap removes is the reason to save beyond it. In the simulation, 57% of adults reached the cap. The research warns that this could weaken the motive to save. If people respond by spending more, demand rises, and the yearly coefficient must take that into account.

"Doesn't a 5× pay range bring back inequality?" Pay differs, but rights do not. Food, a home, health care, education and an income are guaranteed to everyone. And the gap between the highest and lowest pay is capped at five times.

"If saving causes so much trouble, why allow it at all?" Because people need it. Saving lets a family put money aside for a home, a project or a rainy day. The design rule keeps savings and protects them. What the research showed is that savings need a ceiling, not a ban. Below the cap, they are free and safe.

"Won't a digital currency let someone watch everything I buy?" The risk is real, and the constitution names it. Privacy is one of five guarantees the payment system must give, with independent oversight and a right to appeal. Personal gifts need no invoice at all. The Public Allocation Register publishes amounts and places, never personal data. How well these promises hold in practice is untested.

"What if I have no phone, or the network fails?" The system must work without a smartphone and must work offline. Both are written into the constitution, not left to a later choice.

"What stops prices creeping up, as they do today?" Two rules. Prices of essentials are fixed by the constitution. And average pay may rise only as fast as productivity, so there is no extra money chasing the same goods.

What the evidence says

  • UNIRA's money rules balance as a system of accounts. A single fixed price, about 146 U per hour of embodied work, matches spending to the goods available.
  • A base worker saving half their income buys about 86% of what an average French person consumes today. At the average multiplier, about 1.6 times.
  • If everyone saves half their income for life, unspent money piles up: 21 years of consumption after 30 years in the first model, 152 months after 60 in the lifecycle simulation.
  • A low money cap, about 1.4 years of base salary, with zero-interest credit for homes, holds unspent money at about 12 months of consumption. About 20–23% of a rich region's consumption value then flows to the One Earth Fund each year.
  • Average pay must track productivity. A drift of 0.5% a year opens a 13% gap after 30 years.
  • Half the people of France, Germany, Italy and Spain are better off even at the lowest UNIRA salary. The poorest tenth gains 146–258%.

What we still don't know

  • How people really behave under a money cap. No such economy has ever been observed. If people spend instead of saving, demand rises and the coefficient must respond.
  • How the yearly coefficient that sets the cap should be calculated. The constitution leaves this open.
  • How prices are set for new products, and how preferences are read without market prices.
  • The money cap is legally and politically hard. Some wealthy people may try to move money out before a vote.
  • The salary multipliers and the share of people unable to work are illustrative values in the model.
  • The model covers one region, France, one year at a time. Money flows between regions are not yet modelled.
  • A home built today may embody about 3,600 hours of work, roughly 525,000 U at the fixed price. That is just above the 500,000 U ceiling (Chapter 7).

---
