  • Income deciles (Eurostat 2024): v74/outputs/distribution.json.

Chapter 7 — A home for every family

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.4–V7.2 and the draft constitution) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Imagine a family of five in a city in India. They live in one room. At night the mattresses cover the whole floor. The eldest child does homework on the doorstep, because there is no table and no quiet. Their parents have work, and the home is not falling down. It is simply too small for five lives.

Now imagine a young couple in a French town. They both work. They have saved for years and still cannot buy a flat near their jobs. Each month, a large part of their pay goes on rent. They keep postponing a child until they have "a proper place".

Both scenes are imagined composites, not reports about real people. They stand for two different housing problems.

In the first, the world lacks space where people live. In the second, homes exist, but buying one takes much of a working life. This chapter shows how UNIRA tries to solve both. It uses clear rules for buying a home, and a building programme for the parts of the world where homes are missing.

The housing rules

UNIRA's housing rules are short. Here they are in plain words.

  • A home costs between 100,000 and 500,000 U. The price is fixed, and depends on the size and location of the home. The same rule covers buying, building and renovating.
  • Each adult may own at most two homes, and each family at most two in total. Homes are comfortable, but they are not castles or very large.
  • People save to buy. Saving is personal, up to a maximum of 50% of salary.
  • Credit is free. Zero-interest credit is available, repaid directly from salary.
  • An inherited extra home returns to the pool. A home inherited beyond the heir's first home goes back to the regional pool, or the heir chooses which one to keep.
  • Public housing is guaranteed for every adult without a salary.
  • New homes are built with low-carbon materials where possible.
  • Homes are never seized.

These rules are written into the draft constitution, in Article 18 ("Homes"). Article 6 adds a right. Every person has the right to "a safe, comfortable home of a decent size for their household, never seized and never subject to forced relocation".

How long does it take to buy a home?

To understand the rules, it helps to put them in years of work. Chapter 6 introduced the salary scale. The base salary is 5,000 U a month. Pay rises with experience, knowledge and the complexity of the work, up to five times the base. In the research's model of France, the average multiplier is about 1.9 times the base, an illustrative value.

The table shows how many years of saving it takes to buy a home, if a household saves half its income.

HouseholdHome at 100,000 UHome at 300,000 UHome at 500,000 U
One earner, base salary3.3 years10.0 years16.7 years
Two earners, base salary1.7 years5.0 years8.3 years
One earner at 1.5×2.2 years6.7 years11.1 years
Two earners at 1.5×1.1 years3.3 years5.6 years
Two earners, average salary (1.9×)0.9 years2.6 years4.4 years
One earner at 3×1.1 years3.3 years5.6 years
One earner at the top, 5×0.7 years2.0 years3.3 years

Two things stand out. Even the lowest-paid single worker can buy a modest home in a little over three years. And a couple on average pay can buy a mid-priced home in under three years.

Compare this with the French couple in the opening scene. Under UNIRA, credit carries no interest. The price of a home is fixed by its size and location. It does not rise because others are bidding for it.

A worked example: buying with free credit

Saving first is only one way to buy. Zero-interest credit is the other. Here is how it works for an imagined couple, Nadia and Paul.

Both earn the base salary, 5,000 U a month each. Together they earn 10,000 U. They choose a home at 300,000 U.

Option 1: save first. They save half their income, 5,000 U a month, or 60,000 U a year. After 5 years they have 300,000 U, and they buy. That matches the table.

There is a catch. Chapter 6 showed that personal money is capped, at about 1.4 years of base salary. That is about 84,000 U each, 168,000 U for the two of them. They cannot hold 300,000 U in savings. So in practice, they cannot save the whole price first.

Option 2: borrow at zero interest. They borrow 300,000 U and move in at once. Each month, 5,000 U is repaid directly from their salaries. After 60 months, 5 years, the loan is paid.

Nadia and PaulSave firstBorrow at zero interest
When they move inafter 5 yearsnow
Monthly amount set aside5,000 U5,000 U
Years until the home is theirs55
Total paid for the home300,000 U300,000 U

The cost is the same. The difference is five years of living in their own home instead of waiting.

Once the loan is repaid, they no longer need to set half their pay aside. The research's recommended path is to save up to 50% mostly until the first home is paid, then at a much lower rate.

Nadia and Paul are imagined. The figures follow from the rules.

A closer look: what is a home worth in hours?

A price in U is a price in human work. Chapter 6 showed that UNIRA's fixed price is about 146 U per hour of embodied work, in the model of France.

So a 300,000 U home pays for about 2,050 hours of work. The full price range, 100,000 to 500,000 U, pays for roughly 680 to 3,400 hours.

Is that enough to build a comfortable home? The research found a warning sign. In France, construction embodies about 17.9 hours of work for every €1,000 of output, counting the whole supply chain. So a home that costs about €200,000 to build today embodies about 3,600 hours of work.

At UNIRA's fixed price, that is about 525,000 U. It is just above the 500,000 U ceiling.

The euro figure is only a technical way to estimate hours. But the conclusion is in hours, and it matters. Either comfortable homes must be built with fewer hours, or the price range must be checked against the real work they contain. If homes were sold below their labour content, someone else would be paying the difference.

Why saving half forever cannot work

The research found a problem with the original idea, and it is worth telling honestly.

If every household saved half its income every year, for its whole life, the money would pile up. In the France model, households would save about 1,945 billion U a year. But only about 335 billion U of that can become new or renovated homes each year. Builders, materials and land are limited. About 1,610 billion U a year would be left unspent.

In the model, builders could deliver about 1.1 million new or renovated homes a year. That figure assumes half of all construction investment goes to housing, an assumption the research marks as illustrative.

At fixed prices, the unspent money is a claim on goods that do not exist. After 30 years, it would equal 21 years of total consumption. If people tried to spend it, shops would empty.

Could people simply save less? At about 14% saving, the money balances. But then a single base earner needs 36 years to buy a 300,000 U home. That is no better than today.

The answer combines three ideas:

  1. Save up to 50%, mostly until the first home is paid. Saving is personal and voluntary. Fifty per cent is the maximum, for someone who lives modestly but comfortably.
  2. Use zero-interest credit to bring the purchase forward. People move in early and repay from salary.
  3. Cap personal money. Money above a yearly cap goes to the One Earth Fund, which spends it on homes, energy and care where they are needed.

Chapter 6 tells the money side of this story. Here is the housing side.

What happens to homes over 60 years

The research simulated 6,000 adults over 60 years. They work, save, buy homes, retire, die and pass on what they own. The simulation compared a world with no money cap and a world with a low cap and free credit.

Housing resultNo money capLow cap with zero-interest credit
Home ownership after 30 years92%91%
Households with a second home, after 60 years60%38%

With the low cap and free credit, home ownership barely changes. Almost everyone who wants a home still gets one. But far fewer homes sit as second homes while others wait.

Where second homes come from

The simulation found something surprising. Most second homes do not come from buying. They come from inheritance.

A person who already owns a home inherits their parents' home. Now they own two. Meanwhile, renters in the same region wait 15 to 19 years for a home.

The research first tried an obvious rule: block second-home purchases while anyone is still waiting. It changed nothing. Purchases were not the main cause.

So UNIRA added a rule, known in the research as P1. A home inherited beyond the heir's first home returns to the regional pool. Or the heir chooses which home to keep. The two-home maximum stays.

An imagined case

Imagine Omar, who owns a flat in the city where he works. His mother dies and leaves him her house in the village where he grew up.

Under P1, Omar now holds two homes, but only one is his "first". He has a choice. He can keep the flat and let the village house return to the regional pool. Or he can keep the village house and let the flat go.

Either way, he keeps a home, and the one he prefers. The other is offered to a family that has none.

This is not confiscation of a home someone lives in. It is a rule about what an heir can add on top of the home they already have.

Who is still renting?

After 30 years in the simulation, most people own their home. Those still renting are mostly adults without a salary: people not employed, or unable to work. Credit cannot help them, because credit is repaid from salary.

That is why the research proposed a second rule, P3: guaranteed public housing for adults without a salary. It is now in Article 18 of the draft constitution. A person who cannot work still has a safe home, as Article 6 promises.

Picture an imagined man, Kofi, who cannot work after an illness. He receives the guaranteed 5,000 U a month. He cannot take on credit repaid from a salary he does not have. Under the rules, he does not need to. A public home is guaranteed to him.

Homes are never seized

When people hear "money cap", many worry about their home. The draft constitution answers clearly.

Article 17 says: "Homes are not money and are never taken." The transition rules add that, when a region joins, homes are kept. A household that owns more than two homes may sell the extra ones to the regional pool within 10 years. Existing mortgages become zero-interest U credit.

Savings above the cap go to the One Earth Fund. Homes do not. The difference is deliberate. A home is where a family lives, not a store of wealth.

Who gets the house by the sea?

Here is a question the rules do not yet answer.

Prices are fixed by size and location. But some places are more wanted than others: a flat near the centre, a house by the sea. At a fixed price, more people may want such a home than there are homes.

In a market, the price would rise until fewer people wanted it. In UNIRA, the price does not move. So something else must decide.

The research lists this as a task still open: how to allocate desirable locations at fixed prices, when demand exceeds supply. The rule that first homes come before second homes helps. But a full answer, fair and simple, has not been designed. It is one of the honest gaps in this chapter.

The world's housing gap

So far this chapter has looked at rules. But rules cannot build a house. In much of the world, the problem is not the price of homes. It is that there is not enough space.

Across the world, people have on average about 26 square metres of home per person. That average hides huge differences.

RegionFloor area per person
Ethiopiaabout 7 m²
Indiaabout 10.5 m²
World averageabout 26 m²
Franceabout 45 m²
Germany (2025)about 49.5 m²
United Statesabout 55 m²

Within the European Union alone, the range runs from about 19 to 64 m² per person.

A closer look: how much space is "decent"?

Researchers who study decent living give two common answers.

One defines a decent home as 30 m² for a household, plus 10 m² for each person above three. That works out at about 10 m² per person. Another includes shared space and arrives at about 15 m² per person.

UNIRA uses 15 m² as decent and about 27 m² as comfortable. The comfortable figure comes from a scenario with households of about three people. Researchers stress that such thresholds "should be decided at a local level". So the choice of 27 m² is a design decision, not a law of nature.

There is one more caution. No official statistics give floor area per person for every country. The figures above come from a regional model. They are estimates, not counts.

How big is the gap?

StandardWorld floor area ÷ needPeople in regions below itNew floor area to build
Decent, 15 m² per person1.703.5 billionabout 13 billion m²
Comfortable, 27 m² per person0.945.0 billionabout 65 billion m²

At the decent level, the world as a whole has enough floor area. But it is in the wrong places. At the comfortable level, the world is slightly short overall. Most of the gap is in South Asia and Africa.

Homes cannot be moved. And UNIRA forbids forced relocation. So the homes must be built where people already live. Rich regions are already above the comfortable level. The two-home rule, the rule to use existing buildings first, and the inheritance rule keep them from growing further.

Building a generation of homes

How fast can 65 billion square metres be built? At today's building capacity, the research found that only about 40% of the gap closes after 30 years. That is not good enough.

How much does the world build today? The research found that the world's housing stock grew by about 3.4 billion m² in 2024. That is a net figure, after old homes are removed, so gross building is higher. China alone built about 7.3 billion m² of new floor area in 2024, counting all kinds of buildings.

The research then tested faster plans. Building capacity in regions of need grows each year, by 5% or 10%. But it never grows faster than the highest rate ever sustained.

That record belongs to China in 2011: about 1,468 m² per 1,000 people in one year. South Korea may have come close in 1990. It built about 750,000 dwellings that year, about 17.5 per 1,000 people. How close depends on the size of each dwelling, which the research had to assume.

The world's population is also growing. Adding the backlog of 65 billion m² to the new homes needed for population growth, about 109 billion m² must be built or upgraded in total.

Plan over 30 yearsEveryone at 27 m² byGap closed by year 15Embodied CO₂Peak construction workers
Today's capacitynot within 30 years15%29 Gt87 million
Capacity +5% a yearyear 2739%43 Gt253 million
Capacity +10% a yearyear 2770%43 Gt253 million
+10%, stock-firstyear 2783%41 Gt265 million
+10%, stock-first, low-carbonyear 2783%16 Gt265 million
+5%, stock-first, low-carbonyear 2754%15 Gt236 million

The headline is simple. Comfortable homes for everyone within about 27 years are possible, if building capacity in regions of need grows by 5–10% a year. At its peak, that means about 6–7 billion m² a year, at China's past rate per person.

Stock-first: use what exists

Article 11 of the draft constitution sets a principle. Before producing new, maintain, repair, renovate, reuse and convert what exists. For housing, that means extending and upgrading existing homes first.

The evidence suggests this could meet about 40% of the need, using about 40% of the materials of new building. Reusing an existing building emits 50–75% less than building new.

India shows why. In 2012, its urban housing shortage was about 18.8 million homes.

India's urban shortage, 2012HomesShare
Overcrowding15.0 million80%
Obsolete or unsafe houses3.3 million17%
Homeless0.5 millionabout 3%

Overcrowded homes can often be extended where they stand. In Chile, upgrading a slum in place cost about two-thirds as much per household as moving its residents. And only upgrading in place produced lasting gains in housing quality.

Stock-first speeds the early years: 83% of the gap closed by year 15, against 70% without it. But it cannot replace building. Most of the need is missing space.

What about empty homes?

Many people ask: why build, when so many homes stand empty? The research checked.

PlaceEmpty homes
China, urban (2017)about 65 million, 21.4%
European Union (2021)46.6 million of 236 million, 19.7%, including second homes
India, urban (2011)11.1 million, about 12%
Japan (2023)9.0 million, 13.8%

These are large numbers. But they are mostly in the wrong places. The largest stocks are in China, Japan and the European Union, and a home cannot be shipped.

India's empty urban homes are in a region of need. Yet counted as floor area, they would meet only about 0.5% of the world's need. Using them is worthwhile. It is not a solution.

Low-carbon: the material matters

Building 109 billion m² with conventional concrete would release about 41–43 gigatonnes of CO₂. The remaining carbon budget for 1.5 °C is about 130 gigatonnes, counted from 2025. Concrete homes alone would use about a third of it.

Low-carbon homes change that. They use bamboo, timber, vaults or low-cement structures. A conventional reinforced-concrete home releases about 400 kg of CO₂ per square metre as it is built. A low-carbon home releases about 150 kg. Across the whole programme, emissions fall to about 15–16 gigatonnes, roughly 12% of the 1.5 °C budget.

The savings come from the frame, the floors and the roof, not the walls. In Kenya, cement makes up 83–91% of the footprint of earth blocks. Changing blocks alone saves little. In Ecuador and Colombia, a two-storey social house with a bamboo structure released 27% of the emissions of a concrete frame. It also cost 36% less.

Chapter 11 places these figures beside the world's other limits on materials and land.

People: the real limit

The binding limit is not money or materials. It is people.

How many hours does a square metre take? The evidence gives a range.

SourceHours of work per m²
Industrial precast housing, Finlandabout 13
Estimate for China, 2010about 33
India's rural housing programmeup to about 64

India's rural programme takes about 201 person-days per house, with a minimum size of 25 m². The research uses 48 hours per m², the midpoint of 33 and 64.

At its peak, the programme needs about 240 to 265 million construction workers. The world has about 294 million today. Only about 116 million are in South Asia and Africa, where most homes are needed: about 91.6 million in Southern Asia and 24.8 million in Africa.

So the builders in those regions must roughly double. This is where UNIRA's other rules matter. Everyone able to work, works (Chapter 3). Soldiers leaving disbanded armies are offered work and training, including in construction (Chapter 12). Even so, it is a very large workforce programme.

Questions a fair reader might ask

"Why only two homes?" Because the space and the builders are limited, and many families still have none. Two homes allow a family home and, for example, a place near work or family. More than that would keep homes empty while others wait.

"Isn't the inheritance rule just taking people's property?" The heir always keeps a home, and chooses which one. What returns to the pool is a home beyond the one they already have. Homes people live in are never seized.

"What if I lose my job and cannot repay my credit?" The rules guarantee an income to anyone unable to work, and public housing to anyone without a salary. Homes are never seized. How repayments work during a long gap in pay is not yet set out in detail.

"Wouldn't concrete be cheaper for poor regions?" Not always. In Ecuador and Colombia, the bamboo-framed house cost 36% less than the concrete one. And at the scale of 109 billion m², concrete would use about a third of the 1.5 °C carbon budget.

"Will my home in a rich region shrink?" No. Rich regions are already above the comfortable level, and homes are never taken. The rules stop them growing further, through the two-home limit, stock-first and the inheritance rule. New building goes mostly where space is missing.

"Can the poorest regions really double their builders?" It has never been done at this scale. The research says plainly that it is a very large workforce programme. It depends on universal work, training, and former soldiers choosing construction.

What the evidence says

  • UNIRA homes cost 100,000–500,000 U. A single base earner saving half their pay buys a 100,000 U home in about 3.3 years; a couple on average pay buys a 300,000 U home in about 2.6 years.
  • Zero-interest credit lets families move in at once, for the same total price.
  • Saving half for life does not work at fixed prices. A low money cap with zero-interest credit keeps money stable, with home ownership around 91%.
  • Second homes come mainly from inheritance. With the low cap and free credit, they fall from 60% to 38% of households in the simulation.
  • People still renting are mostly adults without a salary. Guaranteed public housing is their answer.
  • Comfortable homes for everyone (27 m² each) mean building or upgrading about 109 billion m². It can be done in about 27 years if building capacity grows 5–10% a year.
  • Empty homes cover only about 0.5% of the need in regions of need. Upgrading can meet about 40%.
  • Low-carbon construction cuts emissions from about 41–43 Gt to 15–16 Gt of CO₂.
  • The real limit is workers: about 240–265 million at the peak, against 294 million in the world today.

What we still don't know

  • Is the price range right? A home embodying about 3,600 hours of work would be worth about 525,000 U at UNIRA's fixed price, above the 500,000 U ceiling.
  • Who gets the most wanted locations at fixed prices, when more people want them than there are homes.
  • How much work does a self-built home take? No verified figure was found per square metre.
  • How much carbon do African and Indian concrete homes really emit? No whole-house assessment was found.
  • The share of homes that can be upgraded, and the materials it saves, are partly judgement.
  • Building capacity was treated for the world as a whole, not region by region. No source splits construction by income group.
  • The floor-area figures are model estimates. No official country statistics exist.
  • The money simulation covers one region, France, with illustrative salary multipliers. How people behave when they reach the money cap is not known.

---

Chapter 8 — Health, education and care for all

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.3, V6.5–V6.8, Working Paper 2 and the draft constitution) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

It is early morning at a small health post in a rural district. The queue started before dawn. A mother holds a feverish child. An old man waits with a bandaged foot. A young woman is pregnant for the first time and has walked a long way.

There is one nurse. They know most of the people in the queue by name. They will see as many as they can before dark, and some will be told to come back tomorrow.

Far away, in another country, a soldier sits in a classroom. Their unit has been dissolved. They are not learning a new weapon. They are learning how to take blood pressure, clean a wound and spot the signs of a difficult birth. In a few years, they could be the second nurse at a health post like this one.

Both scenes are imagined. They are composites, not reports. But they hold the two ideas of this chapter.

The first is that the world's deepest shortage is not food or energy. It is trained people who care for others. The second is that the shortage can be closed, slowly, if the world decides to train them, and if it stops spending people on war.

A promise written into the rules

UNIRA makes a simple promise. Health care and education are free, for everyone, throughout life.

The promise first appeared in the design rules. Rule 15, on retirees, ends with a short sentence: health care and education are free. The draft constitution turns it into a right. Article 6 lists what every person is guaranteed. Among them is "health care and education, free of charge, throughout life".

The constitution then protects that promise in three ways:

  • Article 8 says rights in Article 6 are never withdrawn as a punishment.
  • Article 9 says no law, allocation decision, sanction or emergency may reduce them.
  • Article 7 says study, like illness and caring for others, exempts a person from the duty to work, without loss of income or rights.

And the money is there. Article 17a lists what the One Earth Fund pays for in every region. It includes "the training of health workers and teachers". It also covers the guarantees of Article 6 wherever a region's own means fall short.

A promise on paper is easy. Keeping it is harder. To keep it, the world needs people: nurses, doctors, midwives, care workers and teachers. This chapter asks whether there can be enough of them, and how long it would take.

The world's real shortage

Chapter 1 showed that the world already produces enough food for everyone, and more than enough energy for a decent life. Health care is different.

Today the world has about 47.4 million doctors, nurses and midwives. That is about 58 per 10,000 people.

The World Health Organization sets a minimum: about 44.5 health workers per 10,000 people. On a world average, we are above it. The world has 1.32 times what the minimum requires.

But that average misleads. Nurses and doctors cannot be shipped like grain. And UNIRA forbids forced relocation, so nobody can be ordered to move to another region. What matters is where health workers live. Today about 4.2 billion people live in regions below the WHO minimum. In those regions, about 7.7 million workers are missing.

UNIRA aims higher than the minimum. Its goal is a comfortable level of care, like the European Union's: about 131 health workers per 10,000 people. Measured against that goal, the world has less than half of what it needs: 0.45 times. About 7.3 billion people live in regions below it.

Level of careHealth workers per 10,000World supply ÷ needPeople in regions below
Today's world average58——
Decent (WHO minimum)44.51.324.2 billion
Comfortable (EU level)1310.457.3 billion

To bring everyone to the EU level, the world needs about 59 million more health workers. That more than doubles today's total. By 2050, the world's population will reach about 9.66 billion, so total need rises to about 107 million.

This is the longest lead time in the whole UNIRA plan. Food can be moved within a year. A nurse takes years to train.

A closer look: what these numbers measure

It helps to know exactly what is being counted.

Who counts. The world figure of 47.4 million covers doctors, nurses and midwives. It comes from the World Health Organization's health workforce data. Care workers are not included in it.

What the WHO minimum means. The figure of 44.5 is tied to 12 health indicators of the Sustainable Development Goals, at a low level of achievement. It is a shortage line, not a comfortable level. EU and OECD densities are about three times higher.

Where 131 comes from. The EU level was worked out by the research from Eurostat counts of doctors, nurses and midwives. It is a reference point, not a scientific finding about what people need. Choosing it is a design decision UNIRA makes openly.

Why averages hide the problem. The research counts people in regions below each level. A region's average can hide large gaps inside it, between city and countryside, or rich and poor. So the true number of people without good care is probably larger.

For rich regions: build up, don't take away

Under strict equal shares, a rich region would simply hand over part of what it has. For food and energy, Chapter 1 showed that rich regions would still stay comfortable.

For health, equal shares would be a disaster. France has about 134 health workers per 10,000 people. Under equal shares it would fall to about 59. That is still above the WHO minimum, but far below what French people have today.

So the UNIRA research draws a clear conclusion. For health, "equal share" must mean building up the South, not taking from the North. Nobody's hospital is emptied. New health workers are trained in the regions that need them. Article 10 of the draft constitution says it directly. The new capacity directed to regions by need includes "health workers trained in-region".

First, a problem at home

Before looking at the world, look at one rich country on day one.

Chapter 3 showed that France can keep today's output under a 30-hour week, if almost everyone who can work does work. Care is the exception. In hospitals and care homes, much of the work is being there. Rested staff make fewer mistakes, but they cannot be in two places at once. In care, only 0–15% of lost hours come back through higher productivity.

So a 30-hour week means more people. France's health and social work sector would need about 16.9% more staff. In round numbers:

OccupationWorking todayExtra needed for 30 hoursTraining time
Nursesabout 608,000about 100,0003 years
Doctors (upper bound)about 266,000about 45,00010 years for a GP
Care workersabout 860,000about 145,000about 11 months

No training system can respond within the first year. That is true whatever happens to the rest of the economy.

A closer look: how long training really takes

The research checked each training time against official French sources.

  • Nurses. Training lasts 3 years, six semesters. From September 2026 it includes 4,620 hours, up from 4,200.
  • General practitioners. Six years of medical school, then four years of specialist training. The fourth year was added for doctors in training who started in 2023. That makes 10 years in all.
  • Care assistants. The course is 1,540 hours: 770 hours of theory and 770 hours of placement. That is about 44 weeks, or 11 months.

The numbers are tight even today. France trains about 24,800 nurses a year, while about 20,300 leave each year. Job vacancies in health and social work already run at 2.9–3.8%.

What if France doubled its training intake? The nurse gap would close in about 8 years. The doctor gap would close in about 16.

What can be done meanwhile?

The research lists options. Choosing among them is a democratic decision, not a modelling choice:

  • care-assistant courses for the many new workers who enter work under UNIRA;
  • the voluntary return of qualified staff who have left;
  • help from other regions, under the One Earth framework;
  • redesigning tasks, so that nurses and doctors do what only they can do.

If the 30-hour rule applied to care from the very first day, without these measures, the right to care would be at risk. UNIRA has to plan for this before it starts.

An imagined ward

Picture a hospital ward in a French town, in the first year of UNIRA. Before, its nurses worked long shifts. Now each works 30 hours a week. Someone has to cover the missing hours.

Three new care assistants have just finished their eleven-month course. They were not working a year ago. They take on washing, meals and turning patients. That frees the nurses for medicines, wounds and watching for danger signs. Two retired nurses have come back part-time. The ward is still short. But it is less short than it would have been.

This ward is imagined. It shows how the options above might combine. It does not show that they will be enough.

How long until everyone has good care?

Now return to the world. The UNIRA research built a model of world training. It asks a simple question. How many years until every region reaches the EU level of 131 health workers per 10,000 people?

The answer depends above all on one thing: how many new health workers the world's schools can train each year. Nobody knows that number exactly. So the model tries three levels: low, central and high. Each scenario adds one more step.

ScenarioYears until everyone has EU-level care (low / central / high)
Training as todaynever / never / never
Training grows 7% a year for 15 years (up to 3× today)never / 37 / 27
+ a quarter of the world's soldiers volunteer for health trainingnever / 36 / 25
+ healthier lives (central prevention, −3% need)never / 34 / 24
Ambitious: training grows 10% a year, half of soldiers volunteer, strong prevention42 / 23 / 18

Three lessons stand out.

First, if the world keeps training as it does today, it never gets there. Population grows, and today's training only keeps pace.

Second, with an ambitious UNIRA programme, it takes about 18 to 42 years, with a central estimate of 23 years. That is roughly one generation. A child born at the start of the programme would be an adult by the time every region has good care.

Third, the biggest lever is not disarmament and not prevention. It is training capacity itself: the schools and the teachers who train health workers. That is the bottleneck.

A closer look: how the training model works

The model's low, central and high cases differ in one key number. It is how many new workers the world can train each year, as a share of today's workforce: 3%, 4.5% or 6%.

Why not simply count the world's graduates? Because nobody publishes that total for doctors. The World Health Organization reports about 25.3 new nursing graduates per 100,000 people a year. But it gives no world total for medical graduates. So the research estimated it from OECD ratios, and labels it an estimate.

The model then lets training grow, by 7% or 10% a year. It caps growth at three times today's capacity. Each year, new workers go first to the regions furthest below 131 per 10,000.

The research also says what would prove this plan wrong. If world training capacity cannot grow beyond about 1.5 times today's, EU-level care for everyone stays out of reach for more than 50 years.

Soldiers into carers

Rule 19 says there will be no more wars. Article 5 of the draft constitution goes further. Existing armed forces are dissolved during the transition. Their members are offered work, training and full income, "including in health, construction and civil protection".

How many people is that? The world's armed forces number about 27.4 million. That is about 0.79% of the world's labour force.

The model asks what happens if some of them choose health training. In one scenario, a quarter volunteer. In the ambitious one, half do. Nobody is forced: Article 7 guarantees free choice of occupation.

The effect is real, and it comes early. After 10 years, with soldier volunteers, the world reaches about 71 health workers per 10,000 people. Without them, it reaches about 64.

But the effect is limited. Former soldiers still need training places, and training places are the bottleneck. In the table above, a quarter of soldiers volunteering saves only a year or two. Disarmament speeds the start; it does not replace the schools.

Those not drawn to health work are not lost either. They become available for every other kind of useful work: construction, civil protection, climate action. Chapter 12 returns to what disarmament frees.

Healthier lives: real, but smaller than hoped

Rule 19 also makes a hopeful claim. In UNIRA, better lives, less stress and no fear of the future should prevent much illness. If people are healthier, fewer health workers would be needed.

The research tested this claim against the evidence. Here is what it found.

The potential is large

About 41–48% of the world's disease burden is linked to risks that can, in principle, be changed. The largest single risks in 2021 were polluted air, at 8.0% of the burden, high blood pressure at 7.8%, and smoking at 5.7%.

Some of UNIRA's levers have clear effects

UNIRA leverWhat the evidence showsStrength
30-hour weekVery long weeks (55 hours or more) expose about 488 million people. In 2016 they caused about 745,000 deaths from heart disease and stroke.Strong; a 30-hour week removes the exposure
Secure incomeUnconditional cash transfers in poorer countries cut illness by about a fifth (risk ratio 0.79) within 1–2 yearsModerate certainty
Housing for allHousing homeless people cut emergency visits (0.63) and hospital stays (0.76)Weak: 4 trials, all at high risk of bias
No warConflicts killed 139,000–285,000 people a year directly in 2021–2025. In conflict-affected places, 22.1% of people have a mental disorder at any timeStrong for the harm; benefit hard to size
Less job strainOnly 3.4% of heart disease is attributable to job strainSmall

But the net effect on workforce need is small

There are four reasons.

  • The 41–48% assumes risks fall to their theoretical minimum, not to a level any society can plausibly reach. UNIRA touches only part of those risks.
  • More than half of all disease is linked to no known risk at all.
  • Many premature deaths, which dominate preventable disease, use little care. Maternity care, child care, routine care, care for the old and rehabilitation stay the same.
  • People who live longer need care later. One study found that lifetime health spending is highest for people who live healthily, because they live longest. In England, the share of people over 65 with four or more diseases is projected to rise from 9.8% to 17.0% between 2015 and 2035.

There is even a twist. Where needs are unmet, more income can raise the use of care, because people can finally reach a clinic. In the same cash-transfer studies, use of health services rose slightly (risk ratio 1.04).

Timing: some gains come fast, others take decades

  • Heart disease responds quickly. Within 5 years of quitting, heavy smokers already have much lower risk than those who continue.
  • Cash transfers reduced illness within one to two years.
  • Whole populations change slowly. In Finland, deaths from coronary disease fell 80% over 35 years.
  • Cancer takes decades. Stopping smoking at 30 brings the risk of lung cancer by age 75 down to 2%, against 10% for stopping at 60.

A closer look: how the research put it together

The research combined these findings into one estimate. It multiplied four things, in plain words:

  1. the share of disease linked to changeable risks (central 45%);
  2. the share of that UNIRA could actually remove (central 25%, a judgement);
  3. how much less disease reduces the need for health workers (central 0.5, a judgement);
  4. a discount for longer lives that need care later (at 30 years, central 0.6, so only 40% of the gain remains).

A small amount for peace was then added (central 0.5%).

Here is the central case at 30 years, step by step:

  • 45% × 25% × 0.5 ≈ 5.6%;
  • after the longevity discount, 5.6% × 0.4 ≈ 2.25%;
  • adding peace, 2.25% + 0.5% ≈ 2.75%, rounded to about 3%.

The high case, with bolder judgements, gives about 14%. The low case gives about zero, or slightly less.

HorizonNet cut in health-worker need (low / central / high)
5 years0% / 2% / 6%
15 years0% / 3% / 10%
30 yearsslightly negative / 3% / 14%

In the central case, that is about 3 million workers, against a gap of 59 million. Confidence is low.

So the honest verdict is this. Healthier lives help, but they do not replace training. The real benefit of healthier lives is not fewer nurses. It is more healthy years for everyone. That is worth reporting on its own, and it is one of UNIRA's best arguments.

Why UNIRA does better than a market world

Could a reformed market economy, with the same people and the same technology, close the health gap just as well? The research tested this with matched resources. Chapter 13 tells the full story.

By year 15, share of the health shortfall closedResult
Reformed market−4% (the gap grows)
Ambitious cooperative market4%
UNIRA38%

The reason is not that UNIRA is cleverer. It trains far more people, invites soldiers to join, invests in prevention, and sends every new health worker to the regions in greatest need. The training programme and training in-region are both needed. Either one alone closes only about 10% of the gap by year 15. On health, allocation alone is not enough. Scale is what matters.

Education: the teachers behind everything

Education is the other half of the promise.

How much schooling is enough?

Researchers who study decent living put a decent education at about 9 years of schooling. A comfortable one is about 12.

Class size shows the gap. Primary schools worldwide have about 25.7 pupils per teacher. High-income countries have about 14.3.

Simple arithmetic shows what closing that gap means. Going from 25.7 to 14.3 pupils per teacher needs about 1.8 times as many teachers for the same number of children. That is before counting population growth, or extra years of schooling.

Teachers are the bottleneck behind the bottleneck

Every nurse, doctor and midwife must be taught by someone. The health model found that schools and teachers set the pace of the whole plan. A world that wants more health workers must first train more teachers of health.

Who pays

The draft constitution pays for it at world level. The One Earth Fund pays for "the training of health workers and teachers", in every region. A poor region does not have to choose between its schools and its clinics.

Learning is rewarded, not punished

Study exempts a person from the duty to work, without loss of income (Article 7). And in UNIRA's salary scale, the multiplier rises with experience, knowledge and education (rule 12). Nobody has to give up an income to go back to school.

Keeping school money honest

There is also evidence on making sure school money arrives. In Uganda in the early 1990s, most school grants never reached the schools. Schools received only about 13% of the grants. After newspapers published how much each school should receive, the share captured along the way fell from about 80% to under 20% (Reinikka & Svensson). That finding is one reason the draft constitution requires every allocation to be published in an open register (Chapter 14).

Some questions remain unmeasured. How many more teachers does the world need, and where? Those are open tasks for the next round of research.

Questions a fair reader might ask

"Won't free health care be overused?" In the research, the need for health workers is set by a service standard, 131 per 10,000 people. It is not set by how much people ask for. The evidence does show that more income can raise use of care where needs were unmet. That is a sign of care reaching people, not of waste.

"Why not recruit nurses from abroad, as rich countries do today?" Because it moves the shortage instead of closing it. The research's rule for health is to train new workers in the regions that need them. The WHO itself traces its projected shortfall of 11.1 million health workers by 2030 to training, distribution and migration.

"Isn't 23 years far too slow?" It is slow, and the research says so. But training as today never closes the gap at all. A generation is the honest timescale for training tens of millions of people.

"Is the EU level of care really necessary everywhere?" It is a reference, not a law of nature. The research chose it as "comfortable", and says plainly that choosing a level is a design decision. A lower target would be reached sooner.

"What about care for old people, which is growing everywhere?" This is one of the hardest parts of the plan. Longer lives mean more years with several illnesses. In England, the share of older people with four or more diseases is projected almost to double in twenty years. Care for the old does not shrink with healthier lives. In France alone, the 30-hour week needs about 145,000 more care workers. The world model counts doctors, nurses and midwives, not care workers, so the full need for care at world scale is not yet measured.

"Will soldiers really want to become nurses?" Nobody knows. Article 7 forbids forcing anyone into a job. The model tests a quarter and a half of soldiers volunteering. Both are assumptions.

What it asks of us

None of this happens by itself. Keeping the promise of free care and education asks four things of the world:

  • a health-worker training programme lasting 20–30 years, in every region;
  • training places, schools and teachers expanded faster than ever before;
  • soldiers offered a real path into care, by choice;
  • for France and other rich regions, a plan for the first years, when the 30-hour week needs more carers than exist.

It also asks patience. Food can be shared in the first year. Good health care for everyone takes a generation.

What the evidence says

  • The world has 47.4 million doctors, nurses and midwives, about 58 per 10,000 people. 4.2 billion people live in regions below the WHO minimum of 44.5.
  • Bringing everyone to the EU level of 131 per 10,000 needs about 59 million more health workers, more than doubling the world total.
  • Under a 30-hour week, France would need about 100,000 nurses and 145,000 care workers in year 1. Training takes about 3 years for a nurse, 10 years for a GP and 11 months for a care assistant.
  • Training as today never closes the world gap. An ambitious UNIRA programme closes it in 18–42 years, central 23.
  • Schools and teachers are the bottleneck. Disarmament helps early (71 vs 64 per 10,000 by year 10). Healthier lives cut need by only about 3% (central) to 14% (high) after 30 years.
  • With matched resources, UNIRA closes 38% of the health shortfall by year 15; a reformed market economy lets it grow.
  • Bringing primary classes to high-income levels means about 1.8 times as many teachers per pupil.

What we still don't know

  • How fast the world's training capacity can really grow. The research's caps, volunteer shares and lead times are assumptions or judgements.
  • The world's total number of medical graduates. No source was found; it is estimated from OECD ratios.
  • How many soldiers would actually choose health work.
  • The net effect of healthier lives on care needs. The direction is clear; the size is not, and longer lives add care later.
  • France's reserve of qualified staff who have stopped practising. It is not reported.
  • How many teachers the world needs, and where. This has not yet been measured.

---

Chapter 9 — Security, not scarcity

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.4, V6.6, V7.1 and the draft constitution) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

It is a Saturday in March 2020. A shopper pushes a trolley down a supermarket aisle. The shelf where the toilet roll should be is bare. So is the pasta shelf. Next to them, another shopper loads a trolley with twelve packets of rice.

Neither of them is greedy. They have heard that shops may close. They do not know when food will come back. So they buy what they can, while they can. And by buying, they help empty the shelves for everyone else.

This scene is an imagined composite, but the shelves were real. In Ireland, in the week to 15 March 2020, sales of toilet roll were 181% higher than a year earlier. Pasta was up 199%, and rice 123%.

Now imagine the same shopper in a world where they know, without doubt, that food will always be there. The price will not jump. The shop will be stocked next week, and next year. Would they still fill the trolley?

That is the question of this chapter. It sounds like a question about shopping. In fact it reaches much further. It decides how UNIRA prices energy, how large its emergency stores must be, and whether a comfortable life for everyone fits inside the climate limits of Part III.

The idea: people stop hoarding when they feel safe

UNIRA's twentieth design rule is a hypothesis, not a promise. It says that when people know they can always afford and access food and essentials, they stop over-buying, hoarding and over-eating.

The author of UNIRA offers an everyday analogy. When mobile calls became unlimited for a fixed price, nobody started calling 24 hours a day.

It is a good idea. It is also exactly the kind of idea that should be tested, not assumed. So the research looked for evidence, good by good. It asked one plain question each time: when access becomes secure, cheap or unlimited, does use go up, go down, or level off?

What "security" means here

Before the evidence, it helps to be clear about what UNIRA promises. Security in this chapter has three parts.

  • Guaranteed rights. Article 6 of the draft constitution gives every person food and clean water, "sufficient and secure, with access guaranteed". It also guarantees "energy, transport and digital access sufficient for a comfortable life".
  • Fixed prices. Prices of essentials never change (Article 13). A family need not fear that bread will double next month.
  • Real reserves. Every region keeps "studied, rotated reserves against catastrophes" (Article 11).

The hypothesis is that these three together change behaviour. The rest of the chapter tests that, piece by piece.

What happened to phone calls

Start with the analogy itself. In France, the average mobile customer spoke for about 2 hours 15 minutes a month in early 2011. Unlimited plans then spread. By early 2013, the figure was 2 hours 51 minutes. The French telecoms regulator, ARCEP, attributed the rise to unlimited plans.

Then the rise slowed almost to a stop. In early 2015 it was 3 hours 3 minutes. In 2023 it was about 3 hours 28 minutes a month, or about 7 minutes a day.

So the analogy holds, but with a twist. Use did not fall when calls became unlimited. It rose first, by about 36% within three years, and then levelled off. Why? Because there are only so many hours in a day, and only so many people to call. Calls have a natural ceiling.

A closer look: how the phone figures were measured

The regulator's figures are minutes of calls per customer per month. They are an average over all customers, heavy and light users together.

Two details matter. First, the growth rate. In early 2013, minutes were 15.5% higher than a year before. In early 2015, they were only 1.6% higher. That is the levelling-off, seen directly.

Second, the basis changed. The 2023 figure counts people who hold a mobile device. The figures for 2011 to 2015 count customers. So the 2023 number is not perfectly comparable with the earlier ones. The research notes this openly. It does not change the shape of the story: a step up, then a plateau.

Paying for peace of mind

There is a second lesson from phones. A study by Lambrecht and Skiera (2006) found that up to 46.4% of customers chose a flat rate that cost them more than paying per use would have.

Why would anyone do that? Not to use as much as possible. Their own usage was low enough that paying per minute would have been cheaper. They paid extra for the comfort of never worrying about the bill. In the researchers' terms, they bought insurance.

That is the heart of rule 20. People value security for its own sake. Given it, many do not try to squeeze out every last unit.

Where security helps

The evidence supports the idea for several kinds of goods.

Hoarding

The Irish figures show that scarcity, or the fear of it, drives stockpiling. In that same week of March 2020, total grocery sales were 23% higher than a year earlier. The goods people feared would run out rose far more: toilet roll 181%, pasta 199%, rice 123%.

A closer look. These are "year-on-year" figures. The research compares sales in one week of 2020 with the same week of 2019. That removes normal seasonal swings. What remains is the panic. Notice the pattern: the whole basket rose by about a quarter, but storable staples nearly tripled. People were not eating three times more pasta. They were filling cupboards.

Remove the fear, and much of the panic goes with it. Chapter 4 showed the other side of this coin: in shortage economies, the fear of empty shelves fed itself.

Alcohol and tobacco

A common fear about giving people money is that they will spend it on drink and cigarettes. The evidence says otherwise. Evans and Popova gathered 44 estimates from 19 studies of cash transfers. The results were almost all zero or negative. More secure income did not mean more spending on these goods.

A closer look. Each "estimate" measures how spending on alcohol or tobacco changed when households received cash. A zero result means no measurable change. A negative result means spending on these goods went down. The review also quotes an average effect size, but that exact figure was not confirmed in the text the research fetched. So the chapter relies only on the direction: almost all zero or negative.

Calories

In India, Deaton and Dreze (2009) found that people ate fewer calories as their real incomes rose. A review by Nettle and colleagues (2017) of 125 studies found food insecurity linked to obesity only in one group: adult women in high-income countries. Bodies, like days, have limits.

But security is not the whole story for food. In a controlled trial by Hall (2019), people offered ultra-processed food freely ate about 508 more kilocalories a day.

A closer look. A randomised controlled trial is the strongest kind of test. It changes one thing and holds the rest steady. Here the thing that changed was the kind of food on offer, not income or worry. So the extra calories point to the food itself. The lesson is clear: what kind of food is on offer matters more than whether people feel secure. Chapter 11 returns to diet.

What the review chose not to use

Good research also says what it left out. The review deliberately did not use the well-known buffet studies by Wansink, because of retractions. It also flagged figures it had seen only in search results, not in the original texts. Those were never used as central values. This care matters. A hopeful hypothesis attracts hopeful evidence, and that is exactly when checking should be strictest.

Where it helps only a little: food waste

Does security reduce food waste at home? The evidence here is weak.

Household food waste is similar across rich and poor countries. In the UN's 2021 food waste index, it was about 74 kg per person per year. The spread between country income groups was only about 7 kg.

The pattern is not a straight line. Waste rises at middle incomes, partly from buying extra "to avoid running out". That is an inverted U: waste rises from low to middle incomes, then falls again.

One more data point cuts the other way. During the 2020 lockdown in the United Kingdom, people reported wasting 34% less. That happened at a time of insecurity, not security. It is self-reported, so people may have remembered their habits kindly. But it is a warning against assuming that comfort, by itself, cuts waste.

The research tested what security might do. It tried a cut in household waste of 0%, 5% (the central value) and 15%. That moves the world's comfortable-diet supply only from 1.105 to 1.11–1.12 times the need.

So Chapter 1's lesson stands. Halving waste is the main lever, and it needs deliberate effort: better storage, better planning, no overproduction. Security alone will not do it. Chapter 11 looks at how much waste there is, and where.

Where security fails: goods with no ceiling

Now the other side. Some goods have no natural limit. Nobody's stomach fills up with data, travel or warm rooms. For these goods, making use cheap or unlimited pushes use up, and keeps pushing.

GoodWhat the evidence showsNatural ceiling?
Mobile callsRose by about a third, then levelled offYes
Toilet roll, pasta, ricePanic buying up to +181–199% when scarcity is fearedYes, once fear is gone
Alcohol and tobaccoNo increase from cash transfers (44 estimates, 19 studies)Yes
Food caloriesFell as incomes rose in IndiaYes, but food type matters
Household food wasteOnly a weak link to securityWeak
Mobile data0.5 GB → 14 GB a month, 2015–2023; unlimited plans +20% useNo
HeatingFlat-rate heat uses up to about 30% more than metered heatNo
Public transportFree transit: +14% (Tallinn) to +70% (Dunkirk) tripsNo

Data

Data is the clearest case. Mobile data use rose from about 529 megabytes a month in 2015 to 14 gigabytes in 2023. That is about 2 GB more every year. In high-income countries, users on unlimited plans used about 20% more than users on large capped plans. There is no sign of a ceiling.

Why the difference from calls? A call needs a person at the other end and your own time. Data needs neither, so nothing in the day stops it growing.

Heat

For home energy, two findings stand out. A review by Sorrell (2009) puts the direct rebound under 30%. And homes billed a flat rate for heat use "often" up to about 30% more than homes that pay for what they use. A flat bill removes the reason to turn the radiator down when you open the window.

Travel

Travel tells the same story. When public transport became free in Tallinn, its use rose by 14%. In Dunkirk, weekday trips rose by 70%, and weekend trips by 140%.

Free transport may be good for a city. But it does not show that people use less when access is secure. It shows the opposite.

Even then, free transport did not empty the roads. In Luxembourg, the third free-transit case in the review, the share of people commuting by car went only from 72.3% to 69.3% between 2011 and 2021. Use went up, but most car commuters stayed in their cars.

The pattern: two kinds of goods

Put the evidence side by side and a simple pattern appears.

Some goods have a natural ceiling. There are only so many hours to talk, so much food a body can hold, so much space in a cupboard. Once people feel safe, use levels off. Sometimes it rises first, as calls did. But it stops.

Other goods have no ceiling. Data, travel, heated space and status can always grow. More is always possible, and often tempting. For these goods, cheap or unlimited access raises use without limit.

The author's hypothesis is therefore half right. That is not a failure. It is what testing is for. It turns a hopeful belief into a precise rule.

Why this matters for the planet

This may sound like a detail. It is not. It decides whether UNIRA stays inside the climate limits described in Part III.

Chapter 10 shows that a comfortable life for everyone can fit a 1.5 °C pathway. In 2050, it needs about 386 exajoules of final energy a year. That is about 6–13% less than the world uses today: 409 EJ in the UNIRA model, 442 EJ according to the International Energy Agency. That works only if rich regions use much less energy than they do now.

Now add the rebound. When energy becomes cheaper or easier, people use more of it.

A simple illustration. The numbers here are round and imagined, to show the arithmetic. Suppose a rich region insulates its homes and fits heat pumps. On paper, that saves 100 units of energy. With a 20% rebound, people keep rooms a little warmer and leave lights on a little longer. They use back 20 units. The real saving is 80.

Now scale that up. The research tested rebound on the savings of every rich region. Suppose people use back 20–30% of those savings. Then peak world energy rises to 424–436 exajoules. That is above today's 409 in the model and outside the 1.5 °C range.

With only a 10% rebound, peak use would be about 413 exajoules, just above the line. The difference between those numbers is the difference between keeping the climate promise and breaking it.

Rebound on rich-region savingsPeak world final energyInside the 1.5 °C envelope?
None (the plan)at or below 409 EJYes
10%about 413 EJJust outside
20–30%424–436 EJNo

The answer: meter what has no ceiling

So the research proposed a clear rule, now Article 19 of the draft constitution:

> "Energy, travel and data are always metered and paid per unit, never at a flat rate."

This does not mean these goods are scarce or expensive. Article 6 guarantees everyone "energy, transport and digital access sufficient for a comfortable life". Under UNIRA, the price per unit is fixed and never jumps. What is ruled out is the "all you can use" offer.

There is an important difference between a fixed price and a zero price. A fixed price is still a price. Each extra kilowatt-hour or kilometre still costs something, so people still think before they use it. That limits rebound. Free or flat-rate access removes the signal.

The rule therefore has two halves:

  • Goods with a natural ceiling (food, basic goods, calls) can be offered with secure, guaranteed access.
  • Goods without one (energy, travel, data, space) stay metered and priced per unit.

An imagined evening under the rule

Picture a family in a UNIRA region on a winter evening. The scene is invented. Their fridge is full, and they never worry about the next shop. Their phone calls are part of a simple plan. None of that is metered by the minute.

Their heating, their train tickets and their mobile data are different. A small screen shows what the evening has used and what each unit costs. The price is the same as last year, and it will be the same next year. Nobody is short. But when the teenager leaves a window open with the heating on, the screen shows it.

That is all the rule asks. Not less comfort, but a visible cost for goods that would otherwise grow without end.

Reserves against catastrophe

Security does not come from a promise alone. It must be real, or people will stop believing it, and the trolleys will fill again.

UNIRA's thirteenth design rule asks every region to keep fixed, studied catastrophe reserves of goods. Article 11 of the draft constitution writes this down: each region keeps "studied, rotated reserves against catastrophes". Article 17a says the One Earth Fund pays for them.

Three words in that rule do a lot of work.

  • Studied. Reserves are sized from how often shocks have actually happened. The research notes this can be tested against records of how much harvests vary from year to year.
  • Rotated. Stocks are eaten or used and replaced before they spoil. That keeps the principle of no waste.
  • Fixed. Reserves are not an excuse to overproduce. Production equals what people consume, plus replacing the reserves, plus investment. Nothing more.

Article 3a puts the same idea in one line: "We produce what we need, for all regions", planned "from measured needs and population in every region, plus the studied reserves".

How security and reserves work together

Here security and reserves reinforce each other. The research estimates that a credible guarantee avoids about 25–75% of panic-buying spikes, with a central value of 50%.

That estimate has conditions. It needs the reserves themselves. It needs limits on purchases during a shock, so that one trolley cannot empty a shelf. And it needs people to believe the guarantee.

The payoff is practical. Reserves can be sized for real supply shocks, such as a failed harvest. They do not have to cover demand panics on top. In normal times, the effect of security on buying is small, close to nothing. Its value shows up in a crisis.

A closer look. The 25–75% is a model parameter, not a measurement. No region has yet run guaranteed reserves through a pandemic and counted the panic it avoided. The research sets the range wide on purpose, and uses the middle value only as a central case.

Fixed prices: no more price shocks

Under UNIRA, prices of essentials are fixed. Rule 8 says they should not change, "even after 1,000 years". Article 13 of the draft constitution sets them from the human work and resources each good embodies.

The author gives a homely example. One kilogram of tomatoes costs 1 U. It costs 1 U this year, next year, and when today's children are old.

That removes a large source of fear. A family need not worry that bread will double in price next month. A retired person's income, set by need to cover food, daily life and one holiday a year, buys the same basket for life.

The research found one more benefit. Fixed prices end household price shocks such as the energy-price crisis of 2022.

But history gives a warning. The Soviet Union froze the prices of staple goods for decades. The economist Kornai studied the result in Economics of Shortage (1980). The price held, but the goods did not. There were queues, rationing and informal markets. A fixed price on an empty shelf is not security. Chapter 4 tells that story in full.

So in UNIRA, the burden of balance moves to physical supply. Production is planned to needs. Reserves cover shocks. And the money people hold must never be larger than the goods available at the fixed price.

The money cap keeps prices honest

That last condition is where the money cap comes in. Chapter 6 introduced it, and Chapter 7 showed its role in housing. Here is why it matters for security.

At fixed prices, unspent money is a claim on goods. The research simulated 6,000 adults over 60 years in a France-sized region. Without a cap, unspent money grew without limit: 152 months of consumption after 60 years, and still rising. If even part of it were spent at once, the shelves would empty. That is the Soviet trap again.

With a low cap, about 1.4 years of base salary, and zero-interest credit for homes, unspent money settles at about 12 months of consumption. Money above the cap goes to the One Earth Fund, about 20–23% of the region's consumption value each year. The Fund turns it into homes, energy and care where they are needed.

60-year simulation, 6,000 adultsNo capCap of about 1.4 years' base salary
Unspent money, months of consumption152, still rising12, stable
Flow to the One Earth Fundabout 0%about 20–23% a year
Adults at the cap—57%

Pay is guarded too. If average pay drifted up by 0.5% a year with no matching rise in productivity, demand would exceed supply by 13% after 30 years. So Article 14 says that average pay may rise only as fast as productivity.

When productivity rises faster, Article 13 turns the gain into shorter working hours, not higher prices. With productivity growing 1% a year, the simulated week falls from 30 to about 23 hours after 30 years.

Each of these rules has the same aim. People can trust that what their money buys today, it will buy tomorrow. That trust is what lets the shopper leave the rice on the shelf.

Who gains most from stable prices

Security matters most to people who have the least of it today.

The research looked inside rich regions, using official income data for 2024. A worker on the lowest UNIRA salary who saves the maximum, half their pay, can still buy about 86% of what an average French person consumes today. By that measure, half the population of France, Germany, Italy, Spain and the EU as a whole is already better off. The poorest tenth gains 146% to 258%.

Free health care, education and guaranteed housing come on top, and are not counted in those figures. Chapter 15 returns to who gains and who gives something up.

Honest answers to hard questions

A careful reader will have objections. Here are five, with the best answers the evidence allows.

"Metering energy is just rationing with a nicer name." No. Rationing caps how much you may have. Metering only shows the cost of each unit. Article 6 guarantees energy, transport and digital access sufficient for a comfortable life. Chapter 10 shows that a comfortable level of energy for all fits the planet. The meter limits waste and rebound, not comfort.

"Free public transport is good for the climate. Why rule it out?" The evidence says free transport mostly adds trips. In Dunkirk, weekend trips more than doubled. In Luxembourg, the share of car commuters fell only three points in ten years. A fixed price per trip, which never jumps, keeps public transport within reach while still giving each trip a cost. What the rule forbids is the unlimited pass.

"The phone analogy failed, so the whole idea fails." It did not fail. It was refined. Calls rose about a third, then stopped. That is exactly what a natural ceiling looks like. The analogy simply does not carry over to goods without one. Rule 20 now says so.

"Won't people just hoard money instead of goods?" They might try. That is what the money cap is for. Money above the cap goes to the One Earth Fund, and returns as resources. The research is honest that this is untested. It names a clear test that would prove the money rules wrong. If people at the cap spend rather than save, and demand at fixed prices outruns what reserves can absorb, the design fails.

"If food is guaranteed, won't people overeat?" The calorie evidence says no, in general: people in India ate fewer calories as incomes rose. But the Hall trial shows that ultra-processed food changes the picture. Security is not the risk. The kind of food is. That is a question of what is produced, which Chapter 11 takes up.

What the evidence says

  • Security reduces over-consumption for goods with a natural ceiling. Phone calls rose by about a third, then levelled off at about 7 minutes a day. Up to 46% of customers pay extra for a flat rate, for peace of mind.
  • Cash transfers did not raise spending on alcohol or tobacco across 44 estimates from 19 studies.
  • Scarcity drives hoarding: toilet roll +181% in Ireland in March 2020. A credible guarantee could avoid about 25–75% of such spikes, if backed by real reserves and purchase limits during shocks.
  • Security alone does little for food waste. Halving waste remains the main lever for a comfortable diet.
  • For goods without a ceiling, cheap or unlimited access raises use: data 0.5 → 14 GB a month, free transit +14–70% trips, flat-rate heating up to about 30% more.
  • A 20–30% energy rebound would push world energy to 424–436 EJ, outside the 1.5 °C range. That is why energy, travel and data are always metered (Article 19).
  • Fixed prices are stable only if money stays matched to goods. The money cap holds unspent money at about 12 months of consumption, against 152 without it.

What we still don't know

  • No society has yet lived with permanently guaranteed access to essentials. The evidence comes from phones, cash transfers and short crises, not from a whole economy.
  • Some figures in the review were seen only in search results and were not used as central values. They include an average effect size for cash transfers and some car-use figures for Tallinn and Dunkirk.
  • Studies of basic income in Finland and of the Mincome experiment reported no results on how much people bought or stockpiled.
  • The 25–75% avoided panic is a model range, not a measurement.
  • The size of the reserves, and how often they must be rotated, still has to be studied region by region.
  • How people behave once they reach the money cap is unknown. If they spend rather than save, demand at fixed prices rises.
  • A fixed price limits rebound better than a free good, but how well it works over decades is untested. The 10–30% rebound values come from today's economies.
  • The phone figures changed basis in 2023, from customers to device holders, so the long series is not perfectly comparable.

---

  • Evidence and model effects: v71/RESEARCH_NOTE.md; detailed review in v71/evidence/evidence.md (ARCEP mobile voice data and basis change; Lambrecht & Skiera 2006; Ireland grocery data, March 2020; Evans & Popova; Deaton & Dreze 2009; Nettle et al. 2017; Hall 2019; UNEP Food Waste Index 2021; UK lockdown waste; Ericsson mobile data; Sorrell 2009; Tallinn, Dunkirk and Luxembourg transit data; excluded and unverified items). Rows and URLs in v71/evidence/evidence.csv.
  • Comfortable energy level, 386 EJ in 2050, 6–13% below today: v66/RESEARCH_NOTE.md §1; v67/WORKING_PAPER_02.md, section 6.
