Chapter 10 — A comfortable life inside the climate budget

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.5–V7.1) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Picture two evenings in 2050.

In a flat in Lille, a retired teacher turns up the heat pump on a cold night. The flat was insulated twenty years ago. The electricity comes from wind farms in the North Sea and panels on the roofs of the street. A small screen by the door shows what the evening has used, and what each unit costs. The price has not changed in years.

In a village near Kano, in northern Nigeria, a young nurse comes home from a late shift. The lights are on. The fridge is cold, and so is the medicine stored in the clinic down the road. Twenty-five years earlier, the village had power for a few hours a day, when it had power at all. Now a solar field and a local grid run it day and night.

Neither person exists. They are composites, imagined to show one idea. The teacher in Lille uses less energy than their parents did. The nurse near Kano uses far more. Both live comfortably. And the world, taken together, uses less energy than it does today.

This chapter asks whether that picture fits inside the climate budget. The short answer is yes, under strict conditions. The longer answer is what this chapter is about.

How much energy is "comfortable"?

Chapter 1 introduced the idea of a comfortable level of energy. It means a warm or cool home, hot water, cooking, appliances, light, internet, travel, and a fair share of schools, hospitals, farms and factories. It does not mean excess.

UNIRA sets that level at 40 gigajoules (GJ) of final energy per person per year. Final energy is the energy people and machines actually use: electricity at the plug, heat in the radiator, fuel in the engine.

A closer look: where 40 GJ comes from

The number is not a guess. It comes from a body of research on what a good life needs in energy terms. The main source is a 2020 study by Millward-Hopkins and colleagues.

That study built up energy needs from the bottom. It listed what people need, such as a home kept at a healthy temperature, a fridge, cooking, light and transport. It then asked how much energy each would take with the best available technology.

It found that a decent life needs about 15.3 GJ of final energy per person per year. Across countries, the figure ranged from 13 to 18.4 GJ. Climate and the age of the population drive that spread. A cold country needs more heat. The age of a population changes how much school and hospital space it needs.

The same study then added more comfort: larger homes, more travel, more appliances. Its higher scenarios reached 24 GJ and then about 40 GJ per person. UNIRA takes the top of that band.

Another study, by Kikstra and colleagues (2021), shows how much place matters. Its decent-living figure runs from about 9 GJ in South Asia to about 36 GJ in North America.

So 40 GJ sits well above what a decent life needs. It is a choice for comfort, not for bare survival. The research says plainly that choosing the level is a design decision, which UNIRA must make in the open.

A closer look: final and primary energy

One trap catches many readers. Energy statistics come in two kinds.

Primary energy is what goes into the system: coal, gas, oil, wind, sunlight. Final energy is what arrives at the point of use. Some is lost in between.

In the European Union in 2024, final energy was about 0.66 of the energy available at the start. So primary energy was about one and a half times final energy. A system that runs mainly on solar and wind has a much smaller gap, because those sources are counted at their direct output.

Every figure in this chapter is final energy. Comparing 40 GJ of final energy with a figure for primary energy would make UNIRA's target look smaller than it is. Chapter 1 gives the world's primary figure, with the same warning.

The arithmetic for the whole world

By 2050, the United Nations expects about 9.66 billion people on Earth. Give each of them 40 GJ, and the world needs 386 exajoules (EJ) a year. (An exajoule is a billion gigajoules.)

How does that compare with today? Here there are two answers, and it is worth seeing both.

  • According to the International Energy Agency (IEA), the world used 442 EJ of final energy in 2022, about 55 GJ per person. Against that, 386 EJ is about 13% less.
  • UNIRA's own transition model estimates today's world final energy at 409 EJ. Against that, 386 EJ is about 6% less.

So a comfortable life for everyone in 2050 means using about 6–13% less energy than the world uses now, with more than a billion extra people.

Why do the two estimates differ? Counting the world's energy is hard. The IPCC's own figure for 2020 is 419 EJ, with a range of 367 to 458. The research keeps both of its estimates in view, and its conclusions hold with either.

That 386 EJ figure sounds surprising. It is possible because today's energy is shared so unequally. Many people use far more than 40 GJ, and billions use far less.

Where 40 GJ sits among the climate pathways

Climate scientists have built hundreds of pathways that keep warming within limits. Each one says how much energy the world can use in 2050. The table puts UNIRA's level beside them.

World final energy in 2050Total (EJ)Per person (GJ)
Decent living, the bare essentials (Millward-Hopkins 2020)14915.4
Low energy demand scenario (Grubler 2018)24525.4
IEA Net Zero by 2050 (2023 update)34335.2
UNIRA: 40 GJ for everyone38640
IPCC 1.5 °C pathways, median (range)410 (325–540)42.4
IPCC 2 °C pathways, median44846.4
Today (2022)442about 55

UNIRA's level sits above the IEA's net-zero scenario and below the median of the IPCC's 1.5 °C pathways. It is inside the 1.5 °C range. It fits comfortably under 2 °C.

A closer look: what the IPCC range means

The IPCC's 1.5 °C figure is not one forecast. It summarises 97 separate pathways. Each keeps warming near 1.5 °C with at least an even chance, with little or no overshoot.

The middle pathway uses 410 EJ in 2050. The range shown, 325 to 540 EJ, leaves out the most extreme five percent at each end. So UNIRA's 386 EJ is not at the edge of what scientists consider workable. It is a little below the middle.

The low-demand scenario by Grubler and colleagues goes further. At 245 EJ, it is "around 40% lower than today". The IPCC also notes that decent living for all needs only about 150 EJ, or 40% of today's final energy. UNIRA does not aim that low. It aims for comfort, and still fits.

An independent check agrees with UNIRA's own number. Millward-Hopkins and colleagues found that 40 GJ per person gives "just under 400 EJ" for the world. That matches 386 EJ.

So energy itself is not the hard limit. As the research puts it, the binding limits for a comfortable life are carbon timing, diet and materials. The next chapter deals with the last two.

The carbon budget: almost spent

Here is the uncomfortable part.

Warming depends on the total amount of carbon dioxide (CO₂) humanity releases, added up over time. Scientists therefore speak of a carbon budget: how much more CO₂ the world can emit before it passes a given level of warming.

Remaining CO₂ budget from the start of 202550% chance67% chance
Stay below 1.5 °C130 Gt80 Gt
Stay below 1.7 °C490 Gt390 Gt
Stay below 2 °C1,050 Gt870 Gt

Source: Forster et al. 2025. Gt means billion tonnes.

In 2025 the world emitted about 42.2 Gt of CO₂. At that rate, the 1.5 °C budget is gone in about 3 to 4 years.

Human activity had already warmed the planet by about 1.36 °C in 2024. Warming is rising by about 0.27 °C every ten years.

A closer look: a budget that keeps shrinking

Carbon budgets are recalculated every few years. Each new estimate starts from a later date, so the years of emissions in between have already been spent. Watching the numbers fall shows how fast the window is closing.

EstimateCounted from1.5 °C budget, 50% chance
IPCC, 20211 January 2020500 Gt
Lamboll et al., 2023January 2023250 Gt
Forster et al., 2025start of 2025130 Gt
Global Carbon Budget, 2025start of 2026170 Gt, about 4 years of emissions

The estimates do not line up perfectly. They use different methods and starting dates. The Forster paper even gives two starting dates in different places, the start of 2024 in one table caption and the start of 2025 in its text. The research notes this inconsistency rather than hiding it.

The message is the same in every row. For 1.5 °C, only a few years of today's emissions remain.

A sense of scale helps. From 1850 to 2019, humanity emitted about 2,390 Gt of CO₂. The whole remaining 1.5 °C budget is a small fraction of that history.

What this means

This leads to a hard conclusion, and the research states it plainly. No energy level, however low, now avoids at least a temporary overshoot of 1.5 °C. Not UNIRA's, not anyone's. "Staying at 1.5 °C" now means going over it for a while, then pulling CO₂ back out of the air to come down again.

The 2 °C limit is different. It is still open. Suppose emissions fell in a straight line from 2025. The world would reach net zero around 2066 and stay below 2 °C with a two-in-three chance.

What does that mean for one person? Today the average person emits about 5.2 tonnes of CO₂ a year. A 1.5 °C-class world needs emissions near zero, net, by 2050. The research's central value allows about 0.35 tonnes per person gross, with net emissions about zero. Even a 2 °C world allows only about 1.7 tonnes per person.

What a 40 GJ world needs

Fitting a comfortable life into this budget is not automatic. The research names three conditions.

First, about half of all final energy must be electricity. Today most energy is still burned as fuel. The IEA's net-zero scenario has electricity at 53% of final energy in 2050; the IPCC 1.5 °C pathways, 52%. In the IPCC's 2 °C pathways it is 42%. Heat pumps, electric trains, buses and cars, and electric cooking do the same work with less energy.

Second, fossil CO₂ must be near zero by 2050. Coal, oil and gas can no longer be the backbone of the system. In the IPCC's 1.5 °C pathways, CO₂ from energy and industry in 2050 is about 8% of its 2020 level. In the 2 °C pathways it is about 31%.

Third, solar and wind must be built much faster than ever before.

A closer look: how fast must we build?

That third condition deserves a careful look.

In 2025, the world added 692 gigawatts (GW) of renewable capacity, a record. Of that, 510 GW was solar and 159 GW was wind: 669 GW together. The year before, it had added about 582 GW. By the end of 2025, the world had about 5.14 terawatts (TW) of renewable capacity in total. A terawatt is a thousand gigawatts.

How much is needed? The research worked it out in two steps.

  1. The IEA's net-zero scenario adds about 820 GW of solar and 320 GW of wind a year by 2030. That is about 1.14 TW a year.
  2. That scenario uses 343 EJ in 2050. UNIRA uses 386 EJ, so it needs somewhat more. Scaled up, the research arrives at about 1.3 TW a year.

So solar and wind must be added at about 1.1 to 1.3 TW a year by 2030. That is 1.7 to 1.9 times the 2025 record.

The research calls this a "first-order" estimate. It is a scaling of someone else's scenario, not a detailed plan. It tells us the size of the task, not the route.

It is a big step. But it is not a fantasy. At the COP28 climate summit, the world agreed to triple renewable capacity by 2030: from 3,630 GW in 2022 to 11,000 GW. The renewable energy agency IRENA calculates that this needs about 1,122 GW a year, and growth of 16.6% a year. That is close to the pace UNIRA needs. In other words, the world has already signed up to something similar.

Getting there: thirty years of change

A target for 2050 is one thing. The road to it is another.

UNIRA's research modelled a 30-year transition for about 180 world regions. Each year, new clean energy goes first to the regions furthest below 40 GJ. Regions above 40 GJ reduce their use. Population follows the UN's 2024 projection. The table shows four cases.

30-year caseEvery region at 40 GJ byWorld final energy, year 30
Low: solar and wind at the 2025 record pace, training as todaynot within 30 years380 EJ
Central: 1.2 TW a year of clean energyyear 27390 EJ
Central, and no wars (military energy freed)year 27400 EJ
High: 1.8 TW a yearyear 27383 EJ

In the central case, everyone reaches a comfortable energy level by year 27. At no point does the world need more energy than it uses now.

A closer look: reading the four cases

Each row of the table changes one thing: how fast clean energy is built.

In the low case, solar and wind are built at the 2025 record pace and no faster. Health training also stays as it is today. Even after 30 years, some regions are still below 40 GJ. The record pace is simply not enough. That is why the research treats a build rate below the 2025 record as a test that would prove UNIRA's physical claims wrong.

In the central case, the world builds about 1.2 TW a year. Every region reaches 40 GJ by year 27. World energy in year 30 is 390 EJ, below today's level.

The no-wars case keeps the central build rate but frees military energy. Rich regions can then cut more slowly, 3% a year instead of 4%. World energy in year 30 is a little higher, 400 EJ, but still below the model's 409 EJ for today.

In the high case, the world builds 1.8 TW a year. Convergence still comes in year 27. In the model, building faster than the central pace does not bring that date any closer.

The low case ends with the lowest world energy, 380 EJ, yet it fails. Low total energy is not the goal in itself. The goal is enough for everyone, inside the limit.

That last point depends on rich regions. While poorer regions rise, the world total must not grow. The model starts from its own estimate of today's world final energy, 409 EJ, and keeps the total at or below that. To do so, regions above 40 GJ per person must cut their energy use by about 4% a year.

If weapons production and military fuel stop, 3% a year is enough. The world's armed forces account for about 5.5% of global emissions. Chapter 12 returns to that.

A worked example: what 3–4% a year adds up to

Yearly percentages are hard to feel. Here is the simple arithmetic.

A cut of 4% a year, kept up for ten years, leaves about two-thirds of the starting energy use. That is a fall of about a third in a decade. A cut of 3% a year leaves about three-quarters after ten years: a fall of about a quarter.

Think of the imagined teacher in Lille. Over a decade, their flat is insulated, their gas boiler becomes a heat pump, and the bus becomes an electric tram. Much of the cut comes from machines that do the same work with less energy. Some of it comes from choices, and the meter by the door makes those visible.

Why restraint matters

Why is restraint so important? The research tested what happens if new energy goes to regions in need, but rich regions keep their use. World energy then peaks at 453 EJ, outside the climate envelope.

Sharing and restraint must go together. One without the other fails. Sharing without restraint breaks the climate. Restraint without sharing leaves billions without enough.

A closer look: where clean energy goes today

The model's key rule is that new clean energy goes first to where it is most needed. How different is that from today?

The research checked. Developing and emerging economies, leaving out China, receive only about 20% of the world's investment in clean energy. Yet these countries hold about two-thirds of the world's people. Africa receives about 2%.

When the model is run with that pattern, the world's energy gap does not close. By year 15 it has grown: the shortfall is 14% worse than at the start. With an ambitious cooperative market, sending half of new capacity to regions in need, 14% of the gap closes by year 15. Under UNIRA's needs-first rule, 60% closes. Chapter 13 tells that comparison in full.

Even with realistic mistakes, the advantage holds. With strong safeguards against waste and theft, UNIRA still closes about 52% of the energy gap by year 15. Chapter 14 explains how.

What changes for each country

If the world's energy were shared equally today, the change would be very different from place to place.

RegionChange in final energy per person under equal shares
France−43% (from 90 to 51 GJ), still above comfortable
United States−72%
India2.7 times more
Nigeria9 times more

These are large numbers, and they need to be read carefully.

First, they show equal shares of today's energy. UNIRA's actual target is 40 GJ for everyone, reached over a generation, as new clean energy is built. Rich regions come down step by step, at 3–4% a year, not overnight.

Second, energy does not move like grain. It cannot be loaded onto a ship. A person in Nigeria gets more energy when solar fields, grids and efficient buildings are built in Nigeria. That is why this is a decades-long building programme.

Third, a French person at 51 GJ is still above the comfortable level. A cut in energy is not a cut in comfort. Much of it comes from insulation, heat pumps and electric transport, which do the same work with less.

What does it cost rich regions overall? The research compared each region after 30 years inside UNIRA with the same region in a reformed market world. By that measure, in the 33 richest regions average energy use per person is 6–12% lower: about 6% in France, about 12% in the United States and Japan. That is an average for the whole region, not a loss for every person. Nobody falls below the comfortable level, health care and housing are unchanged, and 44% of their people are better off even at the lowest UNIRA income (Chapter 15). The reduction falls on the heaviest energy users. Chapter 15 asks whether that is a price worth paying.

What is impossible is the opposite path. Giving everyone the energy an average European uses today, about 82 GJ, would need 1.6 times today's world supply. That does not fit any climate budget.

The rebound trap

There is a risk hidden in all of this, and Chapter 9 explored it in detail.

When energy becomes cheaper or easier to use, people often use more of it. This is called the rebound effect. A better-insulated home may simply be kept warmer.

The evidence is clear:

  • rebound from energy savings is usually below 30%, but it is real;
  • homes on flat-rate heating use up to about 30% more than metered homes;
  • free public transport raised trips by 14% in Tallinn and 70% in Dunkirk.

Energy, unlike food, has no natural ceiling. People stop eating when they are full. They do not stop wanting warmth, light, speed or space in the same way.

The research tested what rebound would do to the plan. If rich regions lose 20–30% of their energy savings to rebound, world energy peaks at 424–436 EJ. That is above today's 409 EJ in the model, and outside the 1.5 °C envelope. With a rebound of only 10%, the peak is 413 EJ, just above the line.

The answer is written into Article 19 of UNIRA's draft constitution. Energy, travel and data are always metered and paid per unit, at UNIRA's fixed prices. They are never offered at a flat rate or without limit.

That is why the teacher in Lille sees a meter by the door. It is not there to punish. It is there because every unit counts, and everyone can see it.

Written into the constitution

The draft constitution turns these findings into law. Article 21 says that total energy use, emissions, materials, land and water "stay within limits set from the best science". It adds that regions using more than a comfortable level of energy reduce their use each year, while regions below it rise.

Article 10 says that new energy systems go to regions by measured need and number of people, needs first. Article 6 guarantees everyone "energy, transport and digital access sufficient for a comfortable life".

Together, these three articles are this chapter in legal form: enough for all, restraint at the top, and limits set by science.

Honest answers to hard questions

"No country has ever cut energy use 3–4% a year for decades. Isn't this impossible?" It has never been done. The research says so plainly, and it is one of the largest uncertainties in this book. What makes it plausible is that much of the cut can come from technology: heat pumps, insulation, electric transport. What makes it hard is that it must last for decades and win public consent. Nobody should pretend otherwise.

"If 1.5 °C is already lost, why bother?" Because the limits are graded, not a single cliff edge. The budgets show it: about 490 Gt for a 50% chance of staying under 1.7 °C, and about 1,050 Gt for 2 °C. The 2 °C limit is still open. UNIRA's plan fits it comfortably, and fits 1.5 °C with a temporary overshoot.

"Will a cold country and a hot country really get the same 40 GJ?" Not necessarily in practice. The 40 GJ is the planning level the research uses for every region. But the research also notes that its thresholds are not yet adjusted for climate or age. A cold, ageing region may need more heat. A hot one may need more cooling. How to adjust is still open.

"Why should the poorest wait while the rich cut slowly?" They do not wait. Under the needs-first rule, each year's new clean energy goes first to the regions furthest below 40 GJ. Rich regions, already above 40 GJ, are cutting, not receiving. The cut at the top and the rise at the bottom happen at the same time.

"What about poorer people inside rich regions? Won't they suffer from the cuts?" The research looked at this directly. At the lowest UNIRA salary, half the population of France and Germany is already better off than today, and the poorest tenth gains 146% to 258%. Fixed prices also end household price shocks such as the energy-price crisis of 2022. Chapter 15 gives the details.

"Isn't 40 GJ too generous? Why not aim for the decent level and save the planet more?" That is a fair challenge. A decent life needs far less, about 15 GJ. UNIRA chooses comfort because its aim is a good life, not a minimum one. The evidence says that choice still fits inside the 1.5 °C range. Aiming lower would leave more room for error. Aiming at European levels would not fit at all.

What the evidence says

  • A comfortable 40 GJ of final energy per person totals 386 EJ in 2050, about 6–13% less than the world uses today (409 EJ in the UNIRA model, 442 EJ per the IEA). It sits inside the IPCC 1.5 °C range and comfortably under 2 °C.
  • An independent study finds 40 GJ per person gives "just under 400 EJ", matching UNIRA's figure.
  • It needs about half of final energy as electricity, fossil CO₂ near zero by 2050, and solar and wind built 1.7–1.9 times faster than the 2025 record of 669 GW. The world's COP28 tripling goal already implies about 1,122 GW of renewables a year.
  • The 1.5 °C carbon budget is about 3–4 years of today's emissions. Any path now means a temporary overshoot and some CO₂ removal. 2 °C is still within reach.
  • In a 30-year transition, every region reaches 40 GJ by year 27. World energy stays at or below today's level if rich regions cut energy use about 3–4% a year. Without that restraint, it peaks at 453 EJ.
  • Today only about 20% of clean-energy investment reaches developing economies outside China, home to two-thirds of humanity.
  • Equal shares cut energy use in France by about 43% and in the USA by about 72%, and raise it 2.7 times in India and 9 times in Nigeria.
  • A rebound of 20–30% would push world energy outside the climate envelope. Energy must stay metered.

What we still don't know

  • No country has ever cut energy use by 3–4% a year for decades. Whether rich regions can do it, and how people will feel about it, is untested.
  • Solar and wind at nearly twice the record pace has never been done. The 1.9× figure is a first-order estimate, not a detailed plan.
  • How much CO₂ removal the overshoot will need, and whether it can be done at scale, is outside this research.
  • Two estimates of today's world final energy are used: 442 EJ from the IEA and 409 EJ in UNIRA's transition model. The conclusions hold with either, but the gap shows the uncertainty.
  • The carbon-budget estimates differ in method and starting date, and one source is internally inconsistent about its start.
  • The carbon budgets are global. How emissions and the remaining budget are shared between regions has not been modelled.
  • The energy thresholds are not adjusted for climate or age.
  • The 2050 population of 9.66 billion was not re-checked against the UN source in this research.
  • There is no global statistic for military energy use. The 5.5% figure is for emissions, used as a proxy.
  • Rebound under a fixed-price, metered system has never been observed. The 10–30% values come from today's economies.

---

Chapter 11 — Materials, land and food

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.3, V6.5–V7.1) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

On a Saturday morning in a small town in Brittany, a repair workshop opens its doors. A man brings in a washing machine that has stopped spinning. A teenager brings a phone with a cracked screen. An older woman brings a lamp that belonged to her mother. By noon, most of them leave with their things working again.

Across the square, a family does its weekly shopping. They buy less meat than their parents did, and more beans, vegetables and bread. At home, the leftovers from Friday become Saturday's soup.

This scene is imagined. The workshop, the family and the soup are composites. But each of them stands for a choice that the numbers in this chapter make necessary. Chapter 10 showed that energy is not the hardest limit on a comfortable life for everyone. This chapter looks at the limits that are: the materials we dig out of the ground, the land we farm, and the food we grow and throw away.

The heaviest number: materials

Everything we use is made of something. Stone and sand for buildings and roads. Metals for machines. Wood, crops and fuels. Researchers add all of this up, by weight, and call it material extraction.

In 1970, the world extracted about 30 billion tonnes of materials a year. Today it extracts about 106 billion tonnes. Per person, that rose from 23 kilograms a day to 39 kilograms a day. Over a year, that is about 14.2 tonnes for every person on Earth.

If historical trends continue, extraction would rise by another 60% by 2060. The UN's International Resource Panel also models a "systemic change" path, in which extraction peaks around 2040 instead.

A worked example: 39 kilograms a day

Each day, for each person on Earth, about 39 kilograms of material is dug, cut, harvested or pumped out of the ground. Most of it we never see: it becomes buildings, roads, machines and fuel.

The sustainable level discussed below, about 5.2 tonnes a year, works out at roughly 14 kilograms a day. That is the size of the change: from about 39 kilograms a day to about 14.

How much is sustainable?

Researchers do not agree on one number, but they agree on the range:

  • O'Neill and colleagues (2018) use 7.2 tonnes per person, based on a world limit of 50 billion tonnes a year shared among 7 billion people;
  • Dittrich and colleagues (2012) put the limit at 8 tonnes per person by 2030, also from a world total of 50 billion tonnes;
  • the UN's International Resource Panel proposes 6 to 8 tonnes per person by 2050;
  • Bringezu proposes about 5 tonnes (between 3 and 6), based on returning to the world's extraction in the year 2000, about 50.8 billion tonnes.

A closer look. Notice that most of these limits rest on the same idea: a world total of about 50 billion tonnes a year. The per-person figure then depends on how many people share it. With 9.66 billion people in 2050, a world limit of 50 billion tonnes gives 5.2 tonnes per person. That is UNIRA's central value. Some of these sources were read only as cited in O'Neill's paper, not in the original texts. The research marks them as partly verified.

Material footprint per person per yearTonnes
Today, world average14.2
Sustainable, high8
Sustainable, central5.2
Sustainable, low3

Going from 14.2 to 5.2 tonnes means a cut of about 2.7 times. The research calls this the hardest constraint after carbon. It is harder than energy, and harder than food.

Not only UNIRA's problem

This is not a problem only for UNIRA. In 2018, O'Neill and colleagues found that no country meets the basic needs of its people at a globally sustainable level of resource use.

Their study set a fair share of several planetary limits for each person. A few of them show how tight the limits are.

O'Neill et al. 2018: sustainable share per person per yearValue
CO₂ emissions (consistent with 2 °C)1.61 tonnes
Material footprint7.2 tonnes
Water drawn from rivers, lakes and aquifers574 m³
Nitrogen8.9 kg
Phosphorus0.89 kg

Only 34% of countries stayed within the CO₂ limit alone.

The study's conclusion was mixed. Physical needs could probably be met for everyone within limits. But wider, qualitative goals would take 2 to 6 times the sustainable level of resources at today's efficiency.

A second study, by Fanning and colleagues (2022), tracked countries over time. The share of countries overshooting Earth's limits grew between 1992 and 2015, from 32–55% to 50–66%. On current trends, overshoot deepens to 2050, without ending the shortfalls of the poor.

So the question is not whether we can keep using materials the way rich countries do now. We cannot. The question is how to live well on much less.

Stock first: use what already exists

UNIRA's answer starts with a rule. Article 11 of the draft constitution says: "Before producing new, society maintains, repairs, renovates, reuses and converts what exists." It adds: "Nothing is produced only to sustain sales. There is no waste and no overproduction."

What does this mean in practice? The research looked at France, following the work hours behind every product. Chapter 2 tells that story in detail. Here are the parts that matter for materials.

Durable goods, like electronics, appliances, vehicles, furniture and textiles, use only about 0.4 billion hours of French work a year. That is just 0.9% of all hours. Making them last longer, net of the extra repair work, would save only a little French work time:

Durable goods last longer byShare of French work hours saved
+25%0.14%
+50%0.23%
+100% (twice as long)0.35%

Why so little? Because most durable goods are not made in France. Imported durables embody about 1.27 billion hours of work. That is three times the domestic figure.

A closer look: what "hours-equivalent" means

The 1.27 billion hours is not a count of hours worked in China or Vietnam. The research has no data on that. Instead, it asks a different question: how many hours would France need to make those imported goods itself, with its own factories and methods?

That is called an hours-equivalent. It shows that a lot of labour sits inside imports. It does not measure the actual hours, materials or emissions abroad. Across all goods, France takes in about 10.9 billion hours-equivalent through imports and sends out about 9.7 billion through exports.

The table above also rests on one assumption. Each hour of manufacturing saved is assumed to need 0.25 hours of extra repair work. That number is a synthetic assumption, not a measurement.

Why repair is a world rule

So stock-first does little to shorten the French working week. Its main gains happen abroad: less labour, fewer materials and lower emissions in the countries that make our phones, machines and clothes.

For a world federation, that is a real gain. A washing machine that lasts twice as long means one fewer machine mined, smelted, shipped and assembled somewhere else.

This is one reason UNIRA treats the world as one economy. Seen from one country, repair looks like a small saving. Seen from the whole planet, it is one of the main ways to cut material use.

Building: the biggest pile of materials

Buildings are where the materials question becomes concrete, often literally. Chapter 7 showed what comfortable homes for everyone require: building or upgrading about 109 billion square metres of floor space over about 30 years, mostly in Africa and South Asia. This section looks only at what that means for materials and carbon.

Each square metre carries carbon before anyone moves in. This is called embodied carbon: the emissions from making cement, steel and bricks, transporting them, and building.

Upfront embodied carbon per m² of floorCentral value (range)Over the whole programmeShare of the 1.5 °C budget
Conventional reinforced concrete400 kg CO₂e (250–600)43 Gtabout a third
Low-carbon frame: bamboo, timber, vaults, low cement150 kg CO₂e (30–265)16 Gtabout 12%

Built the conventional way, the homes would use about a third of the world's remaining 1.5 °C budget of 130 Gt. Built with low-carbon frames, about an eighth.

A closer look: where the savings really come from

The evidence on building materials has one surprise. Changing the walls saves much less than most people expect.

  • In Malaysia, the same building made with a concrete frame and fired brick emitted about 406 kg per m². With interlocking compressed earth bricks, it emitted about 265 kg: a cut of 35%.
  • In Nepal, compressed earth blocks emit 75–78% less than fired brick, by weight.
  • In Kenya, cement makes up 83–91% of the footprint of compressed earth blocks. At 10% cement, the blocks are no better than machine-cut stone.

So the big savings do not come from the walls. They come from replacing the concrete frame, floors and roof with bamboo, timber or low-cement designs. One study found bamboo buildings at about 30 kg per m², against 240 for concrete or brick. Overall, the choice of structure cuts upfront carbon by about 3 to 8 times.

The central low-carbon value of 150 kg is a judgement. The research found no whole-house carbon assessment for a concrete home in India or sub-Saharan Africa.

A closer look: how fast the pile must grow

The materials question is also a question of speed. How much must be built each year?

The research answers in two steps. First, the backlog. Bringing everyone to a comfortable 27 m² per person means adding about 65 billion m². Building about 4.7 billion m² a year, kept up steadily, closes that backlog by year 27.

Second, the full programme. The world's population is still growing, and some need is met by upgrading existing homes. Counting both, about 109 billion m² must be built or upgraded. For that, building capacity in regions of need must grow by 5–10% a year. It peaks at about 6–7 billion m² a year, the rate per person that China reached in 2011.

For comparison, the world's stock of homes grew by about 3.4 billion m² in 2024. That is a net figure: new building minus homes lost. Gross construction is higher. The research found no source that splits new building by countries' income group.

Every square metre also takes work: about 33 to 64 hours of labour, in the research's range. That is why Chapter 7 calls people, not materials, the binding limit on the pace. Materials decide the carbon. People decide the speed.

Not only homes

Homes are not the only buildings a comfortable life needs. Schools and hospitals need materials too. The research gives their embodied energy per square metre:

BuildingEmbodied energy per m²
Homes2–4 GJ
Schools4.5–7.5 GJ
Hospitals14–23 GJ

For homes, 2–4 GJ per square metre adds up to about 6.5 EJ a year over 30 years. That is affordable in energy terms, under 2% of the 386 EJ a year of Chapter 10. In materials, it is heavy. That is why the choice of materials matters as much as the speed.

Using what already stands

Stock-first applies to buildings too. About 40% of the housing need could be met by upgrading or extending existing homes, using about 40% of the materials of new building. Renovating an existing building emits 50–75% less than building new.

Why not simply fill empty homes? Because they are in the wrong places. The large stocks of empty homes are far from where homes are missing.

Empty homesNumberShare of homes
China, urban (2017)about 65 million21.4%
European Union (2021)46.6 million19.7%, including second homes
Japan (2023)9.0 million13.8%
India, urban (2011)11.1 millionabout 12%

Homes cannot be moved between regions. India's empty urban homes would meet only about 0.5% of the world's need. Most of the need is missing space, not empty or broken homes. Most of it must still be built.

This leads to one of the research's sharpest tests. If material use cannot fall by at least 2 times while homes and new energy systems are built, UNIRA's physical claims fail.

Land: how much cropland per person?

Now turn from what we build to what we eat.

In 2021, the world farmed about 1,580 million hectares of cropland. That is about 0.2 hectares, or 2,000 square metres, per person. Cropland per person has already fallen by 18% since 2000, as population grew.

Cropland is only part of the picture. All agricultural land, including pasture, covered about 4,788 million hectares. Meadows and pastures alone took about 3,208 million. Most farmland, in other words, is meadow and pasture, not crops.

How much can the world farm without destroying forests and wild land? The research uses three limits for 2050:

Cropland per person in 2050m²Basis
Low1,346EAT-Lancet 2019 cropland limit
Central1,636no expansion of cropland
High1,816EAT-Lancet 2025 scenario
Todayabout 2,000FAO

A worked example: the central limit

The central value is simple arithmetic. Keep today's cropland exactly as it is, about 1,580 million hectares, and share it among 9.66 billion people in 2050. Each person then has about 1,636 square metres. That is a plot of about 40 metres by 40 metres.

The low value comes from the 2019 EAT-Lancet limit of about 13 million square kilometres of cropland for the world. The high value comes from the 2025 EAT-Lancet scenario, in which cropland grows by about 11%.

Even the high limit is below today's level per person. With more people and no new land, each person's share of cropland must shrink. The food system has to do more with less.

The planetary-health diet

In 2025, the EAT-Lancet Commission, a group of scientists, published an updated plan for feeding the world within Earth's limits. Its main message is hopeful: a healthy diet for 9.6 billion people is possible.

It sets clear limits:

  • no conversion of intact land, with all farmland kept below 48 million square kilometres;
  • nitrogen surplus below 57 million tonnes a year;
  • phosphorus loss of about 4.6 million tonnes a year;
  • about 2,000 cubic kilometres a year of water drawn from rivers, lakes and aquifers;
  • food-system greenhouse gases of 5 billion tonnes CO₂-equivalent by 2050.

And it names three conditions:

  • diets must change, with about 33% less beef, lamb and other meat from ruminant animals;
  • food loss and waste must be halved;
  • farm productivity must rise by 7–10% above business as usual.

A closer look: how certain is the land result?

Under the Commission's plan, total farmland falls by about 7%, or roughly 340 million hectares. Cropland rises by about 11%. Less land goes to grazing, and somewhat more to crops.

Those central numbers hide wide ranges. Across the models the Commission used, the change in cropland runs from −3% to +42%. The change in total farmland runs from −26% to +1%. One model even shows cropland falling by 22%.

So the direction is clear: less grazing land, roughly stable cropland. The size of the change is uncertain. That is why UNIRA's research uses a low, central and high limit, not one number.

Calories: comfortable, not minimal

The Commission's reference diet provides about 2,400 kilocalories a day for an adult. That is down from 2,500 in its 2019 report.

UNIRA's comfortable diet, introduced in Chapter 1, is 2,470 kilocalories eaten per person per day: varied, plentiful and enjoyed. A decent diet, by comparison, is about 2,000–2,150. For protein, a safe level is about 0.83 grams per kilogram of body weight a day, or about 50–55 grams for an adult. The world already supplies about 92 grams per person.

Can that comfortable diet fit for 9.7 billion people? The research gives a clear answer: only with a planetary-health diet and halved waste. Halving waste is necessary, but it is not enough on its own. What people eat matters most for land and nitrogen.

Water: the quiet limit

Water rarely makes headlines in debates about a good life. It should.

The research sets out how much water a home needs:

Domestic water per person per dayLitres
Basic floor (WHO)20
Decent living50
Comfortable (derived)about 100
Observed where water is piped into the home155

Home use is only one part of the story. The EAT-Lancet limit of about 2,000 cubic kilometres a year covers the whole food system. O'Neill's fair share, 574 cubic metres per person a year, covers all water drawn from rivers, lakes and aquifers.

The research has not yet tested water limits region by region. Water needs also rise in hot climates and for hard physical work. This is a gap.

Waste: the easiest saving

Food is lost at two stages.

Between the harvest and the shop, 13.3% of food is lost: in storage, transport and processing. That share has not improved. It was about 13.0% in 2015.

In shops, restaurants and homes, another 19% is wasted. That is about 1.05 billion tonnes a year, or 132 kilograms per person. Most of it is thrown away at home: about 79 kilograms per person, or 631 million tonnes in all. Restaurants and canteens waste about 36 kilograms per person, and shops about 17.

Where food is lost or wastedShare or amount
Harvest to shop (lost)13.3%
Shops, restaurants, homes (wasted)19%, 1.05 billion tonnes
— of which homes79 kg per person
— of which restaurants and canteens36 kg per person
— of which shops17 kg per person

Together, food lost and wasted amounts to about 775 kilocalories per person per day. Growing it takes about 20% of the world's farmland.

A worked example: a week's bin

Spread over a year, 79 kilograms of household waste per person is about 1.5 kilograms a week. For a family of four, that is about six kilograms every week: food that was grown, bought, carried home and thrown away.

A closer look: two numbers, two stages

The 13.3% and the 19% are measured at different points, by different agencies. The UN Food and Agriculture Organization counts losses from farm to shop. The UN Environment Programme counts waste in shops, food service and homes.

This matters for the calculations in Chapter 1. The food supply figures used there are measured after most losses on the way to the shop. So only the 19% consumer waste is applied to them. Adding both would count the same loss twice.

What halving waste does

Chapter 1 showed what halving consumer waste would do for today's world:

Comfortable diet (2,470 kcal eaten) for everyone todayWorld supply ÷ need
At today's waste0.99
With consumer waste halved1.11

At today's waste, the world is 1% short. With half the waste, it has 11% to spare. Moving about 7% of world food supply between regions then reaches everyone.

In UNIRA's 30-year plan, food is redistributed from the first year, and waste is halved over ten years. Everyone's diet is covered from year 1. Comfortable diets follow as waste falls.

Will people waste less simply because they feel secure? Chapter 9 looked at this. The evidence is weak. Household food waste differs by only about 7 kilograms per person per year between richer and poorer countries. In the model, a security-driven fall in waste lifts the comfortable-diet ratio only from 1.105 to 1.11–1.12. Halving waste has to be a deliberate goal, not a side effect.

Chapter 9 also found that ultra-processed food, freely available, led people to eat about 508 more kilocalories a day. What is produced shapes what is eaten. Article 11's rule against producing "only to sustain sales" applies to food too.

Food in the working week

How much of the economy does food take? Less than most people think.

In France, the farms, fishing boats and food factories that feed households account for about 3.6% of all work hours. Food manufacturing alone takes about 1.3 billion hours. Restaurants and accommodation take another 2.4 billion hours a year, out of 45 billion in total.

So cutting food waste will not, by itself, shorten the French working week much. The research has not yet measured how many hours less waste would save. Its value lies elsewhere: in land, water, nitrogen and emissions, and in food that reaches people who need it.

Honest answers to hard questions

"Will UNIRA tell me what to eat?" The draft constitution leaves daily life, culture and traditions to each region (Article 4). It does not set a menu. But the evidence is plain: a comfortable diet for everyone fits only if diets shift towards the planetary-health pattern. That plan asks for about a third less ruminant meat, not none. How regions bring that about, and whether people agree to it, is one of the largest open questions in this book.

"Does halving waste mean I get less food?" No. The comfortable diet of 2,470 kilocalories is food actually eaten. Waste is food that was bought and never eaten. Halving consumer waste is what lifts the world's supply from 0.99 to 1.11 times a comfortable diet for everyone. Less waste means more food reaching plates, not less on yours. What changes is how carefully food is bought, stored and used, and the mix on the plate: more plants, less ruminant meat.

"Why not just farm more land?" Because the land would come from forests and wild places. The EAT-Lancet plan forbids converting intact land. Cropland per person has already fallen by 18% since 2000. More people and no new land means each person's share must shrink further.

"Can the world build 109 billion square metres and still cut materials 2.7 times?" Nobody knows. The research names this as a test that could prove UNIRA wrong. Low-carbon frames, upgrading existing homes and making goods last are the main tools. Whether they are enough has not been shown.

"If repair saves so few French hours, why bother?" Because the savings are real, just not in France. Most durable goods are made elsewhere. A longer life for a phone or a washing machine means less mining, smelting and shipping in other regions. For one world economy, that counts.

"Won't better technology solve the materials problem on its own?" It may help. O'Neill's finding that wider goals need 2 to 6 times the sustainable level of resources is measured at today's efficiency. Better efficiency could shrink that gap. But no evidence in this research shows it closing the gap alone. Fanning's study shows overshoot growing, not shrinking, under current trends.

What the evidence says

  • The world extracts about 14.2 tonnes of materials per person a year, about 39 kg a day. A sustainable level is 3–8 tonnes, central 5.2. That is a 2.7 times cut, the hardest limit after carbon.
  • No country today meets its people's basic needs at a globally sustainable level of resource use.
  • Stock-first saves little French work time, because most durable goods are imported. Imported durables embody about 1.27 billion hours-equivalent, three times the domestic figure. The gains are mostly abroad, which matters for a world federation.
  • Low-carbon building frames cut embodied carbon from about 400 to 150 kg CO₂e per m². Over the whole housing programme, that is 43 Gt against 16 Gt. The frame, floors and roof matter far more than the walls.
  • Cropland per person must fall from about 2,000 m² today to 1,346–1,816 m² by 2050.
  • A comfortable diet of 2,470 kcal for 9.7 billion people fits only with a planetary-health diet and halved waste. Today 19% of food is wasted by consumers and 13.3% is lost before it reaches shops.
  • With consumer waste halved, today's world food supply covers a comfortable diet for everyone 1.11 times.
  • Food chains account for about 3.6% of French work hours.

What we still don't know

  • Whether a 2.7 times cut in materials is possible while about 109 billion m² of homes and much new energy infrastructure are built. If material use cannot fall by at least 2 times, UNIRA's physical claims fail. The research names this as a falsification test.
  • How much labour, material and emissions longer-lasting goods would save abroad. The research measures imported hours-equivalent, not foreign hours, materials or emissions.
  • The repair work needed per hour saved (0.25) is a synthetic assumption, not a measured value.
  • Whether people will change diets as much as the planetary-health plan needs. Diet is the binding lever for land, and it depends on culture and choice.
  • How land, nitrogen and water limits fall region by region. The research uses world averages per person.
  • The EAT-Lancet land results vary widely between models: cropland changes from −3% to +42%.
  • Some sustainable-materials limits were read only as cited by other authors, not in their original sources.
  • No verified whole-house carbon assessment was found for concrete homes in India or sub-Saharan Africa. The low-carbon central value of 150 kg is a judgement.
  • The food-waste figures are careful estimates, but they are uncertain. How many work hours less waste would save has not been measured.

---

Chapter 12 — No more wars

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.6–V7.6) and the draft Constitution of One Earth, and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Imagine a corporal called Amina, on her last morning in uniform.

She hands in her rifle at the depot. A clerk checks the serial number against a list and signs a form. Outside, a bus waits to take her and thirty others to a nursing school in the next town. Her income does not stop. Her pay continues while she trains, and in three years she will be a nurse.

Amina is not a real person. She is a composite, and her morning has not happened anywhere yet. But each part of it comes from UNIRA's rules: the arms handed over, the full income, the offer of training in health. This chapter asks what the world gains when its armies stand down, and what could go wrong.

What war costs today

Start with what the world spends on being ready for war.

In 2020 the world's armed forces counted about 27.4 million people. That is the latest year with a complete count.

In 2025 the world spent about USD 2,887 billion on its militaries. That is 2.5% of everything the world produces in a year. Spread over every person alive, it is about USD 352 each.

Armies also burn fuel and need factories. Military activity, including the supply chains that make weapons and equipment, causes about 5.5% of the world's greenhouse gas emissions. The estimate is uncertain: the range runs from 3.3% to 7.0%. Operations alone, without the supply chains, cause about 1.0%.

What the world's militaries useAmount
People in armed forces (2020)27.4 million
Spending (2025)USD 2,887 billion
Share of world output2.5%
Spending per person on EarthUSD 352
Spending per member of the armed forcesabout USD 105,000 a year
Share of global emissions, with supply chains5.5% (range 3.3–7.0%)

None of this feeds anyone, houses anyone or heals anyone. It is the price of preparing for war.

A closer look: how these numbers were counted

Each figure comes from a different source, and each has its limits.

The headcount comes from the World Bank. It is 27,406,000 people in 2020. Later years have no complete figure yet, so 2020 is the latest usable one.

The spending figure comes from the Stockholm International Peace Research Institute (SIPRI), in its fact sheet of April 2026. The research checked it against the original document.

The emissions share comes from a 2022 study by Scientists for Global Responsibility and the Conflict and Environment Observatory. It puts military emissions at about 2,750 million tonnes of CO₂-equivalent a year. Most of it comes not from operations, which cause about 1.0%, but from the supply chains behind them.

One more figure is missing entirely. No one publishes a global total for military energy use. The research looked and found none. That gap matters later in this chapter.

What is freed

UNIRA's Constitution says it plainly: war is abolished. No region may keep armed forces or weapons of war, or make them. Existing armies are dissolved within 5 years of a region becoming a full member.

What happens to the soldiers? Every one of them is offered work, training and full income. The Constitution names the fields: health, construction, civil protection, or service in the One Earth Peace Force. Nobody is thrown out of work. And nobody is forced into a job: Article 7 guarantees free choice of occupation.

People

The 27.4 million soldiers are about 0.79% of the world's labour force. That sounds small. But they are people already trained, organised and paid, who could do other work.

Chapter 8 showed that health workers are the world's hardest shortage. The research tested what happens if a quarter of today's soldiers volunteer to train in health. In the ambitious programme, half do.

The effect is largest early, when the shortage hurts most. By year 10 of the plan, volunteers lift the world to about 71 health workers per 10,000 people, against 64 without them.

Over the full plan the effect is smaller. With central training capacity, expanded 7% a year, a quarter of soldiers volunteering brings EU-level care for everyone about a year sooner: 36 years instead of 37. Schools and teachers are the real bottleneck, and former soldiers cannot train faster than schools can teach them. Disarmament helps. It does not replace building training capacity.

Other former soldiers can build homes, run logistics or work in civil protection. Building is a large need. Chapter 7 showed that a generation of comfortable homes needs about 240 to 265 million construction workers at its peak. The world has about 294 million, of whom only about 116 million are in South Asia and Africa. Every pair of trained hands counts.

Energy and climate

Chapter 10 explained that rich regions must use less energy while poorer regions catch up. The question is how fast.

To keep world energy at or below today's level, regions above the comfortable level must cut their energy use by about 4% a year. Without weapons production and military fuel, about 3% a year is enough.

That difference matters. A 3% yearly cut is hard. A 4% cut is harder still. In the model, the "no wars" case lets rich regions take the gentler path. World energy in year 30 is then about 400 EJ, against 390 EJ in the central case. Both stay below the model's 409 EJ for today.

The research is clear about what this does and does not do. Freeing military energy matters mainly for the climate. It does not make poorer regions reach a comfortable level any faster. That still depends on how fast clean energy is built and shared.

One honest caveat. The 5.5% is a share of emissions, and the research uses it as a stand-in for energy. That is an assumption, stated openly.

Money

Without armies, the world's 2.5% of output is mostly freed. Not all of it, because One Earth still needs a small armed force, as the next section explains. With that force in place, about 2.45% of world output is freed.

That is a gross figure. It does not yet subtract the costs of the change itself. These include retraining and full income for 27 million former soldiers. They also include destroying weapons of mass destruction, and more police where policing is thin.

A closer look at scale. Two comparisons help. UN peacekeeping costs about USD 5.6 billion a year. That is about 0.2% of what the world spends on its militaries. And rich countries' development aid in 2025 was about USD 174.3 billion, only 0.26% of their national income, down from 0.34% in 2024, against a target of 0.7%. Set that beside the USD 2,887 billion spent on militaries. The world spends more than sixteen times as much preparing for war as it gives in aid.

One Earth owns the arms

Abolishing war on paper is easy. Keeping the peace is not.

Disputes between regions still happen. Under UNIRA they go to the Court of One Earth, whose rulings bind. But a ruling needs to be enforceable. People whose rights are attacked need protection. Disasters need fast, organised help.

So the Constitution keeps one armed force: the One Earth Peace Force. All arms belong to One Earth. Nuclear, chemical and biological weapons are destroyed under independent checks and never rebuilt.

This creates an obvious danger. Whoever controls the only armed force could dominate everyone. Article 5a of the Constitution is written against that danger:

  • Purpose. The Force enforces Court rulings, protects people whose rights are attacked, and helps in disasters. It is never used against peaceful protest, a lawful election or a region's democratic institutions.
  • Two keys. It may be deployed only with a ruling of the Court and a double majority of the regions. If lives are under immediate threat, it may act for at most 72 hours before it needs both.
  • No region controls it. No region provides more than 5% of its members or commanders. Commanders rotate every 3 years. Members never serve in their own region.
  • A size limit. The Force may never exceed 1 million members, about 12 per 100,000 people. Its budget may never exceed 0.15% of world output. Size, budget and every deployment are published each year.
  • Oversight. An Inspector-General, appointed by the Court, reports publicly. Anyone harmed by the Force can take their case to the Court.
  • A counterweight. Regions keep civilian police for everyday crime, under regional control, equipped only for policing.

Chapter 14 sets these controls beside UNIRA's other safeguards against power. Here the focus is on two practical questions: how big the Force should be, and whether such forces work.

How big should the Peace Force be?

The closest real-world example is UN peacekeeping.

A closer look: UN peacekeeping in numbers

In September 2025, UN peacekeeping had 59,376 uniformed personnel. They broke down like this:

UN peacekeeping, 30 September 2025People
Troops50,963
Police5,852
Staff officers1,627
Military observers934
Uniformed total59,376
All personnel, including civilians, in 11 operations67,783

They came from 121 countries. That already answers one doubt: a force drawn from many nations can work together.

The budget for 2024–25 was USD 5.6 billion. That is about USD 94,000 per uniformed peacekeeper. The UN reimburses countries about USD 1,428 per soldier per month.

Peacekeeping was larger a decade ago. In March 2015 it had 106,595 uniformed personnel in 16 operations, and a budget of USD 8.47 billion. Its peak, in April 2015, was about 108,000.

A worked example: building up to 500,000

The research built its estimate in three steps.

  1. Start from the largest deployment ever sustained. UN peacekeeping peaked at about 107,000 people deployed at once.
  2. Allow for rotation. A force cannot keep every member deployed all the time. People rotate home, train and rest. The research multiplies by about 3, which gives about 320,000. That multiplier is a planning assumption, not a measured fact.
  3. Add a reserve for disasters and special tasks. Enough for two or three Haiti-sized disasters at once, about 20,000 people each. Then add airlift, logistics, and checking the destruction of weapons of mass destruction.

The result is about 500,000 people. At a cost of about USD 120,000 per member, that is about USD 60 billion a year.

LowPlannedConstitutional ceiling
Members250,000500,0001,000,000
Per 100,000 people3.16.112.3
Share of today's armed forces0.9%1.8%3.6%
Times the 2015 UN peak2.34.79.4
Cost per memberUSD 100,000USD 120,000USD 150,000
BudgetUSD 25 bnUSD 60 bnUSD 150 bn
Share of world output0.02%0.05%0.15% at most
People freed from armies27.16 m26.9 m26.4 m
Share of world output freed (gross)2.48%2.45%2.37%

So the planned Peace Force is less than 2% of today's armies. It would cost about 0.05% of world output, against 2.5% today.

The research proposed writing the ceiling as a share of world population: 0.0125%, about 1 million people. That way the limit moves with the world's population.

What about disasters?

The largest disasters in recent memory needed tens of thousands of military personnel, not hundreds of thousands.

  • Haiti, 2010. After the earthquake, the United States sent 21,493 armed-forces personnel within two weeks, with 25 ships and 79 helicopters.
  • The 2004 tsunami. The United States had more than 13,000 military personnel in the region by 7 January 2005. A later figure of about 16,000 could not be checked, so the research does not use it.
  • Türkiye, 2023. After the earthquake, about 5,000 international rescuers came in 90 teams.

Civilian systems already do much of this work. The European Union's Civil Protection Mechanism links the EU states and 10 other countries. It was activated 64 times in 2025, and more than 900 times since 2001. Its budget for 2021–27 is about EUR 3.32 billion, or roughly EUR 0.47 billion a year. Worldwide, about 632,000 people work in humanitarian crisis response, 90% of them nationals of the country they serve. That last figure is reported secondhand.

So most disaster work can stay with civilians. Under the Constitution, the Peace Force responds "together with the Protection Service", UNIRA's civilian disaster body. How large that service should be is still to be studied.

Does peacekeeping work?

A fair critic will ask whether soldiers in blue helmets actually keep anyone safe.

The research looked at two well-known studies. The first, by Hultman, Kathman and Shannon (2013), examined African civil wars from 1991 to 2008. It found that when more UN troops and police were deployed, fewer civilians were targeted. Several thousand troops with several hundred police made a large difference.

The second, by Walter, Howard and Fortna (2021), reviewed the numerical studies of peacekeeping as a whole. Its authors describe "an extraordinary relationship between peacekeepers and peace".

A closer look. Both claims were checked against the papers' own summaries. The first is a single study of one region and period. The second is a review of many studies, which is why its conclusion carries more weight. Neither is an experiment: they observe real missions in real wars.

Peacekeeping is not perfect, and these studies measure today's missions, not a world force. But the evidence points the same way: a neutral force, deployed in enough numbers, saves lives.

The most dangerous moment

When is a world without armies most at risk? Not when it is finished, but while it is being built.

Research on military coups finds a clear pattern. A coup is an open attempt by the military or other elites to remove a government.

A closer look: how coups are counted

The main dataset, by Powell and Thyne (2011), sets precise rules. A coup counts as successful if its leaders hold power for at least 7 days. By that count, there were 7 coups between 2008 and 2010, and almost three dozen in that decade. Attempts have become less frequent since the end of the Cold War.

A study by Belkin and Schofer (2003) asked what makes coups likely. Risk is higher where civil society is weak, where government lacks legitimacy, and where coups have happened before.

The warning for UNIRA

One study, by De Bruin (2018), has a warning that matters directly for UNIRA. Creating a new armed force raises the chance of a coup attempt the following year. Having several independent forces reduces the chance that a coup succeeds, but not the chance that one is tried.

So the transition is the period of highest risk. Old armies are dissolving. A new Peace Force is forming. Loyalties are uncertain.

The Constitution responds in three ways:

  • the Force is built up gradually, with members from all regions from the first day;
  • the Inspector-General is in place before the first unit is formed;
  • regional police, under regional control, act as a counterweight.

The research also proposes keeping arms depots and supplies under separate control from combat command. That idea has not been tested by evidence. Another study, which found that measures to "coup-proof" a government deter coups, was seen only in a search summary. The research does not rely on it.

Disarmament, step by step

How would standing down actually unfold in one region? The rules give the order of events. The sketch below is imagined, but every step comes from the draft constitution.

First, the vote. A region joins by referendum. It then passes through three stages: observer, associate and full member (Article 40). As an observer, it builds the safeguard institutions. Nothing about its army changes yet.

Second, the guarantee. At full membership, One Earth takes on that region's security. The region's armed forces must be dissolved within five years (T-5).

Third, the offer. Every member of the old army is offered work, training and full income. Some, like the imagined Amina, train as nurses, which takes three years. Others train as builders, or join civil protection. A few join the Peace Force, but they never serve in their own region.

Fourth, the weapons. Arms pass to One Earth. Nuclear, chemical and biological weapons are destroyed under independent checks and never rebuilt (Article 5).

Throughout, the watchers. The Peace Force grows gradually, with members from all regions from the first day. Its Inspector-General is in place before the first unit forms. Regional police keep everyday order.

The order matters. In the rules, dissolving an army follows full membership, and with it One Earth's guarantee. It does not come first.

Countries that already live without armies

A world without armies sounds utopian. A few countries already live that way.

Costa Rica abolished its army in its 1949 Constitution. Article 12 reads: "The Army as a permanent institution is abolished." It keeps police for public order, and any military force must answer to civilian power. It has had no coup since. Whether the abolition deserves the credit is disputed.

Iceland has no standing army. It is the only member of NATO without one. Yet it was a founding member of NATO in 1949. A United States defence force was stationed there from 1951 to 2006. Its Coast Guard has about 280 staff.

Panama also has no army. Search results say it abolished its military in 1990 and banned it in its constitution in 1994, but the research could not verify those dates.

The World Bank records 0% of output spent on the military in all three countries from 2020 to 2024.

But there is a lesson in the details. The World Bank still counts about 10,000 armed personnel in Costa Rica and 28,000 in Panama. These are police and border units that carry weapons.

"No army", in practice, means two things. It means armed police for public order. And it means someone outside guarantees the country's security. Under UNIRA, One Earth gives that guarantee to every region.

How many police does a region need?

In functioning states today, there are roughly 130 to 350 police per 100,000 people.

Police per 100,000 people
Finland132
India155
Iceland184
United States242
Japan255
Costa Rica297
Panama315
Germany349

A UN analysis from 2006, reported secondhand, put the middle value at about 300. A figure often quoted as a "UN recommendation", 222 per 100,000, could not be found in any UN document, so the research does not use it.

At 300 per 100,000, the world would have about 24 million police: roughly 50 times the planned Peace Force. Everyday safety stays local, in the hands of each region.

When wars end, people go home

War does not only kill. It drives people from their homes.

At the end of 2025, 117.8 million people were forcibly displaced. That is about one person in every 70 on Earth. It was down from 123.2 million a year earlier, the first fall in a decade. They included:

  • 41.6 million refugees;
  • 9 million asylum-seekers;
  • 68.7 million people displaced inside their own country by conflict.

By the UN refugee agency's own definition, every one of them fled persecution, conflict, violence or human rights abuse. These are the causes UNIRA sets out to remove. 70% had been in exile for five years or more. Most did not go far: 65% live in a neighbouring country, and 68% in low- or middle-income countries.

The flows continue. In 2025, 5.4 million people crossed a border to flee. In 2024, conflict forced 20.1 million new moves inside countries.

Do people go back when wars end? History says many do.

After the conflictWho returned
Mozambique, peace accord of 1992about 1.7 million refugees by mid-1995; 75% of 3.7 million internally displaced people resettled by mid-1994
Syria, after December 2024more than 500,000 refugees by mid-May 2025, with 1.2 million internally displaced; 1.3 million refugees during 2025
Whole world, 202514.7 million people: 4.4 million refugees and 10.3 million internally displaced

In Mozambique, the yearly records show the wave: 604,387 refugees returned in 1993 and 804,376 in 1994. Worldwide, 2025 was the second-highest year for refugee returns since 1965.

A closer look: what the research assumes

The research does not claim everyone goes home. Its working assumption is that about 50–85% of people displaced by conflict return within roughly ten years. The rest settle where they are. New flows from war fall to about zero. That return share is an assumption, drawn from cases like Mozambique and Syria, not a measured world rate.

Returns are not automatic. The UN refugee agency says many of 2025's returns happened in hard conditions, including forced returns. Long exile, children born abroad and destroyed homes keep some people where they are. Those who stay should be treated as settled residents, not as refugees.

UNIRA also keeps a protection procedure for anyone threatened. Under Article 4b, protection comes first in the person's own region, through rebuilding and needs-first allocation. Where that is impossible, other regions share temporary shelter, paid for through the One Earth Fund. People return home when it is safe again.

Peace does not stop the weather

In 2024, floods, storms and other disasters displaced 45.8 million people, more than twice as many new moves as conflict caused that year. About 99% were weather-related, and almost all stayed inside their own country. That is a separate problem, and Chapter 5 explains how UNIRA handles it.

Honest answers to hard questions

"What stops a region from secretly rearming?" The Constitution forbids any region to keep or produce weapons of war. Disputes go to the Court, and the Peace Force exists to enforce its rulings. But the evidence reviewed for this chapter does not test how hidden rearmament would be detected. That is a real gap, and it needs its own study.

"Won't a single world force become a world tyrant?" That is the most serious objection, and the Constitution treats it as such. Two keys to deploy it. No region above 5%. Rotating command. A hard ceiling on size and budget. An Inspector-General who reports publicly. Regional police as a counterweight. None of this has been tested at world scale. The research says plainly that the build-up years are the riskiest.

"Isn't disarming naive while others stay armed?" UNIRA does not ask a region to disarm and stand alone. Armies are dissolved within five years of full membership, and One Earth then guarantees each region's security, much as an outside guarantee long did for Iceland. How a union of only some regions protects itself while others remain armed is not answered by the evidence in this chapter. It belongs to the study of the transition in Chapter 15.

"Will soldiers really want to become nurses and builders?" Nobody knows. The research tests a quarter and a half of soldiers volunteering for health, but these are assumptions. What UNIRA can promise is full income, training and free choice. Article 7 forbids forcing anyone into a particular job.

"Without armies, won't crime fill the gap?" The draft constitution is careful here. It aims to remove the main causes of crime: poverty, insecurity, inequality and war. But it notes that the evidence does not support a claim that crime disappears. So it keeps independent courts and regional police. Abolishing war is not the same as abolishing wrongdoing.

What the evidence says

  • Today's militaries take 27.4 million people, USD 2,887 billion a year (2.5% of world output) and cause about 5.5% of global emissions, mostly through their supply chains.
  • Disarmament frees people for health and building. Volunteers lift the world to about 71 health workers per 10,000 by year 10, against 64 without them.
  • Without military energy, rich regions can cut energy use by about 3% a year instead of 4% and still keep the world within its climate limits. This helps the climate, not the speed of convergence.
  • A Peace Force of about 500,000, under a ceiling of 1 million and 0.15% of world output, frees about 26.9 million people and 2.45% of world output.
  • UN peacekeeping, at 59,376 uniformed personnel from 121 countries and USD 5.6 billion, is found to reduce violence against civilians.
  • The largest recent disasters needed tens of thousands of military personnel, not hundreds of thousands.
  • New armed forces are most at risk of coup attempts while being built, which shapes how the Peace Force grows.
  • All 117.8 million forcibly displaced people fled causes UNIRA abolishes, and large returns follow peace: 14.7 million people went home in 2025.

What we still don't know

  • No global statistic for military energy use exists. The 5.5% is an emissions share used as a proxy.
  • The Peace Force size rests on assumptions: a threefold allowance for rotation, training and reserve, and a cost of USD 100,000–150,000 per member.
  • The freed 2.45% is gross. Retraining soldiers, destroying weapons and expanding police all cost something first.
  • How many soldiers would actually choose to train in health is unknown.
  • The build-up of a new armed force is when coups are most likely. Whether Article 5a's safeguards are enough has never been tested at world scale.
  • Separating arms depots from combat command is a proposal, not a tested safeguard.
  • How hidden rearmament would be detected, and how a partial union stays secure, are not covered by this evidence.
  • Some figures were seen only in search summaries or reported secondhand: Panama's dates, the UN police median, the humanitarian workforce. They are marked as such.
  • The size of the civilian Protection Service is still to be studied.
  • How many displaced people would return home is an assumption. Return rates vary widely.

---
