Chapter 13 — Better than the best market world

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.2–V6.9) and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Imagine a container of solar panels standing on a dock. It could go to two places.

The first is a wealthy suburb, where families already have heating, cooling and a car each. They want cheaper bills, and they can pay well. The second is a district in a fast-growing city, where people cook on charcoal and the clinic has power for a few hours a day. They need the panels far more. They can pay far less.

This dock is imagined, and so are both places. But the choice is real, and it is made every day. In a market, the container usually goes where the money is. In UNIRA, it goes where the need is.

This chapter asks how much that single difference matters. It also asks a harder question. Could a kinder market do just as well, without the rest of UNIRA?

Chapter 5 gave the short answer in one table. This chapter opens the box. It shows how the test was built, what each part of UNIRA contributes, where the result is strong and where it is weak.

A fair test

It is easy to make any system look good. You give it more workers, better technology or more generous assumptions than its rival. Then you announce that it wins.

The UNIRA research tried to avoid that trap. It compared three worlds with matched resources. All three have:

  • the same population;
  • the same technology;
  • the same capacity to build power plants, homes and training schools;
  • the same response of output to shorter hours;
  • the same goals: a 30-hour week and a comfortable life for everyone.

Only the institutions change. That way, any difference in the results comes from how each world organises itself. It does not come from luck or wishful thinking.

This is sometimes called a counterfactual. It means a careful "what if?" The question is not "what will happen?" It is "what would happen, if only this one thing were different?"

A closer look: what "closing the gap" means

Before the results, it helps to know what is being measured. The research looked at three needs that cannot simply be shipped from one place to another: energy, health workers and homes.

Food is different. Moving about 7% of the world's food supply would cover everyone. But a power grid, a nurse or a house must be built or trained where people live.

For each of the three needs, earlier chapters set a comfortable level:

NeedDecent levelComfortable levelWhere the world stands
Final energy per person, per year15.3 GJ40 GJabout 55 GJ on average, very unequally shared
Health workers per 10,000 people44.5 (WHO)131 (EU level)58 on average; 47.4 million workers in all
Floor space per person15 m²27 m²about 26 m² on average: 7 in Ethiopia, 10.5 in India, 45 in France, 55 in the USA

Averages hide the problem. The world has 58 health workers per 10,000 people on average. Yet 4.2 billion people live in regions below even the WHO level. Workers are where they are, not where they are needed.

A region's shortfall is the distance between where it is and the comfortable level. Add up the shortfalls of every region below the line, and you have the world's shortfall.

"Closing 60% of the gap" means that 60% of that total distance has been covered. A negative number means the opposite. The world's shortfall is larger than when it started.

How can a gap grow when the world is building? Because people keep being born. The model follows the UN's 2024 population projection. By 2050 the world will hold about 9.7 billion people. If new homes and clinics go mostly to places that are already comfortable, the places in need fall further behind.

A closer look: how the test was run

The comparison did not build new models. It reran two that already existed, changing only the institutional settings.

The first is a labour ledger for France in year 1. It counts the hours the economy needs and the hours people work. Chapter 3 described it. It asks whether a 30-hour week can keep today's output.

The second is a 30-year world transition. It divides the world into about 180 regions. Each year, the model decides where new capacity goes:

  • clean energy from solar and wind;
  • health workers, from a training plan, trained in-region;
  • new homes, from the world's building capacity, which the model sets at 3.3–4.7 billion m² a year.

In UNIRA's version, new capacity goes first to the regions furthest below the target. Regions above 40 GJ reduce their energy use each year. In the market versions, only part of the new capacity goes to regions in need. The rest goes where it goes today.

Each lever was set from evidence where evidence existed. Where it did not, the research said so. The comparison comes with 5 tests of its own.

Three worlds

World B is a reformed market. It is a good version of today. Employment is close to the best any country achieves today: 84% of adults aged 20 to 64. Wasteful selling activity is cut a little. Armies remain. New clean energy flows the way it does today.

World B2 is an ambitious cooperative market. It is far kinder than today. Half of all new energy capacity goes to regions in need. Rich regions cut their energy use faster.

World C is UNIRA. Everyone able to work does work, about 88% of adults. New capacity goes by need first. There is no commercial competition for profit, no army, and an ambitious health-training programme.

LeverB: reformed marketB2: cooperative marketC: UNIRA
Employment, ages 20–6484%84%88%
Activity that only serves sellingmodest cutmodest cutmoderate cut
New clean energy to regions in need20%50%100%
Energy cut in rich regions, per year2%3%3%, plus military energy freed
Health training+7% a year+7% a yearambitious, plus soldiers and prevention
New health workers to regions in need50%75%100%
New homes to regions in need30%50%75%

Where does the 20% come from? It is today's real pattern. Only about 20% of the world's clean-energy investment goes to developing countries outside China. Yet those countries hold about two-thirds of the world's population. Africa receives about 2%.

Aid tells a similar story. In 2025, rich donors gave about 0.26% of their national income in development aid. That was down from 0.34% the year before. The long-standing target is 0.7%. Only four countries exceed it: Norway, Luxembourg, Sweden and Denmark.

So B is not a caricature of markets. It is today's pattern of investment, with an employment rate near the best any country has reached. B2 is far more generous than anything today.

The results

The research ran each world for 30 years. It measured how much of the world's shortfall in energy, health care and homes each one closes.

OutcomeBB2C: UNIRA
Everyone at comfortable energy (40 GJ) bynot within 30 yearsnot within 30 yearsyear 27
Everyone at EU-level care (131 per 10,000) bynot within 30 yearsnot within 30 yearsyear 30
Energy shortfall closed, year 15−14% (the gap grows)14%60%
Health shortfall closed, year 15−4%4%38%
Housing shortfall closed, year 30−21%10%50%
France, year 1: extra output per hour needed for a 30-hour week5.0%5.0%0%
Europe pooled, year 1: the same measure6.8%6.8%1.3%
Peak world final energy409 EJ409 EJ409 EJ

Look at the minus signs in column B. In a reformed market, the world's gaps do not close. They grow. Population keeps growing. New capacity flows mostly to those who can pay. Every year, the regions in need fall a little further behind.

B2 does better. But after 15 years it has closed only 14% of the energy gap and 4% of the health gap. UNIRA closes 60% and 38%.

Now look at the last row. All three worlds use the same amount of energy at their peak: about 409 exajoules. That is today's level in the UNIRA model. UNIRA does not win by burning more. It wins by sending energy where it is missing.

An illustration: one year, two allocators

Here is a simple way to picture the difference. It is imagined, with no numbers attached.

Picture two regions. One sits well above 40 GJ. Its homes are warm and its factories run all day. The other sits far below. Its schools close at dusk.

In a given year, the world builds a fixed amount of new solar and wind. In World B, most of it goes to the first region. Its people can pay, and its banks can lend. The second region gets a small share. Meanwhile, its population grows. Next year, its energy per person is barely higher, or even lower.

In World C, the new capacity goes first to the second region. The first region, meanwhile, cuts its use a little each year. Next year the gap is smaller. Year after year, the two meet near the comfortable line.

Neither world built more. Neither world had better panels. The difference is where the panels went.

Which institution does the work?

UNIRA is a package of institutions. Which ones matter most? The research answered this by switching each lever from B to C, one at a time, and watching the result.

Needs-first allocation of energy. On its own, it brings every region to the comfortable level by year 27. This is the single most decisive institution. But alone it has a cost. Without restraint in rich regions and without the energy freed from armies, world energy would peak at 453 exajoules. That is above today's level and outside the climate limits. Allocation and restraint must go together.

Needs-first allocation of homes. On its own, it moves the housing result from −21% to +50% by year 30.

The health programme and training in-region. Both are needed. Either one alone closes only about 10% of the health gap by year 15.

Universal work. Raising employment from 84% to 88% cuts France's year-1 need for extra output per hour from 5.0% to 1.9%.

No commercial competition. Removing work that only serves selling, on its own, cuts the same need to 2.6%. Together with universal work, the gap closes completely.

Disarmament and faster energy cuts in rich regions. These matter mainly for the climate, as Chapter 12 explained. They do little for how fast poorer regions catch up.

One warning about this method. Switching levers one at a time ignores how they interact. The energy example shows that interactions can matter a great deal.

A closer look: why allocation needs restraint

The 453-exajoule result is worth slowing down for. It shows that a fair rule can still break the planet if it stands alone.

Suppose every region below 40 GJ rises towards it, and nobody above it changes anything. The world then simply adds energy at the bottom. Total use climbs well above today's.

The climate cannot carry that. Chapter 10 showed what it can carry. If every person on Earth used 40 GJ in 2050, the total would be about 386 EJ. That is 6–13% less than the world uses today. The range depends on the estimate of today's use: 409 EJ in the UNIRA model, or 442 EJ according to the IEA.

To stay at or below today's level while poorer regions rise, regions above 40 GJ must come down. The research found they must cut their use about 4% a year. If weapons production and military fuel stop, 3% a year is enough.

What does that mean for a rich region? Take France. If the world's energy were shared equally, France's final energy would fall from about 90 to 51 GJ per person. That is a cut of about 43%. It is still above the comfortable level. Nigeria's would rise about nine times.

The supply side must also move fast. The world added a record 669 GW of solar and wind in 2025. UNIRA's plan needs that pace multiplied by 1.7 to 1.9. The central plan builds about 1.2 TW a year.

So "needs first" is never only about giving. It is about giving and holding back, at the same time. A world that does one without the other fails, either the poor or the planet.

A closer look: the year-1 numbers for France

The France row deserves a few more words, because it links this chapter to Chapter 3.

In 2023, France's workers put in about 45.6 billion hours. Capping employees' usual week at 30 hours removes 16.7% of those hours. To keep the same output, each remaining hour would need to produce 20.1% more. At the 2015–19 trend, that is 32 years of productivity growth.

UNIRA does not wait 32 years. It fills the gap in two ways.

The first is more people working. Going from 84% to 88% employment means workplaces for about 5 million new workers in France. It also needs childcare and eldercare, so that parents and carers are free to work.

The second is less work that only serves selling. Advertising, speculative finance and real-estate intermediation could free between 1.7% and 6.6% of all hours. UNIRA cuts this activity more deeply than either market world.

In World B, both levers are weaker. The gap left is 5.0% in France. Across Europe, pooled together, it is 6.8%. In World C, it is 0% in France and 1.3% across Europe.

How kind would a market have to be?

A thoughtful reader will push back. "Fine," they might say. "But what if a market world were simply more generous? Send more aid and more investment to the poor, and keep the rest."

The research tested exactly that. It took the cooperative market, B2, and raised the share of new capacity sent to regions in need, step by step. In this test, the same share applies to energy, health workers and homes. Everything else stayed market: armies kept, today's pace of training growth, 84% employment.

Share of new capacity to regions in needEveryone at 40 GJ byEnergy closed, year 15Health closed, year 15Housing closed, year 30
50%not within 30 years14%−4%10%
65%not within 30 years28%1%34%
80%not within 30 years41%5%50%
90%year 2951%8%50%
100%year 2760%11%50%
C: UNIRAyear 2760%38%50%

A careful reader may spot something. The 50% row shows −4% for health, yet B2 showed +4%. The reason is the health-worker share. B2 itself sends 75% of new health workers to regions in need. In this test, the 50% row sends only 50%.

Three things stand out.

First, a market world catches up on housing at about 80%. Second, it nearly matches UNIRA on energy only at about 90%. That is more than four times today's 20%.

Third, at that point the distinction starts to disappear. A world that directs 90% of new capacity by need, by agreement, is no longer allocating by purchasing power. In practice, it is needs-first allocation. The real question is then how to build institutions that can direct capacity that way, and keep doing it.

Health still lags

Even at 100%, the market world closes only 11% of the health gap by year 15. At 90%, it closes 8%. UNIRA closes 38%.

Why such a gap, when sharing is the same? Because health workers are not shipped like panels. They must be trained, and training takes years and schools.

In France, training a general practitioner takes 10 years: six years of medical school and four of specialist training. Nursing takes 3 years, with 4,620 hours of training from September 2026. Even a care assistant needs 1,540 hours, about 11 months.

The world needs about 107 million health workers to give everyone EU-level care. It has 47.4 million. The research tested how long it takes to close that gap, depending on training:

Training planYears until everyone has EU-level care (central case)
Training as todaynever
Training grows 7% a year for 15 years37
Plus 25% of the world's 27.4 million soldiers volunteer36
Plus healthier lives (3% less need)34
Ambitious: 10% a year, half of soldiers, strong prevention23

The market worlds in this chapter train at the pace of the second row. UNIRA follows the ambitious plan. These years come from a separate health model. The full 30-year world transition gives year 30 for UNIRA.

UNIRA's health advantage comes from three things the market world in this test does not have:

  • a much larger training programme, built in the regions that need it;
  • former soldiers volunteering to train in health;
  • prevention, from healthier and less stressful lives.

Disarmament helps most in the early years. By year 10, it lifts the world to 71 health workers per 10,000, against 64 without it. Chapter 8 tells this story in full.

Allocation alone cannot create nurses. A world must decide to train them, at scale, for decades.

What if UNIRA's institutions work badly?

These results assume UNIRA's allocator works as designed. Chapter 14 looks at failure in detail. Here is the short version.

The research tested real-world errors: wrong census data, late data, regions that exaggerate their needs, and resources that go missing.

ScenarioEnergy closed, year 15Health, year 15Housing, year 30
UNIRA, perfect allocation60%38%50%
UNIRA, strong safeguards52%34%40%
UNIRA, no safeguards36%24%19%
UNIRA, worst governance observed15%12%−9%
Cooperative market (B2)14%4%10%

With strong safeguards, UNIRA keeps about 85% of its ideal performance. Even with typical failures and no safeguards, it still beats the cooperative market. Only under the worst governance ever observed does the advantage vanish.

One fairness point matters here. In this table, the market world keeps its ideal results. It was not stress-tested for leakage or bad data. Yet aid leaks in real life too: in aid-dependent countries, about 7.5% was moved offshore by elites. So on this point, the comparison does not favour UNIRA.

What the critics say, and what the research answers

A good test invites hard questions. Here are the strongest ones, with the most honest answers the research allows.

"You gave the market no advantage. Markets are more efficient."

That is a fair point, and the research does not settle it. All three worlds have the same technology and the same response of output to shorter hours. The model gives UNIRA no efficiency bonus. It gives markets none either.

What the model does not count matters too. UNIRA's own institutions need people. A digital currency, allocation bodies and a world democracy all take hours of work. The research says these hours are "not yet counted". A critic is right to ask for them.

"Your reformed market is a straw man."

World B uses verified numbers for the part that matters most: today's 20% share of clean-energy investment. Its employment rate, 84%, is close to the best any country reached in 2025. World B2 is more generous than anything that exists today. Aid is falling, not rising.

There is one real weakness. The 30% share of new homes in B is an assumption. No source splits new building by income group. The global building report used by the research says such estimates "are lacking".

"Needs-first allocation is central planning. We know how that ended."

Chapter 4 looked at the shortage economies honestly. Fixed prices without enough goods led to queues, rationing and black markets. UNIRA's answer is to plan in physical terms: production matched to needs, buffer stocks, and incomes that cannot exceed the goods available.

But be precise about what this chapter tested. It tested the allocation of three kinds of new capacity: energy, health workers and homes. It did not test planning millions of products for billions of people. The research names that as an open problem. It calls it the "knowledge problem", and lists it among its own tests.

"A market that sent 90% by need would do just as well."

On energy and housing, nearly. On health, no. And a market that sends 90% of its new capacity by need, by collective decision, has adopted UNIRA's core rule. The disagreement then becomes about the rest of the package: universal work, no army, the money cap. Those are fair things to debate. The comparison does not decide them.

"The military energy you free up is a guess."

Partly, yes. Military activity causes about 5.5% of global emissions, within a range of 3.3–7.0%. That figure includes supply chains. But no global statistic for military energy use exists. The research uses the emissions share as a stand-in, and says so. Fortunately, this lever matters mostly for the climate, not for how fast poorer regions catch up.

The honest caveat

It would be easy to end this chapter with a cheer. It would also be dishonest.

UNIRA's advantage is entirely institutional. The research gave it no hidden efficiency bonus. Technology, capacities and productivity are identical in all three worlds. UNIRA does better for three reasons only:

  • it directs new capacity by need rather than by ability to pay;
  • everyone able to work does work;
  • it stops work that only serves selling.

That is the strength of the result. It is also its weak point. The advantage holds only if the institutions work.

The model shows what needs-first allocation and universal work would achieve. It cannot show that a world institution can direct new capacity by need without error, delay or capture. It cannot show that 88% employment is reachable.

No country has reached it. Here are the highest employment rates for ages 20–64 in 2025:

CountryEmployment rate, ages 20–64, 2025
Iceland85.8%
Malta83.6%
Netherlands83.4%
Czechia82.9%
Switzerland82.4%
European Union76.1%
France75.4%

A fair critic can also say this. A cooperative market sending 80% of new capacity to regions in need would close much of the gap, without the rest of UNIRA. That is true for energy and housing, though not for health. The research names testing this boundary as its next step.

So the research sets its own trap. If a reformed market economy, with the same resources, reached the same outcomes, UNIRA would add nothing beyond its assumptions. That is one of the tests that could prove this book wrong. Chapter 16 lists the others.

Back to the dock

Return, for a moment, to the imagined container of solar panels.

The research cannot say who should receive it in every single case. Real decisions involve roads, ports, skills and weather. What it can say is what happens when the same choice is made millions of times, for thirty years.

If the container usually goes where the money is, the world's gaps grow. If it usually goes where the need is, and rich places use a little less each year, the gaps close within a generation.

That is the whole argument of this chapter, in one picture. The next chapter asks the obvious follow-up question. Who decides where the container goes, and what stops them from sending it to their friends?

What the evidence says

  • With matched resources, a reformed market world lets the gaps grow: energy −14%, health −4%, housing −21%.
  • A cooperative market closes 14%, 4% and 10%. UNIRA closes 60%, 38% and 50%.
  • All three worlds peak at the same world energy use, about 409 EJ. UNIRA wins by where it sends energy, not by using more.
  • Today only about 20% of clean-energy investment reaches developing countries outside China, home to about two-thirds of humanity.
  • Needs-first allocation of energy is the most decisive single institution, but it must come with restraint in rich regions, or world energy peaks at 453 EJ.
  • A market world must direct about 90% of new capacity by need to nearly match UNIRA on energy. By then it is practising needs-first allocation.
  • Even then it lags on health: 8% against 38% by year 15. Nurses must be trained, not shipped.
  • With strong safeguards, UNIRA keeps about 85% of its ideal advantage.

What we still don't know

  • The B and B2 lever values are policy scenarios, not observations. The 30% share of new homes in B is an assumption, because no source splits construction by income group.
  • One-lever-at-a-time attribution ignores interactions between institutions.
  • The cost of building UNIRA's institutions, the hours they need, and how people would behave in them are not modelled.
  • Whether a world institution can direct new capacity by need without capture is untested.
  • Whether 88% employment is reachable is unknown. No country has reached it.
  • The comparison covers three kinds of new capacity. Planning the whole economy without prices is a much larger problem, and it remains open.
  • No global figure exists for military energy use, or for the number of new doctors trained each year.

---

Chapter 14 — Guarding against power

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.9, V7.0, V7.2 and V7.5) and the draft Constitution of One Earth, and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Imagine a nurse in a small river town. On a quiet afternoon they open the Public Allocation Register on their phone. It is free, and anyone can read it.

The register says the town's clinic received two vaccine fridges this year. It says when, from where, and on which needs data. The nurse walks into the storeroom. There is one fridge.

So they write a complaint. The law says they must receive a reasoned answer within 90 days. The law also says nobody may punish them for asking. A few weeks later, an auditor arrives without warning.

This scene is imagined, a composite of many places. But the rules in it are real articles of the draft constitution. This chapter explains why they are there. It starts from an uncomfortable question: what if the people who share the world's resources get it wrong, or steal?

A good rule is not enough

Chapter 13 showed that directing new resources by need closes the world's gaps much faster than a market economy. But that test assumed a perfect allocator. It knew every region's needs exactly, on time, and every solar panel reached its roof.

No real institution works like that. The research looked at how real allocators fail today and found four main problems:

  • measurement error: needs are counted wrongly;
  • data lag: needs are counted late;
  • misreporting: some regions exaggerate their needs to receive more;
  • leakage: some resources never reach the people they were meant for.

Each one has been measured somewhere in the world. The research collected 44 sourced observations of them.

A closer look: how the evidence was checked

Numbers about corruption travel fast and get rounder as they go. So the research used a strict rule.

A number counted as verified only if it appeared in text actually fetched on 3 November 2026. That could be a study's abstract, an official page or a report. Some widely quoted figures could not be seen in such text. They were marked as unverified and were not used to set the model.

This cost the research some striking numbers. A famous estimate of missing spending in Indonesia was left out. So was a well-known effect of biometric smartcards in India, and a popular "10–25%" figure for corruption losses. They may be right. But they could not be checked, so they do not appear in this chapter.

Counting wrongly, counting late

Take counting first. South Africa's 2011 census missed 14.6% of people. By province, the share missed ranged from 10.0% to 18.6%. The national total can be corrected afterwards. The gap between provinces is harder to fix, and that is what would steer an allocator wrongly.

World poverty counts move too. A change in price data in 2005 added about 400 million people to the count of the poor. A later revision removed 46 million. The poorest places are often the least measured. Between 2002 and 2011, 57 of 155 countries had at most one poverty estimate.

Then there is delay. Poverty data usually reach users about 2 years after they are collected. Building takes time as well. New renewable plants take 1 to 5 years to build, and new power lines 5 to 15 years. So an allocator is always steering by a picture of the past.

One finding here is quietly important. Errors in judging single households are far larger than errors in judging whole regions. In nine African countries, a common household test classed 80% of poor households as not poor. It classed 40% of non-poor households as poor. Brazil's Bolsa Família in 2004 missed 59% of those it meant to reach, and included 49% it did not mean to. Yet it ranks among the most progressive programmes there are.

UNIRA's allocator does not judge households. It directs new capacity to regions, by measured need and population. People's core guarantees, from health care to a home, are written as rights in Article 6. So the allocator works with the smaller kind of error.

Regions that exaggerate

When resources follow reported needs, a region has a reason to inflate its figures. This is not a fear invented for this book. It has happened.

In China, local statistics inflated growth by about 1.7 percentage points a year between 2008 and 2016. National corrections averaged 5% of output. One province, Liaoning, admitted that its fiscal revenue had been exaggerated by about 20% between 2011 and 2014. Its output was later revised down by more than 20%.

In Brazil, towns receive federal grants according to population brackets. In 2010, 192 of 3,565 towns were in the wrong bracket. That misdirected over US$100 million a year. Near the first threshold, 17–42% of towns sat just above it, by only 62 to 241 people. The pattern grew worse from one census to the next.

The Brazilian case shows how small the cheating can be. A town needs only a few hundred extra residents on paper. That is about 0.5–2.4% of its population. Nobody would notice by walking down the street.

Resources that disappear

Leakage is the oldest problem of all. The research collected the best measured cases.

WhereWhat leakedHow much
Uganda, 1991–95School grants that never reached schools87%
India, 2004–05Subsidised grain that never reached families54%
India, 2011–12The same programme41.7%
India, 2022–24The same programme, after reforms8.8–28%
BrazilFunds diverted in audited towns30%
Aid-dependent countriesAid moved offshore by elites7.5%
European Union, 2023Cohesion spending found irregular (not fraud)9.3%
European Union, 2023The whole EU budget, irregular (not fraud)5.6%

Two lessons sit in this table. Leakage can be enormous where nobody checks. And it can fall far: India cut it by 30 to 45 points in about a decade. Yet even the European Union, with strong institutions, stays above 5%.

The stress test

The research then put all four failures into the UNIRA model of the 30-year world transition. The allocator no longer saw true needs. It saw noisy, late figures, some of them inflated. Part of what it sent simply vanished. Each scenario ran 30 times with random errors.

A closer look: how the stress test worked

The model makes one simple change to UNIRA's allocator. It decides on perceived needs, not true ones.

Measurement error. Each year, each region's need is seen with a random error. The error is as likely to be too high as too low, so it does not bias the total. In the central case it is about 15%.

Data lag. The allocator sees needs as they were some years ago. In the central case, 2 years.

Exaggeration. A share of regions inflate their reported need. Each year, an audit catches each cheating region with a 50% chance. That rate is an assumption.

Leakage. A share of all new capacity reaches no one.

One more rule makes the test harsh. Anything sent to a region beyond its true need is counted as wasted. It is not passed on.

The values came from the evidence above, in three settings:

FailureLowCentralHigh
Measurement error5%15%30%
Data lag1 year2 years5 years
Leakage, no safeguards10%25%45%
Leakage, strong safeguards3%8%15%
Share of regions exaggerating10%25%45%
How much they exaggerate2%7%20%

The research is candid about its confidence. It is medium for leakage and lag. It is low to medium for measurement error and exaggeration. Those two are judgement calls, anchored on the best clues available.

The results

How the One Earth allocator worksEnergy gap closed, year 15Health, year 15Homes, year 30Everyone at 40 GJ
Perfectly60%38%50%year 27
Like the best governance observed57%36%46%year 29
With strong safeguards52%34%40%year 29
With typical failures, no safeguards36%24%19%not in 30 years
Like the worst governance observed15%12%−9%not in 30 years
Very cooperative market world14%4%10%not in 30 years
Reformed market world−14%−4%−21%not in 30 years

The 30 random runs barely differed, by about 1 point, because errors average out across 179 regions.

Even with typical failures and no safeguards, needs-first allocation beats the most cooperative market world on all three needs. With strong safeguards it keeps most of the ideal result. Everyone reaches a comfortable 40 GJ of energy by year 29, instead of never.

The research puts it simply: safeguards are worth about as much as the allocation rule itself.

Under the worst governance ever observed, UNIRA loses its lead on energy and homes. It still leads on health, thanks to the training programme and the soldiers who volunteer for care work. That bottom row matters. It shows the idea can fail, and roughly where.

An illustration: errors cancel, theft does not

Here is an imagined way to see why the four failures are so different.

Picture a teacher marking a hundred exams in a hurry. Some marks come out a little high, some a little low. Over the whole class, the average is about right. That is measurement error. Across 179 regions, it behaves the same way.

Now picture a pupil who copies answers. The teacher may not notice this year. But spot checks happen every year, and copying is eventually caught. That is exaggeration, with audits.

Finally, picture someone stealing a third of the exam papers before they are marked. No averaging brings them back. Those pupils simply get no mark. That is leakage.

Why leakage decides everything

Of the four failures, one does most of the damage. Counting errors are random. Some regions are overcounted, some undercounted, and across many regions the mistakes partly cancel out. Exaggeration is caught by audits.

Leakage is different. A stolen panel is simply gone. It does not average out, and no later audit brings back the years it was missing.

From this the research draws a clear line. If One Earth cannot keep leakage well below about 45%, and data lags under about 5 years, needs-first allocation is no better than a cooperative market. The constitution writes that line into its own text.

Eight safeguards, each with a reason

The draft constitution answers each failure with a rule. Most of them come straight from the evidence.

SafeguardArticleWhat the evidence shows
Public Allocation Register, within 30 days33Uganda: capture fell from about 80% to under 20% after newspapers published school grants
Direct digital delivery, verified on site34India (Bihar): spending losses fell 24%, then 19% at national scale
Community monitoring and protected whistleblowers35Uganda health: under-five deaths fell 33%
Random audits of every region, every year36Indonesia: missing funds down 8 points; Brazil: corruption down 7.9%, 12.7% with repeat audits
Needs data less than 1 year old37Typical lag today is about 2 years
Rules against capture26, 30Built from the design, not from a single study
Correction without punishing people38Built from the design
A built-in test of the whole system39Thresholds from the stress test

Some of these need a few more words.

The register. Every allocation is published, saying what, where, how much, why and on which data. Personal data are never published. Sunlight works because it turns millions of people into checkers. The honest caveat: part of Uganda's fall came at the same time as wider reforms.

Digital delivery. Payments go straight, in U, to whoever delivers the work. Delivery is checked on site and by remote sensing before it counts as done. In India this cut losses with no loss of jobs. But it also slowed payments. A safeguard can add delay, about half a year to a year in that case.

Community monitoring. Every community may watch what it was promised. Anyone may complain and get an answer within 90 days. Here too the evidence is mixed. In Indonesia, grassroots monitoring alone did little, because everyone waited for someone else to act. That is why UNIRA pairs it with professional audits, not instead of them.

Audits. The Audit Authority checks every region, every year, at random and without notice. In Indonesia, raising the chance of an audit from 4% to 100% cut missing spending by 8 points. In Brazil, audits also had effects beyond the towns audited, through the media: 7.5–10.4%. UNIRA's audit budget is fixed by the constitution as a share of what is allocated. So no government can starve it of money.

Data. The Statistical Office measures needs at least once a year, with the uncertainty stated. Data are checked against independent sources, such as censuses, satellite data and sample surveys. No region may change its methods alone.

Rules against capture. The Allocation Authority applies a public formula and has no power to favour any region. Changing the formula takes a full law. And no one may decide an allocation to a region where they or their family hold an interest.

Correction without punishing people. Misallocated resources are recovered and sent where they belong. A region caught misreporting loses priority for extra, discretionary allocations. It never loses its people's rights. A child should not lose a clinic because an official lied.

The built-in test. World leakage and data age are published every year. Then two alarms apply:

  • if leakage passes 25%, or data are more than 2 years old, the Assembly must review the allocation system within a year;
  • if leakage passes 45%, or data are older than 5 years, for 3 years running, the system must be reformed by law.

This constitution says, in advance, how its own central idea could be shown wrong. It also says what must happen then.

A closer look: the finer triggers

An earlier, longer draft of these safeguards proposed sharper triggers for each article. They are not yet in the constitution's short text. They show how each rule could be made to bite:

  • an allocation still missing from the register after 60 days would be void until published;
  • leakage above 15% in any region for 2 years running would start an independent review there;
  • a project whose delivery cannot be verified within 12 months would be listed as undelivered;
  • needs data more than 2 years old would flag that region's allocations for checking;
  • officials found diverting resources would lose office and face independent courts;
  • the allocation rule itself would be reviewed in public every 5 years.

These are proposals. The research is explicit that every threshold "may be set differently by democratic decision".

An illustration: a region that cheats

Imagine a regional statistics office under pressure. A few officials decide to round up the number of people living in crowded homes. More homes will come their way.

For a year or two, it works. Then a random audit compares their figures with a satellite survey and a sample count. The gap is too large to be an honest error.

What happens next is set out in the constitution. The extra homes still under construction are reassigned to where they are truly needed. The region loses priority for extra, discretionary allocations until audits confirm honest reporting. The officials face the courts.

What does not happen matters just as much. No family in that region loses its home, its health care, its children's school or its income. Those are rights under Article 6, and Article 9 says no sanction may reduce them. The officials lied. The people did not.

What safeguards cost

Safeguards are not free, and the research says so.

Checking takes time. In Bihar, direct digital payment cut losses but slowed payments. The research suggests modelling about half a year to a year of extra delay as a cost of safeguards.

Checking takes people. Auditors, statisticians and inspectors must be trained and paid. Their hours are part of the work of running UNIRA's own institutions, which the research admits it has not yet counted.

And checking has limits. Even the European Union stays above 5% irregular spending. The aim is not zero leakage. The aim, on the evidence, is to stay well below the levels where needs-first allocation stops paying off.

Power divided among many hands

Leakage is one danger. Another is older: people who gain power and keep it.

The constitution divides public power into six parts: making law, carrying it out, allocating resources, measuring needs, auditing and judging. No person or body may hold two of them. So the people who count needs are not the people who share resources. The people who share are not the people who check.

Time is limited too:

  • the executive's seven to fifteen coordinators serve a single 5-year term;
  • the chair rotates every year, and no region may hold it twice in a row;
  • judges of the Court serve one 12-year term, drawn from all continents;
  • no one may hold One Earth office for more than 10 years in total.

Money is limited as well. Every office-holder declares their assets publicly each year. And they are under the same money cap as everyone else. A world official cannot grow rich in office, because nobody can grow rich above the cap.

Regions are protected from the centre. Each region has its own elected government and parliament. One Earth cannot dissolve them, except by a ruling of the Court for a grave breach of rights. There are no permanent seats, no vetoes, and no privileges of size, wealth or history.

Science is given a voice of its own. An independent Scientific Council, drawn from all regions, reviews the methods behind decisions. Its opinions are public. A decision that departs from its advice must say why.

Voting that no giant and no clique can control

Chapter 5 described the two chambers. Here is the reasoning in brief.

The Assembly of the Peoples has 1,000 members, elected every 5 years. If seats followed raw population, India and China would hold about 16–17% each. If every region had one equal vote, the people of a tiny island would weigh as much as the people of India. Both are unfair.

The square-root rule, known as the Penrose rule, sits between them. It aims to give every citizen equal voting power in a union of regions. The research tested it on 2024 populations, for 217 regions:

RegionSeats if proportional to populationSeats under the square-root rule
India16.5%4.4%
China16.0%4.4%
United States3.9%2.1%
Indonesia3.2%2.0%
Pakistan2.9%1.8%
The two largest together32.5%8.8%

Every region has at least one seat. No region may hold more than 5%.

The same idea guards the double majority. A law needs at least 55% of regions, representing at least 65% of the population, counted by square root. These thresholds follow the Council of the European Union. Counted by raw population, India and China together, 35.2% of humanity, could block any world law alone. Counted by square root, blocking takes at least 21 regions. Passing a law takes at least 120 of 217.

Changing the constitution is harder still. It needs two-thirds of the Assembly, 65% of regions representing 65% of the population, and a world referendum. And some things can never be changed. The guaranteed rights of Article 6, and the rule that no law, sanction or emergency may reduce them, are out of reach of any majority.

Who guards the only army?

UNIRA abolishes war, so only One Earth may hold arms. That creates the most dangerous power of all. Whoever controls the only armed force could rule everyone. Chapter 12 described the Peace Force in detail. Here are its locks.

ControlRule
Who may send itOnly with both a Court ruling and the double majority of regions
EmergenciesIt may act alone for at most 72 hours to save lives, then needs both
No national army inside itNo region provides more than 5% of members or commanders
No local loyaltyCommanders rotate every 3 years; members never serve in their own region
SizeNever more than 1,000,000 members, about 12 per 100,000 people; planned about 500,000
MoneyNever more than 0.15% of world output; planned about 0.05%
OversightAn Inspector-General appointed by the Court reports publicly; anyone harmed may go to the Court
Forbidden usesNever against peaceful protest, a lawful election or a region's democratic institutions

One detail shows how carefully this was thought through. Research on new armies finds they are most at risk of coups while they are being built. So the Force grows step by step, with members from all regions from the first day. And the Inspector-General is in place before the first unit forms.

Regions keep civilian police for everyday crime, trained to One Earth's human-rights standards and equipped only for policing.

What the critics say, and what the research answers

"Who guards the guardians?"

Nobody, in the end, except other people. That is why the design spreads checking as widely as it can. Auditors check allocators. The Court checks laws and allocations. Communities check deliveries. Anyone can read the register and complain. No single body holds the last word. Whether that holds under pressure, the model cannot say.

"A digital currency where every sale has an invoice is a surveillance machine."

This is a serious worry, and the constitution meets it only in part. Article 12 guarantees privacy, access without a smartphone, offline use, independent oversight and a right to appeal. Personal gifts need no invoice. The register publishes amounts and places, never personal data. But guarantees on paper are not the same as guarantees in practice. How privacy would be protected in the technology itself is not yet worked out.

"A constitution is only paper."

True. Many countries have fine constitutions and poor governance. The stress test is useful here because it does not assume the rules are obeyed. It asks what happens when they are not. Its answer: with typical failures, UNIRA still beats the best market world. Under the worst governance ever seen, it does not.

"Your audit catch rate is invented."

It is an assumption, and the research labels it so. A 50% chance a year of catching a cheating region has no direct measurement behind it. The good news is that exaggeration mattered much less than leakage in every run. The bad news is that nobody knows how a world audit body would really perform.

"Building homes and power plants is far more corrupt than handing out grain."

This may be right. Most leakage evidence comes from cash and food programmes. Construction is often more corrupt. The research names this among its next steps: testing leakage in infrastructure, not only in transfers.

"Corrupt officials do not lose resources. They steer them to friends."

Also fair. The model counts stolen resources simply as lost. In reality, favoured regions might receive extra, which could do more harm. This is not yet modelled.

The limit no safeguard can fix

One case in the evidence is different from all the others.

In 2021 the global vaccine-sharing scheme, COVAX, promised 2 billion doses. By the end of November it had delivered about 596 million, about 30%. The failure was not in deciding who should get doses. It was in getting the doses at all.

An allocator is only as good as what it has to give. That is why this book spends so many chapters on production: energy built, nurses trained, homes raised. Fair sharing of too little is still too little.

What the evidence says

  • Real allocators count needs wrongly, count them late, are misled by exaggeration and lose resources to leakage. All four have been measured.
  • Errors in judging regions are much smaller than errors in judging households. UNIRA allocates to regions and gives people rights.
  • With typical failures and no safeguards, needs-first allocation still beats the most cooperative market: 36% of the energy gap closed by year 15, against 14%.
  • With strong safeguards it keeps most of its ideal performance: 52% against 60%, and everyone reaches 40 GJ by year 29.
  • Leakage is the decisive danger. Above about 45%, with data lags near 5 years, the advantage disappears. The constitution's built-in test sets its alarms at 25% and 45%.
  • Power is split six ways, terms are single and short, and officials are under the money cap. The only armed force needs both a court and the regions to move.

What we still don't know

  • Most leakage evidence comes from cash and food programmes in low- and middle-income countries. Building homes and power plants may leak more, since construction is often more corrupt.
  • The audit catch rate of 50% a year is an assumption, not a measurement.
  • The model counts capture only as loss. In reality, stolen resources may be steered to favoured regions, which could hurt more.
  • Errors were treated as independent between regions. If a whole continent were undercounted at once, the damage would be larger.
  • The model does not include politics: legitimacy, anger in regions that receive less, or institutions slowly decaying over decades.
  • The costs of safeguards, in delay and in staff, are noted but not fully modelled.
  • How privacy would be protected inside a world digital currency is not yet designed in detail.
  • An allocator is only as good as what it has to give. COVAX promised 2 billion vaccine doses in 2021 and delivered about 596 million by the end of November. The failure was supply, not targeting.

---

Chapter 15 — The transition

Second draft, 3 November 2026. Every figure comes from the UNIRA research (V6.4, V7.2, V7.4 and V7.5) and the draft Constitution of One Earth, and is listed in the notes at the end of the chapter.

---

Imagine the evening before a referendum, in a kitchen anywhere in a rich region.

The grandmother worries about her savings. She worked all her life for them, and she wants to know what happens to them. Her son worries about the mortgage. Her granddaughter, who works nights in a warehouse, asks a different question. Would she finally have a home of her own, and evenings free?

The family is imagined, a composite of many households. Their questions are real, and every region would ask them. Who gains? What happens to my money, my house, my debts? And what if it goes wrong, as other great changes have?

This chapter answers with the research. It starts with who gains, then looks at what history teaches, then sets out the eight rules for getting there. It ends where it began, in the kitchen.

Who gains by joining

The research compared each region's situation after 30 years, inside UNIRA or in a reformed market world. It looked at three things people need: energy, health workers and floor space at home. It combined them into one measure of material gain or loss.

The result is clear. 146 regions, home to 86% of humanity, gain materially by joining, compared with a reformed market world after 30 years. In 33 regions, home to 14%, the average falls a little on the same comparison. Neither number compares with today.

But averages hide people. Inside those 33 regions, about 495 million people, mostly the poor and the lower middle, are better off even at the lowest UNIRA income (see below). Counted person by person, about 92% of humanity is better off.

RegionEnergyHealth workersFloor spaceOverall
Ethiopia4.2×2.5×1.9×+170%
Nigeria3.4×2.3×1.6×+136%
Pakistan3.0×2.3×1.5×+116%
India1.8×1.8×1.5×+72%
Indonesia1.5×1.8×1.3×+54%
Brazilabout the same1.3×about the same+11%
China0.82×1.3×the same+2%
France0.83×the samethe same−6%
Germany0.78×the samethe same−8%
Russia0.68×about the samethe same−12%
Japan0.69×the samethe same−12%
United States0.68×the samethe same−12%

Look at the columns, not just the last one. In the poorest regions, everything grows. An Ethiopian family would have four times the energy, two and a half times the health workers, and nearly twice the space.

China is the interesting middle case. It would use less energy than in a market world, but have about a third more health workers. The two roughly cancel out.

A closer look: how gain and loss were measured

Two choices shape this table, and both are worth knowing.

The comparison is with a market future, not with today. Each column compares a region in year 30 under UNIRA with the same region in year 30 in a reformed market world. That world is World B from Chapter 13. So "France −6%" does not mean French people would have 6% less than today. It means 6% less than France would have in 30 years under a good market.

The three needs are combined as ratios. For each need, the research divides the UNIRA amount by the market amount. Then it averages the three, on a scale where doubling and halving count equally. That is what "mean log ratio" means in the notes.

Here is the French case, worked through. Energy is 0.83 of the market amount. Health workers and floor space are unchanged, so their ratios are 1. Averaging the three on that scale gives a result of about −6%. In plain words, a cut of about a sixth in one need out of three counts as a loss of about a sixteenth overall.

This measure has limits. It counts only energy, health workers and floor space. It leaves out time, peace, security, food, clean air and much else. Those things matter to voters. They are not in the number.

What rich regions give up

Now look at the rich regions. Only one column moves: energy. Their health care is unchanged. Their homes are unchanged.

So the loss of 6 to 12% is a loss in energy use only. A French household would use about a sixth less energy than in a market world. An American one would use about a third less. Both stay at or above the comfortable level of Chapter 10.

The largest loss in the model is about 12%. A group of the richest regions all sit near it:

RegionsLoss on the material measure
Luxembourg, Iceland, Belgium, Australia, Finland, Sweden, Netherlands, Norway, Austria, United Statesabout 12.2%
Malta, Canadaabout 12.1–12.2%
South Koreaabout 12.0%
Japan, Russiaabout 11.6–11.7%

Why do so many sit at almost the same number? The model gives them nearly identical results. Each keeps its health care and homes, so only energy changes. And each keeps a comfortable level of energy.

For these regions, joining is a choice, not a bargain. It is worth it if peace, security, a 30-hour week and a stable climate count for more than some energy. No model can decide that. Only their people can, by vote.

Inside rich regions, many are better off

National averages hide who wins and who loses. So the research looked inside rich regions, at each tenth of the population, from poorest to richest.

It started from the lowest possible UNIRA income: a worker on the base salary of 5,000 U a month who saves the maximum, half of it. That leaves 2,500 U a month to spend. At UNIRA's fixed prices, that buys about 86% of what an average French person consumes today.

Here is France, tenth by tenth, measured against today's average:

Tenth of the populationToday, against the averageBetter off at the lowest UNIRA income?
Poorest31%yes
Second51%yes
Third63%yes
Fourth72%yes
Fifth82%yes
Sixth93%no
Seventh103%no
Eighth119%no
Ninth143%no
Richest243%no

The line falls right in the middle. The poorest half of France lives today below the level of the lowest UNIRA income.

Is that true elsewhere? UNIRA's salaries and prices are the same everywhere, so the lowest income buys the same basket in every region. That basket is worth about $21,900 of household spending per person a year, at 2021 prices adjusted for purchasing power. In a region where people consume more on average, such as the United States, a smaller share of people lives below it today. The research checked all 33 regions whose average falls, using World Bank income shares (2019–2024) for the 31 that have them:

RegionAverage consumption today, against FranceBetter off even at the lowest UNIRA incomeGain for the poorest tenthChange in the region's average energy use
United States199%24%+154%-12%
Norway137%22%+74%-12%
Switzerland139%30%+106%-5%
Germany124%35%+202%-8%
Canada118%37%+151%-12%
Australia116%42%+185%-12%
France100%50%+186%-6%
Italy104%48%+258%-1%
Spain92%55%+325%-3%
Japan89%56%+319%-12%
South Korea88%59%+237%-12%
Czechia71%75%+217%-8%

Across these 31 regions, 44% of the people, about 495 million, are better off even at the lowest UNIRA income. The poorest tenth gains between +74% and +446% everywhere. In the United States, about a quarter of the people are better off at the very lowest income, and the poorest tenth gains about +154%. In Japan more than half are better off, and the poorest tenth gains about +319%. Nobody anywhere falls below this basket.

But the lowest salary is only the floor. Most people will earn more: under UNIRA, pay rises with experience, knowledge and the complexity of the work, from 1 to 5 times the base salary. In the research's money model the average is about 1.9 times. Here is the share of people who consume less today than each UNIRA salary would buy, even while saving half of it:

UNIRA salaryUSAJapanFranceGermanyItalyCanadaNorwayAll 31 rich regions
Lowest (1×)24%56%50%35%48%37%22%44%
1.5×42%82%77%64%73%67%54%67%
Average (1.9×)54%88%87%79%85%80%73%77%
Highest (5×)89%100%100%100%100%100%100%97%

At the average UNIRA salary, about three in four people in the rich regions would consume more than they do today. Free health care, education and a guaranteed home come on top.

Why not everyone? Because in some regions a small group consumes far more than even the highest UNIRA salary allows. In the United States, about the richest tenth consumes more than twice the national average. The data come in tenths, so the very richest, the top 1%, cannot be separated, and they consume more still. These are the people with several homes, large fortunes and private jets. UNIRA limits them to two homes, a money cap and energy and travel paid per unit. They keep a comfortable life, but with less than today. Saying "everyone gains" would be untrue, and this book does not say it.

And this is the floor, not the average. Many people will earn above the base salary. Free health care, free education and guaranteed housing come on top, and they are not counted here. Fixed prices also end household price shocks like the energy crisis of 2022.

So even in the regions whose average falls, a large share of the voters gains, and everyone keeps a comfortable life. The granddaughter in the warehouse is one of them.

One caution belongs here. These comparisons use income shares for each country (2019–2024) and apply them to household consumption. In the United States, households pay a large part of their health care out of that consumption, which UNIRA provides free, so the gains there are understated. They measure spending power, not wealth. Someone with low income but large savings would be counted among the poor. The savings rules later in this chapter matter for them.

A union of the willing still works

What if the rich regions say no? The research tested a union of only the 146 regions that gain.

That union holds 86% of the world's people. But it holds only 62% of the world's energy use and 65% of its health workers. It must do more with less.

Share of the gap closedWhole world joinsOnly the gainers joinVery cooperative market
Energy, by year 1552%20%14%
Health workers, by year 1534%15%4%
Homes, by year 3040%25%10%

The "whole world" column already includes strong safeguards against error and leakage, from Chapter 14. That is why it shows 52% and not the ideal 60%.

The union of gainers works at about half speed. Even so, it beats the most cooperative market world on all three needs.

This is perhaps the most important result for the transition. UNIRA does not need everyone on day one. It can start with the willing, show results, and grow.

What history teaches

Large transitions have been tried before. The research reviewed 61 findings on how they went. 51 of them were verified in fetched text. The other 10 are not used as facts. Chapter 4 told the stories of German reunification, the European Union and debt relief in detail. Here are the lessons that shape the transition rules, with a few new ones.

Converting everything at once can destroy an economy. On 1 July 1990, East Germany adopted the West German mark. Wages and prices were converted one to one, at a political rate. Within months, industrial output fell to about half its 1989 level. Only 8% of workers were in firms that could still cover their costs. More than 3 million jobs, about a third, disappeared.

Selling everything off fast can fail too. The Treuhand agency took over about 8,500 firms employing more than 4 million people. It laid off 2.5 million. It closed with a deficit of about DM 256 billion, against about DM 67 billion in revenue.

Giving old property back can freeze investment. Germany chose "restitution before compensation" for property seized in the past. Claims tied up titles and slowed rebuilding.

Compensation eaten by inflation is confiscation in disguise. Japan's land reform after 1947 cut tenant farmers from 43.5% to 11.8% of farmers. But landlords were paid under 1,000 yen per unit of land worth about 20,000 by 1950. Inflation did the rest. Taiwan did better. It paid landlords 70% in land bonds and 30% in shares of state firms, steering them into industry.

Land reform can raise output. South Korea's 1950 reform bought land above 3 hectares, and land of absentee owners, with bills paid over 5 years. Counties with 10 points more tenancy saw rice-yield growth 5 points higher afterwards. But Korean bills, too, were eroded by wartime inflation.

Wealth taxes leak when the wealthy can leave. France's wealth tax raised €4.42 billion in 2007, about 1.5% of tax receipts. In 2006 alone, 843 taxpayers left because of it, a net €2.8 billion. In Norway, at least 30 very rich people left in 2022, more than in the previous 13 years combined. Those who moved to Switzerland held NOK 29 billion. The research draws a careful conclusion. The losses came from a few hundred very wealthy households moving, not from mass flight.

Wealth is highly concentrated. Worldwide, the richest 10% hold 75% of wealth. The poorest half hold 2%. The richest 0.001% hold more than 6%. A money cap touches very few people deeply. But those few hold a great deal.

The encouraging lessons are just as clear.

Voluntary, staged joining works. The European Union grew from 6 members to 27 over decades, by choice and with clear entry rules. Norway voted no twice, and that was respected.

Modest transfers bring large catch-up. Donor countries paid only 0.3–0.4% of their output. Poorer members received about 2–4% of theirs. The 2004 entrants rose from 60% to 80% of the EU average. Poland went from 44% in 1995 to 81% in 2025.

Small savings can be protected. Germany converted savings one to one up to 4,000 marks for adults, 2,000 for children and 6,000 for people over 60. Larger sums converted at two to one.

Cancelling debt frees money for people. Debt relief for the poorest countries cost about USD 119.5 billion. Afterwards, they spent 7.3% of output on fighting poverty, against 1.9% on debt.

Mortgages can be converted without collapse. Iceland's 2014 plan was capped at ISK 4 million per household and cut the average mortgage by about 20%. It reached 56,000 households, and 2,500 of them moved back above water. Hungary converted the foreign-currency loans of more than 400,000 borrowers. It locked the rate just before the Swiss franc shock of January 2015.

People judge by the shop, not the statistics. The euro added only 0.12–0.29 points to measured inflation in 2002. But people felt prices rise, because cafés and groceries went up. The research draws three practical lessons: publish prices in both currencies, ban rounding up, and watch everyday items closely. Fixed prices for essentials remove much of this fear.

The eight transition rules

From these lessons the research drafted eight rules. The author has made the hardest decisions. The constitution turns them into its transitional provisions.

RuleIn one line
T1Join by referendum, in three stages
T2No single political exchange rate
T3Savings keep their buying power; the money cap phases in over 10 years
T4Homes are never seized; mortgages become zero-interest credit
T5Enterprises: compensation, never restitution
T6No public debts, with savers protected
T7A right to leave, for regions and for people
T8Protect production during the change

T1. Staged joining. A region votes to join. As an observer, it shares data and builds the safeguards of Chapter 14. As an associate, it receives needs-based transfers and adopts the 30-hour week. As a full member, it adopts the U and all the rules. Each stage has published entry criteria.

Why build the safeguards first? Because Chapter 14 showed they are worth about as much as the allocation rule itself. A region should not receive needs-based transfers before it can show where they go.

T2. No political exchange rate. This is the lesson of 1990. At full membership, wages move directly onto the UNIRA scale: 5,000 U a month, times a multiplier up to five. Prices of essentials are set from the work and resources they contain, the same everywhere. Living standards come from what is allocated by need, not from a rate someone invented.

T3. Savings and the money cap. Savings convert so that they buy the same basket of food, housing and energy as before. The research calls this "basket parity". Savings up to the cap stay with their owner in full. This answers the grandmother: an ordinary lifetime of saving is kept.

Savings above the cap go directly to One Earth, not into bonds. That was the author's decision. The money returns as homes, energy and care in the regions that need them.

The cap does not arrive overnight. It starts high and falls each year for 10 years, to its steady level of about 1.4 years of base salary. Chapter 6 explains why a cap is needed at all. Without it, a 60-year simulation showed unspent money piling up to 152 months of consumption, and still rising. With it, the pile stayed stable at about 12 months.

To stop wealth from fleeing before the vote, an anti-flight rule applies. The cap counts wealth held on the day the region votes to join. Money sent abroad after that day still counts as held.

T4. Homes. Homes are not money, and they are never taken. The two-home limit applies to new purchases. Extra homes may be sold to the regional pool at the fixed price within about 10 years, or passed on to heirs. Mortgages become zero-interest credit in U, repaid from salary. This answers the son.

T5. Enterprises. Firms become cooperative or public enterprises. Owners' pay becomes a salary, capped at five times the base. Surplus goes to the region. Owners receive their share in U under the same cap rules. Old property claims are settled by compensation, never by handing property back.

T6. No public debts. At full membership, all public debts of member regions are cancelled. Inside one world entity, a debt between regions would be One Earth owing money to itself.

Ordinary savers are protected. Government bonds held by households, pension funds and banks on savers' behalf count as those people's savings. They are kept up to the cap. Pension promises are replaced by UNIRA's needs-based retirement income.

Debts to creditors outside UNIRA are paid by One Earth on a negotiated schedule, until those regions join. Defaulting on them has, in the past, brought lawsuits and seized assets. Paying them avoids asset seizures and trade blockades against new members. When the whole world has joined, no public debt remains anywhere.

T7. The right to leave. A region may leave by democratic vote, with a negotiated settlement. Every person may emigrate and keep their money up to the cap. The author confirmed this right. A union people cannot leave would not be voluntary.

T8. Protect production. New members keep their farms, factories and services running with support from the union. Trade with non-members continues at negotiated prices. East Germany shows what happens when a currency shock is left to destroy production.

Two more provisions complete the picture. Armed forces are dissolved within 5 years of full membership, and every member is offered work, training and full income. And one rule runs through the whole transition, fixed by the author. Energy, travel and data are always metered and paid per unit, never at a flat rate. Free or flat-rate access raises the use of such goods by 14–70%, and the climate cannot absorb that.

A closer look: the money cap in numbers

The cap came out of the money model in Chapter 6. That model followed 6,000 simulated adults in one region for 60 years, under UNIRA's rules.

60-year simulationNo capCap of about 1.4 years of base salary, with zero-interest home credit
Unspent money, in months of consumption152, still rising12, stable
Flow to One Earth, share of regional consumptionabout 0%about 20–23% a year
Adults at the capnot applicable57%

Two things stand out. First, the cap is not a rule for a handful of billionaires. In the simulation, 57% of adults were at the cap. Second, the flow to One Earth is large. It is what pays for homes, energy and training in the regions that need them.

That is also why the cap must stay low. The research found that a cap set above the price of a home cannot keep money stable. Its proposal was a cap of about 1 to 1.5 years of base salary, recalculated each year, with homes bought on zero-interest credit.

These numbers come from a model of people, not from people. How real savers would respond to a cap has never been observed. The research lists this among the tests that could prove UNIRA wrong.

An illustration: one region's road

Here is an imagined region, to show how the stages fit together. The order comes from the rules. The details are invented.

The region holds a referendum, and the yes side wins. From that day, the anti-flight rule applies. Wealth sent abroad still counts.

As an observer, the region opens its books. It sets up an audit office and a statistics office that meet One Earth's standards. It starts publishing its allocations in the public register. Nothing changes yet in people's wages.

As an associate, the region begins to receive needs-based transfers. If it is poor, new solar farms, clinics and housing sites appear. Its working week moves to 30 hours. Employers must find more workers, so jobs open up for people who had none.

As a full member, the region adopts the U. Wages move onto the UNIRA scale. Mortgages become zero-interest credit. Savings convert at basket parity. Public debts are cancelled. The money cap starts high, then falls a little each year for ten years.

At no point does anyone lose the home they live in. At no point are they forbidden to leave.

Back to the kitchen

Now return to the imagined family on the night before the vote.

The grandmother's savings. They convert so that they buy the same food, housing and energy as before. Up to the cap, they stay hers in full. For an ordinary lifetime of saving, nothing is taken. Her pension promise is replaced by a needs-based income for life, covering food, daily life and one holiday a year. Her health care is free.

The son's mortgage. His home stays his. His mortgage becomes zero-interest credit in U, repaid from salary. If he owns a third home, he may sell it to the regional pool at the fixed price within about ten years.

The granddaughter's evenings. Her week becomes 30 hours. If she earns the base salary and saves half, she still consumes more than the poorest half of her country does today. She can buy a 100,000 U home in about 3.3 years of saving, or faster with zero-interest credit.

None of this settles how they will vote. The grandmother may still fear change. The son may still prefer the world he knows. That is their right, and the referendum is how they use it.

What the critics say, and what the research answers

"Taking savings above the cap is confiscation."

It is a transfer, and the research does not hide that. It openly says that taking savings above the cap directly is "legally and politically harder" than paying in bonds. The author chose it anyway. Two limits apply. The cap is phased in over ten years. And homes are never taken. Whether voters accept this is exactly what each referendum decides.

"The rich will leave before the vote."

Some will try. France and Norway show it. The anti-flight rule counts wealth held on the day of the vote, wherever it goes afterwards. The ten-year phase-in softens the blow. Neither removes the risk. No reliable measure exists of how many would try.

"This is East Germany all over again."

The research takes this seriously. UNIRA avoids the single political exchange rate, protects production and keeps trade open. Its enterprises do not compete on price, which removes the mechanism that sank East German firms. But one risk remains. If wages in U are high compared with a region's real productivity on day one, a similar shock could follow. The research says this must be modelled, not assumed away.

"Why would any rich region ever vote yes?"

On average, they lose some energy. But inside them, half the people are better off even at the lowest UNIRA income. Add peace, a shorter week, free care and a stable climate, and the balance may tip. Or it may not. The model cannot vote.

"Outsiders will strangle a small union."

The rules are written to avoid giving them a reason. Debts to outside creditors are honoured on a schedule. Trade continues at negotiated prices. And even a union of only the gaining regions closes the world's gaps faster than the best market world.

"Debt cancellation is a gift to irresponsible governments."

Inside UNIRA, no region carries public debt at all. The rule is not a one-off rescue. It is a permanent change. According to UNCTAD, about 3.3 billion people live in countries that spend more on interest than on health or education (A World of Debt, 2023).

What the evidence says

  • 86% of humanity, in 146 regions, gains materially by joining, compared with a reformed market world after 30 years. Ethiopia gains about 170%, India 72%.
  • In the 33 richest regions, average energy use per person is 6–12% lower than in a market future. Health care and homes are unchanged, nobody falls below the comfortable level, and 44% of their people are better off even at the lowest income.
  • Inside France, Germany, Italy and Spain, half the people are better off even at the lowest UNIRA income. The poorest tenth gains 146–258% across the regions studied.
  • A union of only the gaining regions works at about half speed and still beats the best market world.
  • History warns against one-shot conversion, fire sales, restitution and inflation-eaten compensation. It supports staged joining, protected small savings and capped debt relief.
  • The eight transition rules answer these lessons directly: staged joining, no political exchange rate, basket parity, homes never seized, compensation not restitution, no public debts, a right to leave, and protected production.

What we still don't know

  • Taking savings above the cap directly is legally and politically harder than paying in bonds. The research says so openly.
  • The anti-flight rule and slow phase-in reduce the risk of the very rich leaving before a vote. They do not remove it. No reliable measure exists of how many would try.
  • If wages in U are high compared with a region's real productivity on day one, a shock like East Germany's could follow. Planned production avoids price competition, but the productivity gap is real.
  • The material measure covers energy, health workers and floor space. It leaves out time, peace and security, which matter to voters too.
  • The comparison inside rich regions uses income, not wealth.
  • Full catch-up is a path of decades, not a first-year event. The EU's 2004 entrants took about 20 years to rise from 60% to 80%.
  • There is no verified measure of how strongly wealthy people respond to a wealth cap. Only counts of departures exist.

---

Notes on sources

  • Market comparators (very cooperative market: 14%, 4%, 10%) and whole-world results with strong safeguards: v72/TRANSITION_RULES.md, from V6.9 (v69/RESEARCH_NOTE.md).

Base worker spending 2,500 U a month, home purchase in 3.3 years, and the 60-year money simulation: v67/WORKING_PAPER_02.md, section 4.
